# The packages under `internal/`

**Every package states its own rationale in its package doc.** `go doc ./internal/coord` is the authority on coordination, not this file. What follows is a map, so you know which doc to read, and each row carries what its package decided that a reader would be tempted to "fix" back to the obvious shape, together with what the obvious alternative cost when it was tried. None of it binds: change the code, and rewrite the comment and the row, in the same commit. A new package gets a row.

## The spine — what everything else is built on

| Package | What it is for |
|---|---|
| `queue` | THE EventQueue contract. One interface, two backends (`memory`, `jetstream`), both certified by ONE suite in `queuetest`. Sixteen verbs, and FOUR of them are the opposite of every other: `Ask` (and `AskEach`, which hands each reply over as it arrives) and `Serve` are an EPHEMERAL scatter (core NATS request/reply on the real backend: no stream, no consumer, no ack, no retention, no publish listener) because a query is not an event, and a search fan-out on the durable verbs would write two records and an audit row per keystroke; `Broadcast` and `Listen` are the same shape as a fan-out (core publish and subscribe, at most once, every listener hears every message, one at a time per listener, refused with `ErrUnavailable` rather than buffered into a reconnect), which presence travels on because a heartbeat on a durable verb would be a replicated write per cursor move. An answerer answers CONCURRENTLY, up to `MaxConcurrentAnswers` per registration through ONE `AnswerGate` both backends share, and a withdrawal waits for what is in flight: run inside the subscription callback, every request to an answerer waited behind the one before it while the twin answered in parallel and certified it clean. Nothing above this package may branch on which backend is running. A durable subscription IS a seat's mailbox: it exists without a consumer and retains what is published while nothing is attached — and it outlives every record of its own name, a removed seat's handle included, so the BROKER has to be able to list them (`ListSubscriptions`) or a leaked mailbox can never be found again. Handlers have three outcomes — ack, nak, and *defer*. A failed verb is MARKED with one of three sentinels and nothing else may be branched on — `ErrTooLarge` (permanent), `ErrNotLive` (consumes nothing until started again; a connection NATS closed for good is one, `jetstream.ErrConnectionLost`) and `ErrUnavailable` (the broker did not answer — a CONDITION, never proof of teardown; `Ask` refuses rather than buffer its request into a down connection, and a `DeleteSubscription` the broker cannot hear has still detached); the suite holds both backends to it through the `BrokerDown` capability. Attachment has four verbs of differing destructiveness, because `unsubscribe` never said which one it was — and only `Stop`, never `Detach`, also ends an idle consume loop's outstanding fetch, since the connection closes next anyway: a loop parked in a fetch noticed its cancellation only when the fetch ran out, so every stop of a queue with an idle attachment waited out a poll it then abandoned. Its real backend's test support is THREE packages and deliberately not one: `jetstream/jetstreamtest` stands up a multi-member cluster, and importing it IS declaring one (see `solo`), so the single EXTERNAL server a suite restarts under a running client with another `max_payload` is `jetstream/externaltest`, and the ONE SIZE a test broker declares is `jetstream/brokertest` (`StoreMaxBytes`), because every embedded fixture — a node's, a cluster member's, a lone domain log's — needs that figure, and importing the first declares a solo suite while the second is the external server alone. A TEST BROKER THAT RESERVES A FIXED AMOUNT IS NEVER SIZED FROM THE DISK (one that reserves nothing, reserves only what it reports it has left, or whose subject is the derived limit still derives it): an unset limit is three quarters of the free space on a volume every package beside it shares, and a fresh node reserves several gibibytes before it does anything, so a boot passed or failed on how full the runner was; a declared limit is accounting, which nats-server never checks against the disk. `StoreIn` sets a node's store directory and the size TOGETHER, because the directory is what turns the limit into a derivation from its volume; `jetstreamtest`'s members take the figure unless their base states one; the shared fixtures of `engine` and `cmd/crewlet` and the cluster harness each have a case that goes red when they lose it, and `engine`'s `TestTheTestBrokerSizeHoldsAFreshNode` is the failure that names the cause and the constant when the register outgrows the figure, which then refuses every boot on every machine rather than only on a small disk. `externaltest`'s server has a fixed cap and its account states `StoreMaxBytes`, which a node reads back as an operator's limit; `StartUnstated` is the broker that states none. An embedded member LOCKS its configured store directory (`StoreDirLock`, through `flock`) before the server exists and lets it go only once it has stopped: nats-server accepts a second broker on one directory and both write it, and the node database's lock cannot stand in, because the engine starts its broker before it opens that database and the two paths are independent settings. `OpenCoordination` reaches the broker for its coordination store ALONE — dials an external one, or starts and owns an embedded one with the same readiness waits a queue makes — and builds no queue, because a queue PROVISIONS the engine's streams. |
| `coord` | Cross-process ownership: TTL leases with a monotonic fencing epoch, plus everything else a fleet has to agree on (`fleet.go`). THE DOCUMENT FAMILIES ARE GONE, both native backends left them for ordered logs, and what survived is the KEY GRAMMAR (`keys.go`), because every class the fleet still writes composes an id, a stream name or a node id into a subject token path and each of those can carry a byte a token cannot, the activation pointer (a COMPARE-AND-SET: there is no leader, so the flip is what stops two operators overwriting each other, and the sealed company document rides INSIDE its record as a JSON object, because as a key of its own a racing writer's body replaced the one the winning flip named; and its expectation is STRICT on both backends, ADR-0057: a write expecting a revision lands only while the pointer names it, and a pointer deleted or purged since the read is a race rather than nothing to have raced with, because every caller reads the pointer first and passes `ExpectAbsent` when it read none, and under the lenient rule this replaced the KV backend refused that write over the removal marker while the twin landed it, a 409 on every config write for up to three hours and a quarter; only `crewlet run -import-company` passes neither) and per-node apply status (which names the revision the node SERVES beside the one its epoch asked for, because the two differ exactly on a node that refused the fleet's revision, the epoch it last APPLIED beside the one it attempted, because a refusal says the node did not reach that epoch and never which one it did reach, and the one it BOOTS ON beside both, because the boot row moves only after an apply has succeeded and that write is local and best effort, so a node runs a revision it would not restart on for as long as a failed write takes to repair — and a reader keeping what a node needs in order to start, which the Kubernetes operator does with a revision's sealed credentials, would otherwise drop exactly what its next boot needs; `GET /fleet` reports all three per node), the completion ledger, the delivery dedupe, the custody claims (which one data node keeps each batch of a stateless node's events), the RATE COUNTER behind the notification valve and the Dashboard Assistant's per-person turn rate (a fixed window per bucket, at most `MaxRateWindow`, one minute, wide: the bucket keeps a few multiples of the widest window, bounded by the age it actually holds, and both backends refuse a wider one rather than restart its count mid-window), the TOKEN COUNTERS (one record per scope, `org`, a seat's `agent:` or, where a company caps the assistant, a person's `person:`, with a slot per calendar window, day, ISO week, month on the company's clock, each carrying its window's LABEL, so the roll onto a new window happens inside the compare-and-swap that counts the charge and there is no reset of any kind, ADR-0019), the capacity window (ONE record that is both the operation and the exclusion, beside the per-node admissions and acknowledgements the handshake is made of), the OBJECT STORE's backend record (create-only, compared at every boot, so a node naming another store refuses to start rather than splitting the files between two) and its collector's last report (and NO LOCK of any kind on an object, since a key named only by the write that uploaded it leaves a deletion nothing to race, ADR-0027), which seats a person PAUSED (one compare-and-set record per seat, filed under the seat's ID, as the inbox it holds is named; with no age, because an expiring pause is a resume nobody chose; every node watches them, holds a paused seat's inbox and takes that hold again before attaching a paused seat it acquires), each human seat's CHAT READ STATE (its person's read cursors, per-room mutes and do-not-disturb, one record per seat merged forward under a compare-and-set so two tabs never lose each other's progress — kept under `iam.RecordOwner`, so the seat's next holder inherits it as it does the seat's inbox; every node serving sockets WATCHES them and pushes a changed record to that seat's own tabs, derived from the record rather than told by the node that took the write, through the one watch the pauses use (`kv/watch.go`); with no age, because an expired cursor marks a room its reader had finished as unread; and in a bucket of its OWN although its retention is the positions register's, because it is the busiest record the fleet writes and as a class there its flushes would share the one raft group and leader the trim's inputs go through) and the company's SECRETS, last-write-wins for an operator's rotation, with a version on every row and the conditional writes (`CreateSecret`, `UpdateSecret`) the engine's own key material and a rekey need, a create for a company key's mint, an update at the version a rekey read, since a plain put of a key is how two minters each derive under their own, and a plain write of what a rekey read undoes whatever landed in between. A RESOURCE NAME IS SEGMENTED for the same reason, `seat:<id>` is the two segments `seat` and the seat's id (derived from its immutable handle, ADR-0013, so a lease, a mailbox and a pause all name a seat the same way on every node), so its lease and epoch keys carry the CLASS as a subject token and `ListLive`/`PreferredResources` take a `Class` rather than a prefix string: the membership read asks the broker for one class, the presence leases, instead of every lease in the fleet, and the sweep's hints for the seats instead of every epoch the deployment has ever minted. There is deliberately NO all-classes listing and no empty segment, because both fail the same silent way, a filter matching nothing answers exactly like a class with no members, and `seat:` writes a key nothing can decode, so the lease lands and no listing ever returns it, which every node reads as a free seat. Every "do I hold this?" is three-valued, held / definitively not / **unknown**, and treating unknown as loss tears a healthy company down over a two-second store blip. A LISTING IS THREE-VALUED TOO, every key live throughout it, or unknown, never a shorter list, and on the KV backend that is CERTIFIED rather than trusted, because a pass cannot tell it is complete: a renew rewrites a key behind the marker that ends the pass, and 848 of 3,634 membership reads missed a live lease. So each listing reads the stream's key index beside the pass and reads again, from the stream LEADER, every key the index names that the pass lost or delivered only as a delete marker, never through the bucket's own get, a direct get that a replica behind the quorum answers "not found", and a marker because a replica behind a re-creation serves one for a key live throughout, and an index answered while the stream has no leader is refused, so an election is unknown rather than a short fleet. A pass that goes five seconds without a delivery once the index has answered (`passIdle`, the client's own idle-heartbeat interval) is ENDED and certified, because the client's end marker never comes for a pass whose pending messages were removed before it reached them. The removal MARKERS that re-read pays for are swept by the maintenance duty from every ageless bucket after `MarkerRetention` (three hours, which is `MaxDutyTTL`), each purged through its own revision: a COST bound, never a correctness one, since no read here takes a marker as an answer and a create asks the leader what the key holds rather than conditioning on a marker a sweep may already have removed. Retention here is a BUCKET's age, never a per-write TTL, a delivery CLAIM included: it carries its own deadline only so that a lapsed claim is taken over by a compare-and-set, while the claims bucket's age (`MaxClaimTTL`, set by the longest claim anybody takes) is what removes it, and a claim asking for longer is refused rather than clamped. The DETACHED CODING RUNS' record (`SandboxRuns`) is the one opaque value here, and every change after its create is a DECISION its caller hands to `MutateSandboxRun`, which the store takes again on the record as it then stands whenever another writer landed first — for the store's patience, never a count of attempts, since one run's record takes every tool call its box makes over the bridge, from as many as twenty subagents at once; the memory twin decides outside its lock too, so a decision asked twice is what every test sees as well. A run is WATCHED by its own key (`WatchSandboxRun`): its first state certified as a listing's answer is, then each later state, a closed channel meaning "stopped hearing", never a delete. `NodeStatus` also carries each node's own `api.auth.admin` POSTURE (`serve` or `refuse`, meta key `admin`), absent rather than empty on a node that serves no API, because the posture is applied at decision time and never written, so a fleet mid-rollout legally disagrees and nothing else would say so; `/query/fleet` reports it per node A node's MODELS ride its presence lease beside its MCP servers (`NodeStatus.Models`, `NodeStatus.Runners`): one row per entry with only a 12-character prefix of its sign-in id, bounded text, never a fan-out. Two model classes live in the positions register, one record per entry: `llm_retry` (a person's retry, the later stamp kept, applied by its stamp) and `llm_device_sign_in` (a device sign-in in progress, whose lifetime is a record field its readers keep, since a bucket age restarts at every update). Two MCP classes live there too (ADR-0074): `mcp_listing`, one node's listing of one server, written under the node's PRESENCE EPOCH and counted only while that presence holds at that epoch (`LiveMCPListings`, the one filter), and `mcp_schema`, a tool definition's schemas stored once, create-only, under its digest; both bounded by the store as well as the writer, a listing's server and node stamped from its key. |
| `store` | The node's LOCAL databases: its OWN file (the audit event log, learning memory, config revisions), brought up by `OpenNode`, and the REPLICATED estate beside it — everything a state log's applier writes — opened on that handle by the engine (`OpenReplicated`; an adoption closes it with `CloseReplicated` and opens it again). Every data node holds it from boot whatever its company runs — holding is the node's, and `engine.HoldsEstate` is the one answer the engine and the backup read (`crewlet migrate` reaches it through Tier A's rule that a node without `data` is scratch, and refuses a scratch store), so a backup copies what the node holds and refuses a held estate that is closed at that instant. Two files because a snapshot is a copy of ONE of them: taken from a single file it would be a copy of everything followed by a delete, and with no in-place `VACUUM` the audit log's pages ride along in the artifact, the transfer, the checksum and the integrity check. No transaction spans the two, no read joins across them and nothing `ATTACH`es — a static walk holds it. The replicated estate reaches a caller as one of two TYPES: `ReplicatedHandle`, which can write and which only the framework, the appliers, the runtime and two named exceptions hold, and `ReplicatedReader`, which every reader is handed and whose one transaction the engine runs with writes refused (`query_only`) — so who can write it is a property of the type before `TestOnlyTheApplierWritesTheReplicatedEstate` reads the code, and `TestOnlyTheRuntimeReachesTheReplicatedEstate` keeps the reach through the node's own store to the runtime, the backup and the operator's offline commands, since a node without `data` has no file to reach. Each file's pool is its ORDINARY READERS — `store.max_open_conns`, or `max(8, GOMAXPROCS)` unset (two dashboards' worth of socket queries), never under two on the replicated estate — PLUS one connection held from open for identity work (`store.Identity`), so no read burst can starve the lookup that answers who is acting, PLUS one pinned writer per log the replicated estate carries: every one ADDED on top rather than taken out of the readers, an explicit setting included, because one taken out is a read burst queueing behind it with nothing naming the loss. Every connection of either file keeps the same 32 MiB page cache. **One file, one process** — exclusively owned, so nothing here has to be safe against a peer, and the migrator's advisory-lock idiom simply disappears: a migration run is serialised by the FILE's own claim, one run per path at a time, never by one lock for the process, behind which a test package spent most of its wall clock queued. The runner applies what THIS binary carries and STEPS OVER a version it does not — a newer build's, before a rollback — so the node boots, and `ForeignMigrations` (on the replicated estate's read-only `ReplicatedReader` too) names what it stepped over for the one caller that must not act as if those tables were empty: the object store's collector. The driver's capabilities are MEASURED ONCE PER PROCESS (`caps.go`) and reported on every open — the binary decides them, not the file — and `MaxVariables` reads the probe's own 32 766 ceiling, which the pinned driver reaches, so what bounds an applier's statement is `RowsPerInsert`'s 1 000-row cap (it used to read 2 000, the driver's limit on result COLUMNS, and every applier chunked to that). Anything the COMPANY has to agree on belongs in `coord` instead, and migrations 0010–0013 are what that rule cost when it was broken. The config history is that rule's sharpest case (`Configs`, ADR-0057): WHICH revision is the company is `coord`'s activation pointer, and `company_config` holds only this node's own facts about it, kept apart since node migration 0052 — the revision it BOOTS on (`is_active`, moved online by `Serve` alone), the FLEET's revision as it last saw it, runnable here or not (`is_fleets`, `SawFleet`), and an OFFLINE OFFER not yet published with the base it was built on and the instant the boot planned for it (`offered`, `offered_over`, `offered_at`). One column used to say all three, so a node that refused a revision booted on it at its next start and was refused before the engine ran, a write was walked back by a tick a moment behind, and an offline change was published over a fleet that had moved on. `Restorable` is the company as the node knows it when the pointer is gone (an offer built on the fleet's row, or on anything where the node has seen no fleet's row, else the fleet's row, else the boot row: a node that refused R and runs P restores R), and `Purge` keeps the boot row, the fleet's row, the chain behind each, read inside the deleting transaction, and every revision activated inside the window, whatever its creation date. One driver: `OpenNode`, `OpenReplicated` and `Pending` reach a connection through ONE helper, because the second path was the one that skipped the native-library preparation and panicked. A node without the `data` role opens `Scratch` (the file DISCARDED under the store's own lock before it is opened, so a database another process or another handle holds is refused rather than deleted beneath it; the handle says so, `DB.Scratch`, the one reading of the fact that cannot disagree with what the open did, which is where the reconciler learns that the boot row it writes outlives nothing) and holds no replicated estate, so a path that reaches for it is told `ErrNoEstate` rather than handed an empty database that reads as a company with nothing in it. And a replicated estate that is NOT OPEN answers `ErrNoEstate` rather than crashing — not open is a legitimate, documented state (a node without `data`, an adoption between its rename and its reopen, and after `Close`), so a goroutine already in flight reaches it honestly; the open handle sits in an atomic slot for the same reason, since an adoption swaps it while readers are running. A write whose line never reached the lock gives up with `ErrBusy`, the one failure here a caller clears by trying again. `Probe` asks a node database's lock WITHOUT OPENING THE FILE AND WITHOUT KEEPING THE CLAIM — how a `crewlet` command learns whether the engine runs — because opening the file to learn it would migrate a database the command never reads (and create one on a node that never booted), and keeping the claim would make a second command read the first as a running engine. `Hold` is its opposite, for the one caller that must exclude another process while holding no handle: `crewlet run` keeps the claim from the read it decides what to boot on with until the engine has opened the same file, so no offline command can write the row the boot already decided on, and the engine's own open shares the claim it gives back. |
| `statelog` | The durable-state framework, with TEN domains on it — the tracker, the knowledge base, the identity estate, the artifacts, their datastore, the Dashboard Assistant's conversations, chat and the seats' executor sessions strictly ordered and claiming identity, the vectors and each data node's usage compacted and claiming neither — and each domain is a site in several per-domain lists across the tree that fail SILENTLY when an eleventh is added and one is missed. One ordered stream per domain is the write-ahead log, N identical SQL copies are the durable state, and the checkpoint commits in the same transaction as the rows. NOT the store's own WAL — that is Turso's local journal, per file and invisible to any peer; this is an APPLICATION log a fleet derives its SQL state from, and the two share only a word. The write authority is one sentence — take ONE snapshot of your own rows, decide and form the expectation inside it, publish, let the broker arbitrate, never guess — and every clause buys a specific failure: a second read pairs an old decision with a new expectation and the broker accepts it, and an expectation formed from `version` rather than from the ARBITRATION ANCHOR wedges on the one case a gate creates. A write that cannot carry one expectation is SEQUENCED instead (`PatternSequenced`: the artifact datastore and chat's messages): it publishes with no expectation, the log orders it, its applier decides it from the record and its own rows and owes a VERDICT ROW for every record, and its writer reads that row back by operation id before it answers — because the applier's own transaction is the only place that knows what was decided, the same on every node. Three values with three readers (anchor, version, coverage), three write outcomes (`applied` / `pending` / `unknown`, never a bool) — and TWO PENDINGS, told apart by `Waited`: one waited and the node is behind, one never asked, and reported as a single fact a deliberate skip reads to an operator as a node falling behind under load. `NoWait` has exactly ONE correct caller, which holds its answer without the rows: a sign-in's session record, because the cookie carries the record's own position and every node that serves the API — a data node — validates against its own applier, so the row this node would wait for is a row nothing in the answer reads; it does NOT touch the session wait, which is the one that makes a decision read the caller's own previous write, and the FLOOR THEOREM in the package doc with all three of its enforced preconditions, because retry-at-zero on a trimmed anchor is safe only because of them and any consumer using per-subject conditional append inherits the hazard. AN OPERATION ID CARRIES THE INSTANT IT WAS MINTED — `<uuidv7>[.<name>][.<step>…]`, minted only by `NewOpID`, `DeriveOpID` (an id a retry must reproduce, at the start of its unit of work) or `StepOpID` (one append of a gesture), and held by `CheckCallerOpID` where a caller brings one back — because THE LEDGER ANSWERS FIRST: an operation already in it is `Collapsed` and its decide never runs, so a caller builds on nothing that decide would have filled; the ledger TRAVELS in every snapshot (`Divergent`) beside a watermark of how far back it may have lost rows, per table and per subject KIND (`Rows.LostBefore`, `statelog_ops_lost_kind`), so the identity estate's hourly session sweep leaves every other kind vouched for over the month; and an operation minted before the watermark with no row is `unknown` and `Unvouched` — ask another node — rather than decided twice. AN ID'S NAME AND EVERY STEP ARE ONE SEGMENT each — visible ASCII with no dot, at most `MaxOpSegmentBytes` (91, what the 128-byte bound on a caller's id leaves after the uuid and its dot) and not beginning with `~` — and the three minters write anything else ESCAPED, as `~` and 128 bits of its SHA-256 in hex, deterministic so a derived id still reproduces and injective since a segment written as itself never begins with `~`, because written as handed a file path or a dotted node id became steps of another operation in an id the engine's own caller rule refuses; `OpNamed` compares through the same rule, a keyed write's verb must be a segment as written since a reader finds the operation by it, a value composed into a segment goes through `OpNamePart` (64 bytes) so the verb beside it stays readable, and the dashboard's gate minter carries the same escape over a SHA-256 of its own, held to the engine's by `testdata/opname_vectors.json`. Every record is STAMPED with its writer's node and generation (`Request.Decide(tx, Stamp)`) and is a SIGNED FRAME (ADR-0031), so a reader that decodes straight off the log rather than through an applier is handed a `VerifiedReader` (a `VerifiedLog` over the log and the keyring's verifier), and a raw log does not compile there. Every identity-claiming log carries NODE GATES (`Domain.NodeGate`, `EvictionProbe`, `Evictions`), GENERATION RECORDS of its own for a per-log reanchor (`GenerationEncoder`), and a GATE RESERVE at the top of its ceiling that ordinary writes are refused `log_full` short of and an eviction or a readmission lands in, so the gesture that unpins a full log is not refused by the fullness it exists to clear — the larger of a sixteenth of the ceiling and the FLEET TERM, seven peers' largest appends and a mebibyte, because what the reserve absorbs is what peers hold in flight and that is a property of the LOG: every domain DECLARES its largest record (`StreamSpec.MaxRecordBytes`), the publisher refuses a record past it and the stream's `max_msg_size` holds a peer on another build to it, so the identity estate's (128 KiB) is sized by its own records rather than by the transport's 8 MiB, which a sixteenth of its 64 MiB floor could not hold even one of. A RECORD IS STAMPED WITH THE LOWEST VERSION ITS FIELDS NEED, never the build's: every domain declares the fields its records gained since the base format and the version each arrived at (`RecordFields`, one table per domain, which `statelogtest` certifies), because stamped below what its fields need an older node decodes the record, drops what it has no home for and applies the rest — its rows then differ from every upgraded node's for good, with nothing to report it — and stamped at the build's version every write the new build makes, the ones carrying nothing new included, is retained on every older node for the length of a rolling upgrade. A GATE record is the one an older node does not retain — it STOPS on one above the version it reads, because deferring an eviction would go on applying everything the evicted node appends — so a field a gate's record gains is marked as one (`VersionedField.Gate`), the gates stay at `GateRecordVersion` until it lands, and its writer waits for the fleet's census of read versions (below) before publishing one. One suite certifies every domain — `statelogtest`, in the `queuetest`/`coordtest` idiom, with a control expected to come back clean and a meta-test that hands it a domain which lies. Its ALARM TABLE lives here rather than beside any one subsystem, because an alarm is the framework's answer to "is this node doing its job" and everything above it asks the same question. Every alarm fires at a threshold ANOTHER decision already made — the grace that takes a copy out of service, the grace that stops a node serving a copy it cannot decode, the budget a caller was promised, the replay window an operator set a log's ceiling to hold — because an alarm with a threshold of its own is a second opinion about one event, and the two drift. One evaluation feeds every surface, so a gauge, a log line and a screen can never disagree about whether something is wrong. Three of its inputs are MEASURED rather than read off a gauge: a strict log's intake over the trailing day, counted out of the log's own records (every record of the day is still in it, `min_age` being floored at a day) and a POINTER in the reading, because a log that took in nothing and a log nobody measured — compacted, younger than the day, a node that has not ticked — are opposite facts; how many records a BLOCKED TRIM is keeping past `min_age`, the published age term against the log's first record, because every young fleet's trim is blocked (a first backup, first donors, a log nobody writes to) and only a trim blocked for longer than the window and a tick (`TrimInterval`, declared beside the arithmetic because it is the block's resolution) while its log keeps something past the window is `trim_blocked`; and how long the trim floor has been UNREADABLE, an age this node's own evaluations observed from first sighting to latest, because no record carries when it began — a health read has no memory, and an alarm fed one could only say "unknown now" and stood its own threshold in for the age. A deferral's age is the position heartbeat's first sighting read through `DeferredSince.Age`, the one arithmetic the copy's judgement and `deferred_old` both take, so the instant the alarm names is the instant the copy goes out of service — and a held record and an unreadable floor are evaluated PER LOG, like the headroom, the ceiling, the blocked trim and the census, because what the grace does and which re-anchor a floor needs are the log's: one alarm naming a node's oldest record said an identity record took nothing out of service while a younger tracker record had taken the copy out. What is per log is the EVALUATION and the LOG LINE, never the alarm: an alarm is ONE PER KIND and lists every log it stands on in `Alarm.Domains`, each with its own measurement (and, for a held trim, the term holding it and since when), its detail the most pressing log's — and a log the backup term holds past its replay window while `backup_age` fires is listed under `backup_age` rather than raising a `trim_blocked` of its own, since the backup is its remedy (a younger hold is listed under neither) — because raised once per log, one missing backup and four held logs read as five alarms on the health count while the gauge said two, and the screen drew one cause as five problems; so the count, the report, the gauge (one series per kind), the dashboard and `crewlet retention status` say one cause once. The TRACKER still writes its lines per (kind, log), forming an instance from each alarm's `Domains`: a second log joining a kind already up writes its own `alarm_raised` and a log leaving it its own `alarm_cleared` with its own start, because keyed on the kind alone it folded the second log into the first — no line when it started, none when the first cleared, and one final clear carrying the last log's detail against the first one's start. A node-wide kind is one instance, `backup_age` included, whatever logs it lists. THREE LOOPS PER DATA NODE make its recovery path real rather than declared, and each is useless without the other two: a POSITION HEARTBEAT (the trim takes a minimum across these rows, so a node that publishes nothing is one it cannot see and trims past — and each row names the record version the node's build reads on each log, which a successor's writer of a record this build cannot even DEFER — a gate above the version it reads — waits on through `statelog.Readers` over the same counted set; nothing this build writes needs it, since every domain writes the base format), a SNAPSHOTTER (one artefact series per data node, of the replicated estate it keeps whole from boot — taken and offered whether or not the node may WRITE: a barred machine back with its files writes nothing and may hold the fleet's only copy, and held to the write rule its joiner had no donor — declining while it is catching up, holds an undecodable record, is short of disk, or the trim counts no OTHER node on the estate's logs — and retrying soon rather than waiting out the interval, because every one of those clears at boot), and a DONOR (every data node offers its newest artefact, because a fleet whose only donor was down would have nothing to give). The recipient half alone is the worst state this has: it reports itself working while asking a fleet that can never answer. A FOURTH loop is the TRIM, and it is the one without which a log only grows: a fleet singleton (`retention`) evaluating six terms per log over the log's counted set — every live data node, and a listing that cannot be read publishing the floor BLOCKED rather than skipping the tick — purging what all six permit, and PUBLISHING what it concluded — because which term came lowest is one tick's conclusion and HOW LONG it has been lowest is a property of a series of them, and a duty that moves on a lease carries no memory across the move. A stream's CEILING is the one setting none of that can change while the engine is running: a resize is decided against the usage the log is at and a publisher makes that a moving quantity, and what retires a request the broker already queued is the broker PROCESS restarting. So it is a fleet-wide window across three modes — `normal` publishes, `maintenance` may write configuration, `seal` may NOT, which is precisely what makes a seal-mode acknowledgement evidence rather than a claim — driven by an evidence table (`maintenance.go`) that states every transition once. Two of its inputs have no other source: a backup ANNOUNCES what it covers when its manifest is written (the node holding the duty is rarely the node that took the copy and can never see a directory on another host), and the evictions come from the REPLICATED rows rather than a bucket, because that is where an eviction actually lives. SETTLED IS NOT APPLIED: the checkpoint moves past a record a node RETAINS, so a reader that decides from an ABSENT row reads `PrefixIn` — the prefix its own snapshot has APPLIED — and `DeferredIn` — whether any retained record's scope covers what it reads, asked of the whole deferral index and never the earliest record — inside the transaction that reads the rows; and an applier whose listeners hold a decision made before such a commit implements the optional `RetentionHook`, told after the commit and immediately before `Committed` how many records the batch retained, because a retained record never reaches Apply and so moved nothing an applier could have gathered The MODEL ALARMS are per ENTRY (`perEntry`, `Alarm.Entries`), the analogue of per-log: five `llm_*` kinds at the owner's fixed thresholds (30 days, 7 days, 15 minutes), with no setting; `Tracker.Latest` is what the Models page reads, so it cannot disagree with the health count. |
| `textindex` | The engine's own lexical search: the analyzer that turns a document into terms, and the BM25 arithmetic over the inverted list they are stored in. It exists because Turso has NO fts5 and no `fts` index method — `internal/store/caps.go` measures both once per process and reports them on every open, and behind the experimental flag that gates every `USING` clause the engine answers `unknown module name 'fts'`, so the gate is not what is missing, and the alternatives were refusing knowledge search on the only driver this build ships or embedding a search library with its own index format, file and backup story on every data node. PURE FUNCTIONS OVER VALUES — the SQL is `search`'s — so the ranking is testable without a database and an index rebuild cannot quietly change it. The BM25 tuning is a named PROFILE rather than two package-level numbers, because a knowledge base and a chat want opposite length normalisations from one arithmetic. K1 is 1.2 on BOTH — these documents repeat their subject constantly and a higher one ranks by verbosity instead of by coverage, and a person who says a word twice in a message means it as much as a page that says it twice — while B splits: `ProseProfile` at 0.75 for pages and work items, `ChatProfile` at 0.30 because a chat corpus is bimodal — one-line acknowledgements beside pasted blocks — and the prose tuning hands every query to whoever typed the shortest reply. A test pins each by the BEHAVIOUR it buys rather than by its value, and the zero profile scores nothing rather than ranking by a tuning nobody chose |
| `keyset` | A row's place in an ORDER as the ARMS that read the rows after it, or before it — for an order over c0 … cn, the rows equal on c0 … ci-1 and past the row on ci, at every depth, read deepest first and each in the order until enough are in hand — so that every arm is an equality prefix and one bound, ONE SEEK of the index the order is served by, and a later page costs what the first does. The obvious spelling is one lexicographic disjunction, and this engine's planner cannot seek on it: no conjunct bounds the order's leading column, so a board's next page walked the board from its first card and paging to a listing's end cost the square of its length; a range conjunct beside it still re-reads every row tied with the cursor, and a NOT NULL column defaulting to zero is one tie group of most of a table; and the arms' union as one predicate is that disjunction again. A NULL is a POSITION, last in both directions, with arms of its own (`IS NULL` its equality), and a column the caller declares never null gets none, which is what keeps its order one an index serves. PURE OVER VALUES — an arm is data, rendered only by `Arm.SQL`, tested against rows in memory in every direction with NULLs at every depth — and the tracker's cursor, an around task's neighbours (the arms before it, read with the order reversed) and the knowledge base's listing windows are read through it; where no index can hold the order (a column of a joined table) there is no seek to lose and `Disjunction` reads the arms as one predicate |
| `search` | BOTH HALVES OF KNOWLEDGE SEARCH, and the lexical one arrived here when the package that held it stopped projecting: `Indexer` maintains the BM25 inverted list over `textindex`'s analyzer, and it is the one reader in the tree that CROSSES THE ESTATE BOUNDARY — its sources are replicated rows and its index is this node's own, so every comparison is a batch from each side rather than a JOIN, and the walk that replaces the join repairs a drifted row where a version compare only ever noticed a source that moved. Plus the SEMANTIC half's arithmetic and the two-stage retrieval it composes, and the vector DOMAIN that fills it — the framework's second, and its first COMPACTED one. `Quantize`, `Hamming`, `TwoStage`, `Fuse`, `TrainIVF` and `ChooseProbes` are PURE FUNCTIONS OVER VALUES for the reason `textindex` gives for the same shape: a ranking that can only be exercised through a database is a ranking nobody re-measures. What the deterministic gate measures is the ARITHMETIC — that an exact rerank over a 1-bit candidate pool recovers the exact ranking at sufficient depth — and never recall on a company's own documents, which is a property of that corpus's distribution and of nothing else (0.29 to 0.98 over a family of embedding-shaped generators); `crewlet search eval` is what answers that, judged against the exact f32 scan so nobody authors a judgement. The floor is a CURVE, per corpus size, because recall from a sign code decreases with N. THE FIRST STAGE IS AN INDEX — ADR-0028: an inverted file over those same sign codes, k-means in HAMMING space with a majority-bit centroid and the lowest list winning a tie, because integer arithmetic is the only kind two CPUs agree on to the bit and a float k-means files one document in two lists. It is REPLICATED STATE on the vector log rather than a per-node build — per-node training gives every data node a DIFFERENT index, so one holder finds what another misses: the embedding duty trains it from a seed derived from the log, MEASURES it against the exact scan on held-out documents (kept out of the k-means, never their own answer), and publishes the centroids as a record whose own packed POSITION is the index's generation (a counter is minted twice by an unresolved publish), then re-files the older rows in reassign batches on a later tick — the batches' key RANGES cut once by the training and carried in the centroids record, each batch on a subject naming its generation and its number and every index record's scope NESTED under the centroids', because ranges re-cut at each publication left a compacted log a replaying node could not tile — and a search reads every row not yet re-filed in full beside the lists it probes, so a rollout never hides a document. The probe count is that measurement's answer and never a knob — the smallest meeting `FloorAt` with NO HEAD MISS in EVERY QUERY SHAPE a search is issued in (unfiltered, each source, one container), which is the evaluation's own pass rule and the term that binds at scale — and an index that would read more than half its lists is NOT INSTALLED: below `IVFMinCorpus`, on a corpus with no topics a list can find, and in a space the index was not trained in, the first stage is the full scan it always was. A NARROWED search reads lists until it has seen as many of its own rows as an unfiltered one reads (`ProbeCount`), and scans past half of them — the unfiltered count kept 0.949 of a pages-only answer and under 0.75 of one container's. The duty re-measures an installed index daily and retrains when it misses; it judges imbalance against what its training achieved, never the mean alone (identical codes pile into one list whatever the seed); and it acts only on a node that has applied the whole log. The covering `kb_vectors_bin_ivf_idx` (replicated 0033) is the one index that table carries, and it does not contradict 0004: a probe reads a SHARE of the rows, which is the premise 0004 dropped every index for lacking. The SQL is here too and the estate boundary is why: the vectors are replicated and the lexical index is this node's own, so no read joins them and the fusion is over two ranked lists of ids rather than a UNION. Every indexed document also carries a SHARD in both estates — a stable hash of its own identity into 64 buckets, unrelated to every other thing this engine partitions on, so a source that is re-filed keeps its bucket and the division follows the corpus rather than the company's shape. Nothing routes on it: a data node holds every bucket and reads every bucket, and what the column buys is that a scan's cost is measurable per BUCKET RANGE rather than per corpus held. NEITHER ESTATE INDEXES IT, which is the same conclusion migration 0004 reached about the vector scopes and measured at 1 min 44 s against 50 ms: a bucket range is seekable, so an index over it is how a planner stops driving on the term and starts walking a sixty-fourth of the corpus instead. The FAN-OUT is here too: a coordinator divides the 64 buckets across the live data nodes, each scans its own range, and the merge is SCORE-WITHIN-METHOD FIRST and RRF ACROSS METHODS SECOND — because RRF combines different rankers over one corpus, and run over one ranker across disjoint slices it ranks by PLACEMENT (a rank-1 on a weak slice contributes 1/61 against a rank-2 on a strong one at 1/62, so 0.10 outranks 0.98). Taking FuseN per slice is EXACT rather than sampled, because the slices are disjoint; and BM25 stays comparable across them for the one reason a design that divided STORAGE could not borrow — every data node holds the whole corpus, so THE SCAN IS BUCKET-LIMITED AND THE STATISTICS ARE GLOBAL. An assignment that does not answer is NAMED (`buckets_answered` / `buckets_missing` / the absent nodes) rather than silently truncated, because the coordinator holds the whole corpus and could have answered alone — a short result set is indistinguishable from a short corpus. EVERY READ OF ANOTHER DOMAIN'S TABLE is a NAMED, EXPORTED statement its consumer runs exactly — the task and page tables the lexical index and the embedding duty walk, fetch, check and count, the backlinks' edges, the window report's bodies — so the gate of the package that owns the table can plan the statement that runs, never a copy; and the embedding duty selects its stale pages by walking `pages_heads_live_updated_idx` (replicated 0065, partial on the corpus's own predicate) in update order, as its task half walks the tracker's update index — where it read and sorted every page row in the company on every tick. Its two plan gates, the vector tables' and the lexical index's, refuse a plan answering a per-row aggregate from its whole child table (`storetest.Decorrelated`), as the tracker's and the knowledge base's index gates do, and the lexical gate plans the INDEXER'S OWN statements — each a named constant or builder — and holds them complete with `storetest.StatementLiterals` over `kb_docs` and `kb_postings`, because the hand-written copies it planned before had drifted from what the indexer runs and could not fail. `ChatIndexer` is the SECOND corpus and it is nothing like the first: chat is keyword-only (ADR-0071), its walk is FORWARD ONLY over `chat_messages.version` with a durable watermark rather than a lap by id, a batch ending on a whole position, and its statistics are MAINTAINED rather than counted and kept PER ROOM (`chat_index_rooms`, `chat_index_terms`) — a term's documents counted off the postings is a range count the length of the term's list, invisible at a few thousand documents and some 160 ms a term at a year of chat — because a search is RANKED BY THE ROOMS IT SEARCHES, never the company's, so a score never moves with what is said in rooms its asker cannot read. What a forward walk cannot see is a row that VANISHED, so the two things that delete chat rows are followed explicitly — the retention prune by a ROTATION over the rooms the index holds, comparing each room's oldest document with the oldest message its transcript still has, the compliance erase by a forward walk of its own over the markers' positions (`chat_deletions.version`), because the markers are never pruned and a pass over all of them grows with the deployment — and an estate REPLACED under it is announced (`RequestRebuild`) and walked again from the start, since nothing a forward walk reads can show it — its index dropped a batch a sweep from a DURABLE mark, because emptied in one transaction it holds the node's only writer for as long as the index is large and a drop that stopped halfway would resume the walk over a half-emptied index. Its search carries a VIEWER: a private room's contents are not a fact about the company the way a page is, so a query names the rooms its asker may see and one that names none is REFUSED rather than read as everything; a hit is hydrated from the transcript row and carries a snippet cut from it, because the index keeps no second copy of what anybody said, and a frequent term contributes its NEWEST postings, the scan walking its list backwards by UUIDv7 id |
| `events` | The envelope and the typed-payload registry. Two load-bearing properties: evolution is additive-only, and an event type this build does not know decodes, round-trips and re-publishes losslessly — a rolling upgrade puts unknown types on the wire, and dropping them would make every upgrade an outage |
| `org` | The organization model: the hierarchy, the seats that hold it, and the identity derivation every node shares. A seat's id is DERIVED — a UUIDv5 over (org name, HANDLE), ADR-0013 — so every node computes the same id with no database and no running instance, which is what lets the node that wins a delivery route to a seat it is not running; a handle is IMMUTABLE, so a document that changes one removes the seat and creates another, and there are no aliases. A pool miss means "not on this node", never "does not exist" |
| `iam` | The engine's IDENTITY VOCABULARY: who is acting, what sort of thing they are, how far through enrolment they got, and which permissions they carry. A LEAF, and that is the point, config, the tool layer and the query registry all have to be able to SAY "principal" without any of them pulling in a password hasher, a store client or a coordination backend, so this package holds VALUES ONLY and a test walks its imports to keep it that way. THREE NAMESPACES THAT CANNOT COLLIDE, by the SHAPE OF THE VALUE rather than by a uniqueness check somebody has to remember to run: a seat handle is ONE SEGMENT, a person's login joins segments with DOTS, a machine's handle joins them with a COLON, so both of the grammars here require a separator, which is the one thing a seat handle can never carry, and they require DIFFERENT ones, and both stop at `MaxLogin` (64, a seat handle's own width, because a login is a SUBJECT TOKEN the broker indexes for the life of the deployment and sits in the author column beside a handle), enforced IN the grammar so every surface that enrols, renames or composes one inherits it; nothing bounded a login before. The SEAT HANDLE's grammar lives here too (`ValidSeatHandle`, which `org.ValidHandle` is), so every surface that names a seat asks one rule, a looser address rule elsewhere once accepted `jane.doe`, `ci:release` and `token:ops` as seats, which `OwnerOf` then sent to the directory, so "cannot collide" held only where `org` was asked. And EACH GRAMMAR BELONGS TO ONE KIND (`ValidLoginFor`), which disjointness alone does not give: `token:<id>` is the login a Tier A token acts under and the directory row holding it is what binds that token to a seat, so while either kind could take either shape a person redeeming an invitation as `token:ops` made the deployment's own credential act as their seat, and config refuses a Tier A token id unless `token:<id>` is itself a machine handle (`ValidTokenID`), since a token IS a machine. That replaced the `operator:` prefix the operator's MCP surface used to write, which showed one person as two people three rows apart in the audit feed because a work commit carried the bare id and a page commit the prefixed one; the kind is a COLUMN now. Nothing here decides anything, a gate asks `Can`, a store writes what `ActorFor` returns, and a caller's OWN RECORD (inbox, pins, priorities, personal views) is written AND read under `RecordOwner`, the name `ActorFor` writes with: the seat for a person, every person holds one (ADR-0032), and for a token or service account the directory binds to one, the login for a credential bound to none. There were two answers, the tools writing under the login and the query surface reading under the seat, so an unbound caller's record was written where nothing read it, and `NamesSelf` says a bound principal's login names the same record their seat does. SOMEBODY ELSE's record is `OwnerOf`'s, the one function every tool, question and route resolves a name through: a LOGIN is its holder's record (the seat they hold), read through the `Holders` seam the engine implements over the identity directory and the running org, the one read this leaf is HANDED rather than makes, and only once the caller's `MayLook` gate has let it be, so the ORDER (the authority first, the directory second) is enforced in the leaf rather than remembered at three call sites and a refusal comes back as a `LookRefused` carrying the caller's own answer; a `token:<id>` login is held by the Tier A entry of that id on the answering node and by nobody when none declares it; and a login is never matched against the roster (`NamesLogin`), because `jane.doe` resembled the seat `jane` ("Jane Doe") and `set_priorities` replaced that seat's queue; anything else is handed back UNSETTLED for the chart. `ErrNoHolder` and `ErrHolderUnseated` are the two answers, and every other error is UNKNOWN. What a request actually presented is `From`'s THREE-VALUED job, because the engine knowing you are nobody and the engine being unable to tell are different answers. `ActorFor` answers the CREDENTIAL too (`Actor.OperatorID`): a principal's `Via`, `pat:<credential id>` for a machine token acting as its owner, `session:<lineage>` for a browser session, or its login, because a token is composed AS its owner and without it every write one made was recorded exactly as the owner's own, and a session's person is the author already, so repeating their login said nothing about which sign-in wrote; the `pat` and `session` classes are refused to every machine login so each name only ever means its credential. `NormalizeEmail` is here for the same reason the name grammars are: TWO DOMAINS MATCH ON AN ADDRESS and must never disagree, the party registry (`notify.Registry.ByEmail`) folds a seat's resolved address and a payload's with it so a vendor payload resolves to a SEAT, and `iamdomain` derives a person's keyed blind from it so a sign-in resolves to a PERSON, and written twice one address would reach a seat and a different person. FIVE PERMISSIONS (ADR-0033): `admin`, and ONE PER APP — `worklet`, `doclet`, `artifacts` and `chatlet`, the fourth appended LAST so every list drawn in the vocabulary's order kept its first members — because what a company decides about somebody who joins is whether they RUN it and which of its apps they USE; the eleven grants this replaced carved the engine by surface, nobody inviting a teammate could tell them apart, and a person handed none of the right ones saw nothing. `Apps` is THE ONE PLACE AN APP IS DECLARED, an agent seat holds exactly it, the dashboard's picker and its contract row are held to it, authz's walks require every app to open a person-facing verb, and `Expand` is THE ONE COMPARISON: a set plus every app when it holds admin, a FRESH slice that never aliases, idempotent, so `Can`, a node's posture, a token's mint against its owner, `MayConfer`, an invitation's bound and Tier A's check all read "admin includes every app" from one function rather than six copies of it. A NAME THIS BUILD DOES NOT KNOW IS KEPT AND IGNORED in a replicated row, `Valid` false, `Can` never matching, `Expand` carrying it through, and may be removed and never added; Tier A refuses one, naming `AllGrants`, because a file an operator wrote is a typo rather than an upgrade. `AdminPosture` (`posture.go`) is `api.auth.admin` as a value, `serve` or `refuse`, here in the leaf for `SecondFactor`'s reason, and `Effective` is what a principal composed on a node carries: `Expand` FIRST and admin clamped SECOND, so a refusing node keeps every app, in `AllGrants` order with unknown names dropped; an invalid posture, the zero included, confers NOTHING (a non-nil empty set), which fails closed. `admin` IS HOST ACCESS by decision (one permission, no runtime split), because the configuration it writes runs commands on every engine host (`mcp_servers`, a seat's `mcp_env`, a `providers.llm` model, a harness the host runs, and on a `run_in: direct` entry every seat's executor, a fully permissioned harness, on the host itself), so it is conferred like shell there, and a machine token may carry it (a deploy job applying configuration is what a token is for), what keeps a token off a person-present gesture is authz's `presence` rows and `iamdomain`'s refusal of an administrative record written through one, not a permission it cannot carry. A unit's LEAD writes the seats and units inside it WITHOUT admin (authz's SUBTREE), every field but a credential's or a keyed one's, so the model chains and sandbox cells the settings OFFER are a lead's to hand to their own seats, while what is offered, every credential and every setting stay admin's. Nobody confers what they do not hold, on anybody, themselves included. THE PERMISSION IS THE ONLY AUTHORITY A PRINCIPAL CARRIES beside its relations, and EVERYTHING ELSE IS EVERYBODY'S, the overview a principal holding no permission still reads, while the "colleague level" that once stood beside the grants, stored on every person, invitation and token, configured on a Tier A token, was decided on by nothing, so it is gone rather than kept as a setting that changes nothing. `proof.go` holds the two VALUES config reads about proof (`SecondFactor`, `MinPasswordChars`) rather than `iam/credential`, for this package's own premise: internal/config has to be able to say "a second factor is required" without linking a password hasher, and `Recency`, whether a rule asks for a recent proof, spelled as the `api.auth.session` key that sizes the ONE window (`step_up`), which `Principal.Proved` judges against the principal's one deadline (`ReauthAt`), one window over every sensitive gesture, as GitHub's sudo mode keeps one, because a second, shorter window sent people to re-prove inside the hour they had proved in while what keeps a stolen machine credential from those gestures was never a proof's age. A THIRD CREDENTIAL CLASS, `assist:<grant>` (`AssistPrefix`, `AssistName`, `ValidAssistName`), is a request the Dashboard Assistant made for a person: the principal is the person, composed from their rows as their own browser's request would be, `Principal.Assisted` reads the grant off `Via` once, and `Principal.AssistScope` (read or exec) is set exactly when `Via` names one, on a person and nobody else (ADR-0050) |
| `authz` | MAY this party do this, to this? ONE FUNCTION over ONE TABLE, pure over values apart from the chart. SIXTEEN authority classes (everybody, app, any app, app-or-asked, self, own record, own or lead, SAVED VIEW, container, TIDY, SUBTREE, destructive, authored, admin, directory and SHARED) and a row per verb, because there are dozens of verbs and sixteen ways to decide one, a table keyed on the verb would state each rule dozens of times and drift on whichever copy somebody edited. THE APP IS A FIELD ON THE ROW (`rule.app`, ADR-0033): a `ClassApp` row admits whoever holds it, a RELATION row admits its relation only BESIDE it (`withApp`, so a lead who does not use Worklet does not set a project's policy in it), and admin passes every app through `iam.Expand`; `AppFor`/`Shows` are the ONE per-app filter every cross-app answer's rows go through (a feed, the decisions list, a page's `linked_from`, a knowledge answer's two halves). APP-OR-ASKED is `get_work_item` and `comment_on_work_item`: the app, OR the person an OPEN ask on the item names (`Object.Asked`, resolved by `worklet.AskedOn`), and an unread object without the app is `ErrUnresolved` so the tool reads the item and decides again, the asked person reads it and posts their answer (or posts it again once it landed, which writes nothing), nothing wider. TIDY is removing/restoring an item, renaming/trashing/restoring a page and archiving a chat room (`chat.channel.archive`, asked inside `update_channel` on the stored room): the container's lead, admin, OR the AUTHOR (`Object.Author`: an item's reporter, a page's or a room's creator), the purge staying admin's; a unit's room has its UNIT as its container (`leadsByKind` asks `LeadsUnit`), and a named room has none, so it is its creator's and admin's and anybody else is refused `not_author`. AUTHORED is a remark's — a comment, a chat message — and its admin path is decided on the UNREAD remark (`Object.Unresolved`), which is moderation: an admin takes a message down in a room they cannot read without being shown it, and anybody else is `ErrUnresolved`, reads it as themselves and is decided again on its author. EVERYBODY is the overview's verbs and the two route admissions a tool then decides (`work.thread`, `iam.seats.read`). THE SUBTREE is the org chart's, two verbs (`config.org.write`, `config.org.read`) asked of ONE PLACE at a time: `admin` is the admin path, and otherwise the principal must lead the unit the place names or a unit above it (`Chart.LeadsUnit`), the company root being nobody's and refused `not_lead` like a unit somebody else leads, since the remedy is the same permission; `/config` asks it of every place a write reaches on both sides of it (`config.DiffOrg`), and a write nobody has read yet passes the route for anybody bound to a seat and nobody else, asking whether they lead SOMEBODY would refuse a lead alone in the team they are about to staff. "NO AGENT MAY TAKE THIS" IS A FIELD ON THE ROW (`humanOnly`) and not a class, because it is a fact about the VERB that composes with whatever class decides it, purging takes admin AND a principal that is not a seat, archiving a project takes the container's lead AND the same, and as a class of its own (`ClassPurge`, now gone) it was already two spellings of one rule; it is checked BEFORE the class, so an agent is told it is a seat rather than that it lacks a permission it plainly holds. "NOTHING UNDOES THIS" is another (`irreversible`, read through `IrreversibleOf`: the purges, erasing and pruning a chat room, a room's horizon), deciding nothing in `Decide` and read by what shows the gesture to a person before they make it, since a route's method cannot say it (a purge is a POST, a horizon a PUT); a walk holds every such row to a recent proof. Its MIRROR is `seatOnly`: `a2a_ask` is an AGENT's alone, with no permission, the role's registry deciding which seats are offered it — it replaced an ask riding on the tracker's permission, so a role that did not use the tracker could ask nobody anything — checked where `humanOnly` is, and refused to a person or a token `seat_only` naming no grant; a walk refuses a row that is both. A THIRD mark, `seated`, is a verb made AS a seat (`answer_knowledge`, and EVERY chat verb, the reads included — a room renders a speaker by seat and its membership is seats): a principal `iam.ActorFor` names by no seat (a Tier A token, a service account bound to none) is refused it `unseated` naming no grant, checked after `seatOnly` and before the class, so `builtin.Admits` (and with it `viewer.acts`) never offers such a caller a control its every press is refused; a walk refuses a seat-only row marked seated. A SAVED VIEW is its own class because the table is keyed on the ACTION: two verbs would let the CALLER pick which question is asked, and a caller writing a shared view would ask the personal one, so the PAYLOAD decides, an owner making it that person's record and none making it its container's (a project's lead, a unit's, a person's page, or the workspace, which is the admin path alone). And a save REPLACES a view whole by id, so the payload is only half of it: `save_work_view` asks the same action again on the STORED view's owner and container once it has read them (`worklet.ViewPrior`), and the write refuses a prior that no longer holds, decided on the arguments alone, anybody who may keep a view could overwrite a project's shared tab or a colleague's personal view. A VERB WITH NO ROW IS REFUSED rather than defaulted, which is how a new verb ships ungated and ships looking correct; three walks fail the build on a verb with no rule, a class no verb reaches, and a PERMISSION NOTHING ASKS FOR, the last of those once caught a read grant opening nothing at all, and the vocabulary's own walks beside them: every app is the app of a non-seat-only row, every app row admits a holder and refuses a non-holder naming exactly `[app]`, seat-only and human-only are checked before the class, and EVERY DOTTED VERB IS ASKED by a non-test call site, read off the source with `go/ast`, so a verb nothing asks cannot sit in the table deciding nothing. Those three hold the table against ITSELF, which a stale row satisfies perfectly, so a FOURTH walk lives in `internal/agent/builtin` and holds it against the tools this build registers over BOTH surfaces, in both directions, it caught two rows that outlived the tracker's goal verbs and seven tools a seat uses every turn with no rule at all, and a FIFTH beside it goes one level down, holding the ARGUMENT NAMES each tool's object is read from against that tool's own JSON Schema: four rows named arguments their tools do not have, so `my_work`, `mark_inbox`, `set_pins` and `save_work_view` read an empty owner on every call and were gated shut for everybody but an admin. SHARED decides ONE ARTIFACT per object rather than per app (`KindArtifact` belongs to no app): the caller's ROLE on it is the BEST of owning it, a share naming their seat, a unit they sit in (`Chart.MemberOf`) or the whole company, and leading the agent that owns it (`Object.OwnerIsAgent`, never a person's), against the row's least role (`rule.minRole`, refused at zero by a walk); the `artifacts` app gates AUTHORING only, asked beside the role on the authoring rows. It resolves every relation rather than the first that admits, because the role is what a write is STAMPED with: `Decision.Standing` carries it, `StandingOn` is the effective-role resolver every surface asks, and a role an unreadable chart arm might raise is UNSETTLED (`ErrUnsettled`) rather than stamped lower. No relation is NOT FOUND (`Decision.NotFound`, rendered `404 not_found` with no reason, byte for byte an unknown id), so a private artifact's existence never leaks; the admin path admits after the relations and before any chart failure and is marked `Decision.Elevated` only where it alone admitted, which the surfaces record; an agent is capped at contributor on work it does not own unless `artifacts.agents_manage_others` (`Object.AgentsManageOthers`) lifts it, refused `seat_refused` naming nothing above it; `DecideWithin` decides an agent's verb AGAIN for the requester whose turn it acts in (`Ceiling`), sparing only the agent's edits to its own work (`rule.steerable`), never a read and never a share; and `Visible` shows an artifact row in a cross-kind answer by relation alone, never by the admin path, while `Shows` never shows a per-object kind by its kind. The [Chart] seam is CONSUMER-DEFINED, SIX relations: who leads a person, a project, a unit and a PAGE CONTAINER, whether somebody leads ANYBODY, and who SITS in a unit or below it; every one is THREE-VALUED. The fifth is asked of a record NOBODY HAS RESOLVED YET (`Object.Unresolved`): somebody else's LOGIN is resolved by the identity directory, whose answers differ by login, so a person verb decides first what it can without the record, `admin` admits, a class with no lead path and a caller who leads nobody are refused with EXACTLY the decision a seat they do not lead gets, and a caller who leads somebody is answered `ErrUnresolved`, the one unknown a caller acts on: resolve it, then decide again on the record. Decided after the lookup, a login nobody holds was refused while a held one this node could not place answered 503 naming its seat, so anybody read which logins exist off the difference. The first four are questions never asked with another's key: a unit declares its tracker project in `project:` and its container in `space:`, so the project relation asked with a container key matched only a company that spelled the two alike and refused the lead everywhere else with no error to notice, which is what `LeadsContainer` exists to make impossible. Three-valued because the seam replaces a `func(...) bool` whose body opened `if c == nil || c.Org == nil { return false }`, so a node booting or applying a revision told every lead in the company that they lead nothing, while reporting itself healthy. Err is UNKNOWN and answers 503, never 403, and admin is checked BEFORE the chart, so a lagging node never tells an operator it cannot tell, while a caller short of a relation row's app is refused naming it whatever the chart says (`withApp`), since no answer the chart could give would admit them. Route policy travels with the REGISTRATION and the wrapper is the only reader of `r.Pattern`: a middleware runs before the mux matches, so anything it decided from would be a second router. A refusal the router renders itself is the ENGINE'S ENVELOPE (`EnvelopeRefusal`), `403 unauthorized` carrying the rule's `reason` and `Decision.Grants`, the MINIMAL cover, any one of them, added to what the caller holds, admits: `[app]` on an app row or to a lead missing the app, `[admin]` to a caller missing a relation, the row's own set where any one of them would do (`rule.anyOf`, the one ANY-APP row naming the apps its gesture draws on, never `iam.Apps`, which admitted a third app's holder to a search of nothing), which a walk holds against `Decide` over every verb, kind and grant; `503 unavailable` with a `Retry-After` for UNKNOWN, because it was `http.Error`, and `/iam` answered every refusal in `text/plain` no client of the rest of the API could branch on. `RefusalFields` is the ONE MAPPING from a refused decision to its status, code and detail — `step_up_required` naming the window for a stale proof, `token_refused` for a machine token where a person must be present, `unauthorized` with the rule's reason and grants for everything else, and `404 not_found` with nothing beside it for a caller with no relation to an artifact — and `EnvelopeRefusal` writes what it returns, as the operator surface does beside its own tool sentence (never beside a not-found, which must read as an id nothing holds), `/config`'s admission beside its refused parts and `/iam` — its answer to a record's refusal, its mint route's and its seat listing's — and the overview's REST mirrors, the socket's handshake and its query and watch frames (through `stream.RefusedFor`), the query surface's REST answer and `/auth`'s step-up, because a surface answering with fields of its own spelled the mapping again, and its copy answered a token's refusal `unauthorized`, sending the caller after a permission no token could carry; the two detail halves it composes are unexported, so a refusal's fields are reachable only beside the code the mapping picks, and a walk in `internal/api` refuses any of the three codes handed to a call, assigned or returned anywhere under it. `Combine` decides a GESTURE OF SEVERAL DECISIONS — a configuration write's parts — as `Decide` would have answered it as one question, in `Decide`'s own order: a bar on the caller first, the one `Decide` reports first, as it stands; then the refusals on authority, their grants the smallest set any one of which admits every part and their reason the parts' own where they agree and `no_grant` where they do not; then an unknown, asked only once nothing refused, since no answer that part could get would admit a gesture another part already shuts; then a stale proof last, because no earlier answer is one a fresher proof changes — and one decision is itself. A REQUEST IS JUDGED AT ONE INSTANT (`DecidedAt`): a `Router` route stamps the instant its guard decided at, keeping one an outer route already stamped, and every later check of the same request — `Admit` inside a handler, every part of a `/config` write and its address check, the human write surface's and the artifacts surface's checks of a row they read once the route admitted the request (an artifact's gate, which a tool reaches with no route, keeps its own clock there: `RouteInstant`), the tool gate (`builtin.Decide`) a tool-backed route dispatches into — decides at it, so a proof the route accepted is never refused as stale while the body is read; a socket's watch and its query frames keep their own clock. The one authority concern that IS outside is the canonical-path refusal, because Go's own mux cleans and redirects before it matches, measured, a check inside the route handler never fired. EVERY ROW ALSO STATES HOW RECENTLY ITS VERB NEEDS THE PRINCIPAL TO HAVE PROVED WHO THEY ARE, the STEP-UP, `iam.Recency`: none or `step_up`, ONE window (config, setup, credential and deployment writes, a reveal, the three purges, every identity-directory write, how somebody proves who they are whichever surface changes it, and ending everybody's sessions), and a row may ask its SELF arm another answer (`selfRecency`), which is how ending your own sessions asks none while an administrator ending somebody else's asks `step_up`, because it was a setting nothing read and a week-old cookie reached every one of those; asked AFTER the class admits, so a caller who could never take the verb is told what they lack, and refused `403 step_up_required` naming the window, the code `/auth` already used. A zero recency fails a walk (read as none it is a step-up verb shipped open) and so does a TOOL row asking for one or needing a person present — and so does any verb a tool asks the same gate from INSIDE itself once it has read a row (archiving a project, renaming a page), read off `internal/agent/builtin`'s code by the one tree scan the dotted-verb walk shares, since a walk over the undotted verbs alone held what the gate is asked first and none of what it is asked next — because a seat has no keyboard and `/operator/mcp` is not a step-up surface; a credential with nobody at a keyboard is fresh by construction, stamped by the guard, a Tier A token, the development principal and a MACHINE TOKEN alike, so the clock keeps no token off the gestures that need a person present, and two other locks do, both on the row: "A PERSON MUST BE PRESENT" IS A FIELD (`presence`, `authz.Presence`) beside `humanOnly`, and `Decide` refuses a principal acting through a machine token (`iam.Principal.MachineToken`, off its `Via`) `token_refused` on every arm or on every arm but the self arm, revealing a secret, every directory write, ending everybody's sessions and changing how somebody proves who they are (`iam.credential.proof`, its own verb beside the token verb `iam.credential.write` for exactly this) need a person on every arm, while minting or revoking a token and ending sessions spare the token's own owner, so a leaked token still revokes itself and signs its owner out, because it was a refusal each route remembered while the table admitted the token on the same verb; and the SECOND lock is `iamdomain`'s, which refuses every administrative identity record written through a machine token (`ErrTokenRefused`) whatever it carries, since a token MAY carry admin. A walk derives the presence rows from the table rather than a list: a row's presence refuses a token on exactly the arms it names, and a token holding admin is refused every such gesture about anybody but its owner. Ending every session in the company is ONE permission now, `admin`, with a person present and a recent proof, the route and the record ask the same thing. `Decide` takes `now` as an ARGUMENT for it, which is what keeps it pure over values AND EVERY ROW SAYS WHAT ITS VERB IS TO THE DASHBOARD ASSISTANT (`rule.assist`, ADR-0052): `AssistRead` (a read grant takes it) or `AssistApprove` (only the one request an exec grant binds), the zero refused by a walk and read as never by `Decide`. PARITY WITH THE PERSON decides it: the assistant takes every verb its person may, but for the HUMAN-ONLY LIST (`assistantNever`, a reason per verb, the ONE place `AssistNever` is spelled): the sign-in and session ceremonies (`iam.credential.write`, `iam.credential.proof`, `iam.signin.write`, `iam.session.end`, `iam.invalidate`), a credential or a secret in clear (`secrets.reveal`, `secrets.write`, `setup.connect`, `artifacts.ticket`), the assistant's own `assistant.*` verbs, an effect a grant would falsify or hide (`answer_knowledge`, `artifacts.ready`), and the seat-only verb (`a2a_ask`), each entry with a plain sentence the person is told per family (`AssistNeverSentence`, written as the refusal's `detail`). `Decide` refuses a listed verb `assist_refused`, and a read grant an approve row or a person-present row `assist_scope`; an EXEC grant takes a person-present row, because the person's approval of that one request is the presence it asks for. Both bars sit before the class, keep the artifact rows' concealment, and read the credential's class rather than its spelling; a walk pins the list verb by verb and by family, and one in `internal/agent/builtin` holds every assist-read verb to a read-only tool. A PUT route's `Policy.Absent` declares, where it is mounted, what a write to an address holding nothing does (creates it, or is refused with where one is added instead), refused at the mount on any other method or saying neither, so the Dashboard Assistant proposes a create only where the handler takes one and an API test holds every previewable PUT to a declaration. A POST route's `Policy.Effect` declares what it does to what its path names (it creates, rewrites, destroys or only reads it), refused at the mount on any other method and on `EffectCalls`, the act route's, which the assistant's route table declares where it binds that route, so a POST is titled and rated by what it does rather than called a create, and an API walk holds every POST to one |
| `authz/orgchart` | `authz.Chart` over ONE `org.Organization`: who leads a person, a project, a unit and a page container, whether somebody leads anybody, and whether a seat SITS in a unit or below it (its own unit chain compared by pointer; leading a unit from outside it is not sitting in it), which is what a share with a team admits — pure arithmetic over a tree, tested against a tree rather than through a running engine (measured, the walks inside the engine's own methods could have their loops deleted with that suite still green). Its own package because TWO callers hold a tree: the engine asks about the company it is RUNNING (`engine.ChartAuthority`, which delegates here per call), and a `/config` write asks about the two DOCUMENTS it compares — the revision it replaces and the one it proposes — which the running company is neither of. A nil tree answers `authz.ErrNoChart`, never `false` |
| `config` | The two tiers. Tier A (`Bootstrap`) is ops-owned, on disk, restart to change, including the retention estate (`stream.tracker_retention`, `store.snapshot_dir`, `retention.backup_owner`), because how a deployment backs up and how long its disk holds a replay window are facts about the OPERATOR rather than about the company.
