A PERSON HOLDS ONE HUMAN SEAT FOR AS LONG AS THEY ARE HERE (ADR-0032, the package doc's anchor): a create names it (`Creation.Seat`, `ErrSeatRequired`), every invitation names it and its redemption binds it, an identity change MOVES it and never clears it, `seat: ""` on a person is `ErrSeatRequired`, decided in the record's own snapshot from the row's kind, before the no-op arm, and only moving or removing them frees it; a suspended or retired person keeps theirs, and a SERVICE ACCOUNT's seat stays optional. EVERY BINDING, of either kind, is checked as a HUMAN seat of the running org (`Writer.humanSeat`), a create and an edit used to ask only that the chart held the handle, so a person bound to an AGENT's seat was refused `403 seat_unavailable` on every request they made, and a node running no company has none to offer (`ErrNoCompany`, from the seam's `session.ErrNoCompany`, rather than the `ErrUnavailable` no wait would clear). The WRITER's decides enforce it and the APPLIER never does: an applier writes whatever a record says, because refusing a record there stops the log on every data node. AN ADMINISTRATOR'S CREATE IS DERIVED (`CreatedPersonID`, from its operation key) AND NEVER REWRITES: one reaching an existing person is a reused key (`ErrOperationReused`), and its own retry is answered from the ledger; a redemption's person is minted per attempt, and what keeps the link single-use is the link its record spends. A CREATED PERSON IS ACTIVE AT ONCE AND HANDED A FIRST PASSWORD LINK in the create's own record, a `reset` credential whose id and secret are DERIVED from the person under the company's key (`Blinder.PasswordLinkID`, `Blinder.PasswordLinkSecret`), a week long (`credential.EnrolmentLinkLifetime`), spent through the ordinary reset path, because the retry an unknown answer asks for is answered from the ledger without its decide running, so only a derivation hands it the link its first attempt issued: it reads the link back by that id (`firstLinkAs`) and answers it with its stored expiry while it opens, `LinkClosed` once it is spent, revoked or aged out, and unavailable on a node that has not applied it. A service account gets none, it proves itself with a token. And THE RECORD IS FORMED INSIDE THE DECIDE, the counters its credential is stamped with included (`stampIssued` over `countersOf`): formed before it, as every enrolment used to be, a carried credential was stamped at zero, and the first company-wide invalidation, the last step of every restore, ended every first link any create would issue from then on. The SCOPE IS FLAT AND BUCKETED (64, by FNV-1a over the id nothing renames, this domain's own and unrelated to `search`'s) and that is forced rather than chosen: a directory record's subject names nobody and the person it is about is inside a payload the deferring node could not decode, so a per-person path would be computable by every data node except the one that needs it, and a person's id in a deferral row is personal data in the one place this estate most carefully keeps clear of it. THE ROOT SCOPE IS REFUSED to every kind but the FOUR that can never be deferred, because a deferral at the root freezes every suspension, revocation and login at once, three of the four install a gate and the fourth is the framework's own barrier. TWO COUNTERS END SESSIONS and they are deliberately not one word: a person's REVOCATION EPOCH ends every session THEY hold, and the fleet-wide SESSION GENERATION (`iam.invalidation`, one subject for the whole company) ends everybody's, which is what a restore needs, because a revocation performed after an artefact was taken is rolled back with it and the generation is the only number that can be pushed forward without knowing who was affected. It is also the FIRST strict domain whose ReadinessInput is FALSE: no turn reads it, a seat's authority is the chart's, so taking a node's tracker or knowledge-base copy out of service because a human cannot sign in would be an outage caused by the wrong subsystem, what a stall gates is the REQUEST path, per request, with a 503 and never a 401. NAME AND EMAIL ARE SEALED BY THE WRITER before publication, under the FLEET KEYRING every node already holds, one envelope format and one rotation story rather than a second key hierarchy, which is what keeps the identity claim a byte comparison, and so is a SECOND FACTOR'S SEED, the one credential kept as a secret rather than a verifier, sealed by the enrolment and bound to the credential it was enrolled as (`Sealer.SealCredential`), because in the clear it was a second factor every data node, snapshot and backup held; every value carries ASSOCIATED DATA naming whose it is and which of their values (`AADFor`, `AADForCredential`, `AADForInvitation`), because under one keyring a value pasted into another row would otherwise open there; a LOGIN is deliberately in the clear, because blinding a value the dashboard prints on every row costs an operator their own audit trail for nothing. The APPLIER never decrypts: every sealed value is written through as bytes and a removal erases by SHAPE without opening one, because an applier that opened a value would write rows that depended on which keyring keys its node holds, two nodes a rotation has not yet reached would write different rows from one record. A REMOVAL ERASES: its apply deletes the person's rows and CLEARS every sealed value of theirs that rows not their own still hold, an invitation addressed to them, the trail rows whose records carried their name, address or seed (`apply_erase.go`), found by the address the removal releases, the only one a person ever holds, with the record declaring that address's bucket beside the person's, as a seat bind declares its leavers' buckets, so a node that retained a record in one of them holds the removal behind it, recognising a sealed value by its shape, since every envelope here is somebody's, so afterwards no row on any node holds a value of theirs that opens; the log's copy waits for the trim and a backup for its own deletion. A ROTATION moves a person's values the only way anything here changes, a RECORD per person holding one under a key other than the active one (`Writer.Reseal`, counted by `Reader.SealedKeys`), because a node that re-sealed its own rows would hold different bytes from its peers and a replay from the floor would write the old ciphertext back; an outstanding invitation's address is counted and waited out rather than moved. The blind-key mint guard proves its "no blind here" only on rows that have APPLIED the whole log (`CoversLog`), and APPLIED is not CONSUMED: the applier's checkpoint moves past a record it RETAINS (a newer build's, one signed under a keyring key the node was not restarted with), and a node holding one reads that record's rows as rows nobody wrote. It runs on EVERY NODE THAT HOLDS THE ESTATE, whatever its other roles and company or none, so every node that validates a session reads its own identity rows; a node WITHOUT `data`, a seats-only satellite, which routes deliveries by who holds each seat, applies none of it and asks a data node instead, through the ESTATE ROUTER's identity reads (a login's binding row for `iam.Holders`, `SeatHolders` for the party registry), floored on the identity log so a data node behind answers unknown rather than an old binding. THE ADMINISTRATIVE PERMISSION IS `admin`, checked at the RECORD rather than only at a route because a record can be published by a CLI, a duty, a migration and a test, none of which passes through one, once two grants, one asked of the record and another held by the NODE's own writer, so every invitation redemption was refused on a real deployment while the surface's own suite, built on a stub writer, stayed green; the node's writer now holds `[admin]` itself, and authors as the ENGINE (`NodeKind`, `iam.KindEngine`) under the node's id, never a machine, whose rows the trail draws as a credential's login and whose grammar no node id passes, so the sweeps, re-seals, re-hashes and company-wide invalidations it writes for itself read as the system, as the tracker's node writer's do. AND NEVER THROUGH A MACHINE TOKEN: `mayAdminister` refuses every administrative record, a create, a redemption, an identity change, a status change, a removal, an invitation and its cancellation, an edit, ending everybody's sessions, whose `OperatorID` is of the `pat:` class with `ErrTokenRefused` (which wraps `ErrRefused`) BEFORE it reads or publishes, whatever the token carries, because a token MAY carry admin and deciding who may do anything needs a person at a keyboard; a Tier A token's `token:<id>` is no machine token and is admitted, and the eviction and sweep gestures (`mayOperate`) take admin with no such lock. NOR ANY SIGN-IN CEREMONY THROUGH THE DASHBOARD ASSISTANT: an `OperatorID` of the `assist:` class is refused an enrolment (a first password link or a redemption), an invitation and ending every session, and a token mint, while every other administrative record it carries is one the administrator approved (ADR-0052), with `ErrAssistRefused` (which wraps `ErrRefused`), checked first beside the token, because an administrator's assistant is composed holding admin and the authority table guards only the routes. EVERY REFUSAL THE WRITER MAKES IS AN `iamdomain.Refusal`, still `ErrRefused` to `errors.Is` and in the same words: its `Grants` are the permissions any one of which would lift it, EMPTY where the record's own rule refused it whatever the party holds, a person's token minted by anybody but that person, a permission the owner lacks, an owner who may not act, a name this build does not know, every invitation, session and password rule, built only through `needs` and `forbidden`, which an AST gate holds, with `ErrTokenRefused` and `ErrAssistRefused` riding inside one naming none (the mint's token arm included), and it crosses the estate wire whole (`estate`'s typed kinds), because one sentinel left a surface answering both kinds as a missing permission. A MACHINE TOKEN IS MINTED ON ITS OWNER'S SUBJECT, reading the owner in the snapshot the credential is formed in (`MintToken`), because what it may carry is a fact about the owner NOW, a subset of `Expand` of their permissions, admin included, defaulting to everything they hold that the minting party may confer, on a node that refuses admin, their apps, the most they could have asked for there, where defaulting to their whole set was refused for the admin nobody there can confer, and nothing the minting party does not hold, and a caller handing in a finished credential would be stating a read from another transaction. A PERSON'S TOKEN IS MINTED BY THAT PERSON ALONE, decided in the same snapshot on the minting party's ID, the WRITER's own (`Writer.Principal`, derived with the rest of the party from the principal `Writer.As` is handed whole), never a field of the mint a caller fills in and never a login, which a rename moves between people, and never through a machine token, which would renew itself for ever with nobody present: whoever mints a token is shown its value and it acts as its owner, so an administrator minting on a person's account could hold a credential acting as anybody, admin now mints for SERVICE ACCOUNTS only, the one kind with nobody behind it to mint for itself; `Reader.MachineToken` answers the row, the owner, their epoch and the fleet generation in ONE snapshot for the guard. AN ENROLMENT AND AN INVITATION CONFER ONLY WHAT THEIR WRITER HOLDS, exactly as an edit does, an enrolment is a permission change from nothing, and it was held to nothing, so an administrator of people alone could create a colleague holding every permission, judged through `iam.Expand` (an unknown name already on a row is kept, one added is refused), and the one enrolment the NODE's own writer performs, a REDEMPTION, NAMES its authority instead, checked in the enrolment record's own snapshot: the INVITATION (nothing outside `Expand` of what it carried, no address or seat it did not carry, and no link already spent or aged out). There is NO EXEMPTION FOR THE FIRST PERSON: a company with nobody in it still holds the Tier A token every serving node requires, and that token's invitation is held to its grants exactly as an administrator's is, and names a human seat exactly as theirs does, so a company's first person waits for a revision that declares one. AN ENROLMENT NEEDS TO BE FINDABLE rather than addressed: a PERSON needs an email (it is the interactive login key) and a MACHINE needs a login (it has no login page, so requiring an address would mean inventing one), the flat address requirement meant a company could not declare a service account at all. A login is never cleared, only changed, a person's seat neither: an identity change stating none is refused, because a principal with no login is recorded as nobody and a Tier A token's machine row is how it is bound to a seat. And EVERY PRINCIPAL NEEDS A LOGIN, a person too although their address finds them: it is the name they sign in with, the name `iam.OwnerOf` resolves when a lead names a report, the `by <login>` in every trail reason, and what `Principal.Validate` requires, a person enrolled by address alone was written as `anonymous` beside every change and failed `Principal.Validate` on every request, so an invitation's view PROPOSES one from the address (`iam.LoginFromAddress`) for the person to keep or change, and an administrator's create that names none takes that same proposal at `/iam` and answers the login it took, never one typed by nobody and shown to nobody. The retention SWEEP'S PUBLISHER is here beside its applier (`sweeppublish.go`): the only clock read on the whole retention path, turned into POSITIONS once per tick, and a bucket is swept only when something is a day past its horizon, which bounds it at 64 records a day with no memory across a lease move. A sweep collects what lapsed AND what was spent, redeemed invitations and revoked or expired credentials, the last from the person's DOCUMENT as well as the rows, since the next credential change would republish them, and every record NAMES THE CREDENTIAL its party acted through (`operator_id`), a removal and an invalidation included, and none where the node's own writer acted. Every record is written at version 1, the field table being empty: a record above a peer's decode ceiling is deferred there, so the ceiling is what a build READS and never what it writes. Whether a record this node RETAINED is about somebody is asked of the WHOLE deferral index for their bucket in the rows' own snapshot (`deferredFor`), because the runner's earliest deferral carried no scope and was about one bucket, so the session table's and a token binding's "this node cannot vouch" answer never fired. AN INVITATION is a directory record held against every person and every open invitation to its ADDRESS AND ITS SEAT, since its own id would never contend and two administrators would each get a link that creates the same person, or two people on one seat; that id is its issue's operation key blinded under the company's key (`Blinder.InvitationID`), so a retry finds and answers its own invitation, the key on other terms is `ErrOperationReused`, and nobody holding a key can compute a link; the decide reads `iam_invites` as well as `iam_people`, because an address or a seat an open invitation holds is held, an administrator's create of it, a bind to it and another invitation naming it are refused naming the invitation (`heldByInvitation`). THE LINK IS THE ID AND A SECRET, derived from the id under the same key (`Blinder.InvitationSecret`) so a retry hands back the same link, of which the invitation keeps only the SHA-256 (`Invitation.Verifier`) and the redemption's record checks it again in its own snapshot (`Redemption.InvitationSecret`): the id alone was the credential, and it is the row's key in every snapshot, backup and access log, an invitation with no verifier admits nobody. An invitation nobody redeemed is CANCELLED on the directory too (`CancelInvitation`, `OpCancel`), and its apply DELETES the row rather than marking it, because the row is what holds the address and the seat and an id the estate does not hold is exactly what a dead link must look like, with no tombstone, since the log applies the issue first and a redelivery below the checkpoint is never applied; a redeemed one is refused (`InvitationRedeemed`) because it created somebody. A NEW PASSWORD IS ONE RECORD (`SetPassword`, `OpPassword`): the person's document with the password replaced and every live RESET LINK revoked, and their revocation epoch moved, on their own subject, as two records a change that landed its password and not its epoch left every session a thief held working. The writer requires no grant for it: the surface that calls it is the authority (the person's current password, or a reset link) and states it as `PasswordSet.Check`, judged in the record's own snapshot. A RESET LINK is a `reset` credential an administrator puts on a person through an ordinary person write, the SHA-256 of its secret, a day long, the one before it revoked, and revoked by any edit that ADDS a permission to its person (judged over `iam.Expand` on both sides, so storing `[admin]` as `[admin, worklet, doclet, artifacts, chatlet]` adds nothing; `RevokeResetLinks`, in `UpdatePerson`'s own decide), because the link was judged against its issuer at the issue and a grant gained since was judged against nobody holding it, and `Reader.ResetByID` answers it with its person's login and stage in one read, and whether it sets their FIRST password (`ResetRow.First`), DERIVED from their holding no password credential rather than stored on the link, so a reset issued to somebody whose first link lapsed unspent reads as the first password it sets. EVERY WRITE ANSWERS ITS WHOLE `statelog.Result` and never a bare position, because a position cannot tell `applied` and `pending` from `unknown`: a zero one beside a nil error read as success, so a second factor whose spend may not have landed opened a session. THE STAGE HAS ONE WRITER and every reader takes it from the row's COLUMN: the status apply moves the column and the document together, and a read-modify-write decide forms the next document from the column, because the document's copy went stale on the first suspension, sessions kept validating, and the next grant edit re-published `active` and reinstated the person. The applier's post-commit hook says what a batch MOVED (`Moved`): whether a seat's standing moved (a bind or a move, a service account's unbind, a stage, a removal), and WHOSE CREDENTIALS, the people whose rows it moved with the LOGIN each holds (a Tier A token is bound through the row under its own `token:<id>`, whose id it never learns), the sessions it ended, and only where no list can say whose, everyone: the fleet generation, and a record the node RETAINED rather than applied, which the framework says through `statelog.RetentionHook` because a retained record never reaches Apply and its person is inside a payload the node cannot read. An identity change names its person with the login they held BEFORE it as well as the one they hold after, because a record naming nobody ended every socket on the node for an ordinary unbind, an open dashboard socket is decided at its handshake and ended by the record that ended its credential, and a move naming everyone closes them all; `SeatHolders` answers who holds each seat at what stage in one snapshot, a removal read off its tombstone, which speaks for the BINDING it released and no other: the first bind of the seat after the removal stamps `seat_rebound_by`, so a successor's later move off the seat (or a service account's unbind) hands the seat to the chart rather than back to the leaver, which is what lets a suspension withdraw a seat's contact identities with no configuration change. A SEAT IS BOUND BY ITS HANDLE, which is immutable (ADR-0013): `seat_id`, a person's, and an invitation's, a removal's tombstone and the stamp on it name it; every binding checks the handle against the running org the writer is handed (`WriterDeps.Seats`), ADVISORY, since a revision applied elsewhere first is one this node has not seen, and the residue is the dangling binding `iam check` reports; the other end is advisory too, a `/config` write that takes a human seat away (a removal or a kind flip) refused `409 seat_held` naming whoever holds it at any stage short of removal, or the open invitation that holds it (by id and expiry, never its address), after one read of the directory at the node's clock (`Reader.SeatClaims`, through `configapi.Holders`), which an offline import never makes, and every reader finds the seat by that handle. `SeatClaims` is the ONE read of who and what holds the human seats, every binding with its holder's KIND, and every open invitation's seat, in one snapshot, and `ClaimsBySeat` folds it: two rows on one seat are unknown (`ErrUnavailable`), never a pick, while two open invitations on one seat, which only a build before invitations held their seats leaves, keep the newest. EVERY STATEMENT OVER THE PEOPLE, THE INVITATIONS AND THE TRAIL IS NAMED and planned whole by the package's own gate (`TestTheDirectoryStatementsArePlanned`, held complete by `storetest.StatementLiterals` in the tracker's and the pages' idiom), and a value's statement is picked from a table of whole statements rather than formed from a column name — internal/store's schema case planned COPIES of the lookups, two had drifted from what any node ran, and the first plan of the real set found an index no statement reached, `/health`'s existence question and every page of the trail reading the heap, and an erasure seeking the wrong index (replicated 0069) |
| `iam/credential` | WHAT SOMEBODY PROVES THEMSELVES WITH: argon2id passwords, RFC 6238 second factors, recovery codes, machine tokens, one-time password reset links (`reset.go`, `ResetLinkLifetime`, a day long because one travels out of band to somebody locked out today) — and the same credential as the FIRST PASSWORD LINK an administrator's create hands the person it creates, which lives `EnrolmentLinkLifetime`, A WEEK, beside it: the invitation's lifetime, since both bring in somebody who does not work here yet and may not read the link before their first day, and ONE constant for both, here, where it was the invitation surface's own (`iamapi.InviteWindow`, gone) — and the throttle that keeps a sign-in endpoint from being a ROSTER. Everything stored is a VERIFIER and never a secret, because the identity estate is replicated, snapshotted, backed up and donated to joining peers. THE RULE IS NOT "hash secrets with argon2id", it is SPEND COST WHERE AN ATTACKER HAS A SHORTCUT — a password came from a space small enough to grind, so it gets 64 MiB and t=3; a recovery code, a machine token and a reset link's secret are values this engine minted — `crypto/rand`, or for a first password link a MAC under the company's key, so a create's retry hands back the same link — so there is no dictionary and SHA-256 is the whole of it, and a token is presented on every request a pipeline makes. `p=1` is the parameter most often set wrong: parallelism makes ONE verification finish sooner and does nothing to an attacker already running every core they own, while costing the ability to serve concurrent sign-ins at all. `VerifyCap` is DERIVED as max(1, NumCPU/p) because each verification holds 64 MiB — uncapped, an unauthenticated caller turns the endpoint into an out-of-memory kill — and it bounds EVERY derivation through one path, a verification at the stored verifier's own cost as much as a hash: `Verify` used to call argon2 directly, so the cap bounded setting a password and nothing a stranger could send. THE CAP IS A PLAIN SEMAPHORE, first come first served, that every `Hash`, `Verify` and `Decoy` waits in on the REQUEST'S CONTEXT, so a waiter whose request went away leaves and nothing is derived for nobody — it was one queue with no bound and no context, so derivations ran for clients that had hung up; and it was then per-source turns, one slot per address served in turn, which bought a timing equalisation nothing asks for any more at the price of a scheduler of its own. `Rehash`, the one derivation nobody waits on, takes a slot only if one is free NOW and never one a freed slot hands to a waiter. The PHC string is what makes a cost raise possible: the plaintext is not stored, so the only instant a stronger digest can be computed is the one where somebody presents their password — and STALE MEANS WEAKER (below this build's cost in memory, passes or digest length and above it in none), never merely different, because a cost moves only with a build and so during a rolling upgrade, where a node on the older build read every verifier the newer one wrote as stale and rewrote it DOWN, flapping each person's verifier for the whole rollout. TWELVE CHARACTERS AND NO COMPOSITION RULES, with a blocklist of HUNDREDS rather than a published ten-thousand corpus — those are ranked by frequency and cluster at six to ten characters, so almost none is reachable behind the floor, and what is reachable is a few families caught by FOLDING a candidate (`password1234`, `P@ssw0rd1234` and `p4$$w0rd1234` are one entry). THE ENUMERATION ORACLE is what the file is shaped by and it takes two mechanisms: a throttle keyed on the subject AS TYPED from its source, decided before anything is looked up (keyed on what the subject RESOLVED to, its delay IS the roster), and a DECOY for a subject that does not exist that VERIFIES what was presented against ONE DUMMY VERIFIER at this build's cost — drawn, never derived, once per hasher and held as bytes — so a miss is a hit's work: one derivation at the same cost under the same cap and the same constant-time compare. It does NOT go back through `Verify`, which answers an unreadable verifier with a decoy: it did, and a hasher whose dummy did not decode recursed until the stack overflowed and took the process with it — and `NewHasher` panics on a cost argon2 cannot derive at (zero passes, lanes or digest), which `decode` refuses in a stored verifier for the same reason, since argon2 panics there rather than refusing. THE RESIDUAL IS STATED, NOT PADDED: the directory read before the derivation, and a real verifier written at another cost than this build's. Every refusal was once padded to one deadline measured from admission, and holding it under load took a turn per address at the cap and a decoy whose hold was DRAWN from the node's last sixteen derivations — a scheduler and a sampler to hide one indexed read. THE THROTTLE IS A CURVE AND NEVER A LOCKOUT: each failure doubles its key's next wait from 1 s to 30 s, a wait up to 5 s is served inside the request and a longer one is `429` with a `Retry-After`, because a refusal after N is a lockout anybody who can type a login can cause. ONE KEY for a password, the (typed subject, source) PAIR (IPv6 by its /64) with no allowance, and a success clears ITS OWN pair and NOTHING ELSE: it flushed the whole source, so anybody holding an account signed in as themselves between guesses at somebody else's and wiped every one. NO ADDRESS IS EVER REFUSED ON ITS OWN: there was a SOURCE curve too, ten free and then the same wait, and a refusal decided on an address is one anybody sharing it holds shut for everybody else — one stranger's failure every twenty-five seconds, at any name, kept a whole office or everybody behind an untrusted proxy at 429, right passwords never compared, and since a pending attempt counted against it, fourteen honest sign-ins in flight met the same 429. One password across many names from one address therefore meets no curve — the derivation every name costs under the verify cap, the floor and the blocklist bound it and the per-minute failure count shows it — and an attempt that NAMES NOBODY (an invitation or password link, whose secret is 256 bits nobody outside the engine can compute — a MAC under the company's key, or a `crypto/rand` draw) is ADMITTED UNCOUNTED, a nil `Ticket`, since there is nothing a curve would slow. A SECOND FACTOR ALSO CLIMBS THE PERSON'S OWN CURVE (`AdmitSecondFactor`), keyed on the RESOLVED person under a digest domain of its own, on each node, lifted by the code that completes the sign-in: on the pair alone somebody holding the password spread their guesses at six digits over every address they owned, a /48 finding the code in about an hour — and keying on the resolved person is safe only here, past the password, where it tells nobody who exists; `Ticket.Fail` reports the TRANSITION into the ceiling — the failure that takes the key there, not every failure while it is there — which the sign-in surface announces as `iam_second_factor_throttled`, since it means the password is known, and the curve's own state is the dedupe: a run held at the ceiling announces once, and a curve that has aged back down announces its next climb. An admitted attempt counts as a failure against its pair until its `Ticket` resolves (once: fail, succeed or release), so a burst at one account is served along the curve rather than at once — counted APART from the bounded memory of failures, because filed there at admission a burst of fresh names nobody verified, abandoned before the cap, evicted a climbing pair for the price of the requests alone, where forgetting one is meant to cost `MaxPairs` failures; EACH NODE KEEPS ITS OWN CURVE, and the residual is DECIDED rather than overlooked: the pairs' failures are a bounded LRU (`MaxPairs`) timed on this process's monotonic clock under a digest key it generated and never wrote, so a run a load balancer spreads over N nodes meets N curves and is admitted up to N times as often — bounded instead by what bounds it on one node, thirty seconds per attempt at the ceiling, an argon2id verification under the verify cap, the twelve-character floor, the blocklist and, past the password, the second factor. Sharing the curve through the coordination store was a round trip on the sign-in path and a subsystem of its own, bought for a factor of N on a rate the curve already cuts sixty-fold at its first step, and for nothing at all on the single node most deployments are. TOTP is hand-written over `crypto/hmac`, `crypto/sha1` and `encoding/base32` and certified against the RFC's own vectors, because forty lines of arithmetic against a module with its own idea of tolerable drift is the dependency rule working; the LAST ACCEPTED STEP is stored, or the drift tolerance is a ninety-second window in which one shoulder-surfed code works repeatedly. Every secret comparison is constant-time and every candidate loop runs to the end, asserted BY AST because no behavioural test can see the difference. A MACHINE TOKEN is `cwl_pat_<id>_<position>_<secret>` and whether one is good is a TABLE over one snapshot's values (`CheckToken`), THREE-VALUED on the position it carries in the clear: a node below the mint answers UNKNOWN (503) and one that covers it and holds no row answers REFUSED (401) — the two are otherwise one observation. The verifier covers the prefix, the id and the secret and deliberately NOT the position, which is a hint about when to look and can only ever buy a forger a 503. What a valid token carries is its mint's grants cut, PER REQUEST, to what its owner STILL holds, so a demotion reaches every token they made; and it is minted at its owner's revocation epoch AND the company's session generation and refused once either moves, because a restore brings a revoked token back exactly as it brings a revoked session back. A token held by its NAME rather than its secret (a view ticket minted through it) is decided by the same table less the two facts only a bearer has (`CheckHeld`): no secret to verify, and an absent row is unknown rather than refused, since no mint position says whether this node has applied it |
| `iam/session` | THE SIGNED COOKIE a browser holds, and the two THREE-VALUED TABLES that resolve it to a person and a seat. The seat table's SEATLESS arm serves a row bound to no seat under its own login, and who lands on it is a SERVICE ACCOUNT or a Tier A token's unbound machine row — never a PERSON, who holds a human seat for as long as they are here (ADR-0032), which the directory's writer enforces since the table carries no kind; every binding the directory writes names a human seat, so the GONE arm is a revision that took one away. The seam's `Chart` answers a node running no company `ErrNoCompany`, EXPORTED HERE so the directory's writer can name its remedy, while `ResolveSeat` reads it as the unknown arm like any other error, a 503, since another node can serve the request. SIGNATURE-STATELESS AND ROW-STATEFUL, because neither half works alone: a stateless cookie cannot be revoked before it expires, so off-boarding would be impossible, and an opaque id into a table costs a read for every forged cookie and has nothing to say until the row it names has been applied on the node the request reached. THERE IS NO VALIDATION CACHE ANYWHERE, stated in the package doc — the lookup is a local read of a replicated row and a map lookup on the running org, so a cache would be a second idea of who is signed in, with its own staleness, in front of a read that is already local. The rule the whole package is shaped by is 503 AND NEVER 401 ON A NODE THAT IS BEHIND: a browser reads 401 as "sign in again" and discards the cookie, so one stalled applier answering 401 logs everybody on that node out and sends them all back to the sign-in form at once — and the grace exists only for the arm a lagging node can honestly serve (reads of a session it has not yet seen), ending at `statelog.StallGrace`, so a node serving stale identity is by definition one already alarmed. What makes those three answers REACHABLE is the START POSITION in the bearer: an absent row on a node that covers it is a session that ended, and on a node below it is one that has not arrived, and the two are otherwise one observation with two opposite answers. ONE COOKIE NAME AUTHENTICATES — `__Host-crewlet_session` on https, the bare name on plain http, read as issued (`Presented`) — because the bare name is one a sibling host can plant, and read too it signed a visitor who held no session in as whoever's session the sibling planted; a sign-out still reaches both (`Held`), so a browser holding the name an http deployment issued before it moved to https has that session closed. THE KEYRING IS REQUIRED — a per-process random key is REFUSED by name, here and in `runtoken` alike, because every ingress node would derive a different key and a browser would be signed in on whichever node its request reached — and the key TAG is `runtoken`'s own, so a rotation is add, restart, flip, restart, drop, and dropping a key early is what ends live sessions. A RE-ISSUE WRITES NOTHING — the idle deadline it moves is inside the signed bearer — and THERE IS NO ROTATION INDEX, because a cookie carries nothing that tells its owner's copy from a replayed one: the v2 bearer's index, the session's age in windows, served a LAGGING index as the idle session it is indistinguishable from, and its one arm that acted — an index AHEAD of the clock past a two-minute overlap — was inside the signed payload and so written only by a node holding the keyring, from its own clock, which made it a detector of clock skew that bumped the person's revocation epoch and signed them out of every session and machine token they held. So the bearer is v3 with no index and no `session.rotate_after`, and what bounds a captured cookie is the idle and absolute deadlines and the two counters. A bearer carries a SCOPE only when its session may do less than everything (`enrol`, one that may only enrol a second factor) — signed, re-issued with it, and read with the row's copy through `Validation.EnrolmentOnly` — and a whole session's bearer is byte for byte the nine fields every build reads, so a rolling upgrade keeps every ordinary session while a build that knows no scope refuses a scoped one as malformed rather than serving it whole. A session exchanged from a Tier A token carries NOTHING OF THE TOKEN: its person is the token's login, which the guard's token table answers by the entry's ID — a gone entry is a subject that may not act, on a node that has not applied the session's row too — so a new value under the same id leaves the sessions the old one opened working until their hour ends, and an operator who must cut them off at once rotates by giving the token a new id. A MAC over the value once rode in the cookie to end them on a rotation as well, and went: a second credential check on every request, a key derivation and an ending of its own, to shorten an hour an id change already shortens to nothing. A WRITE on a node below the bearer's start position WAITS for it rather than answering 503 — at most `auth.SessionCatchUp`, the publisher's own resolve budget, over the identity reader's `AwaitApplied` — because a sign-in answers before any node applies the session it opened (`NoWait`), so the request that reads the bearer next owes its position the wait the sign-in skipped: refused outright, every client that signs in and then acts met a 503 on every node, and `crewlet iam token -login` failed on every real run. A DEADLINE IS DECIDED BEFORE ANY ROW IS READ, so an expired cookie is refused even by a node that cannot read its estate — and nothing announces it: an expiry nobody authored is no row, where announcing it once per session took a remembered set the size of every session a node had noticed and a second read of the rows to tell it from an ending a record had already announced. An OPEN CONNECTION's bearer is decided with its idle deadline set aside (`Signer.ValidateOpen`), because a socket can never be re-issued a cookie and an open live view is activity — its rows and its absolute deadline still end it, and nothing is re-issued. `session.Idle` is a CONSTANT and not config, because it rides in every bearer and two nodes disagreeing about it would hand one browser two expiries. A session held by its NAME (a view ticket's lineage, on the artifact origin where no cookie travels) lands on the same rows through `StandingByName`, the session row's own epoch standing in for the bearer's; the generation and the absolute deadline are the caller's to have judged first |
| `iam/authevents` | WHERE THIS NODE'S AUTHENTICATION FACTS BECOME EVENTS — the one trail the sign-in surface, the request guard, the identity directory, the identity writer and every state-log applier hand what they saw to, built ONCE PER PROCESS by the engine because its per-minute count is the node's: a failed sign-in and a refused bearer are counted together only because both reach the same trail. TWO DOORS and they are the whole design: a fact a VERIFIED credential or the engine authored is published as it happens (`Emit`), while a FAILED ATTEMPT publishes NOTHING — it bumps `crewlet.auth.attempts.failed` (by method and outcome) and one on its SOURCE's count for the minute — the throttle's own unit (`credential.SourceKey`: an address, an IPv6 client's /64), folded by the trail rather than by any surface, because counted per raw address one host cycling addresses inside its /64 took every named place in a minute's first milliseconds and pushed every other caller into the overflow — and the engine's own loop publishes ONE `iam_login_failures` per node once the minute has CLOSED, carrying each source's count, because internal/events admits no type whose rate an unauthenticated caller authors and a row per attempt was 6.9 million a day from one host. A FAILURE CARRIES NOTHING ABOUT WHO — `Failure` is a source, a method and whether the throttle turned it away, and a test holds the shapes of it and of the row: the presented value is where a password typed into the login box lands, an unsalted hash of it reverses against the company's own roster in one pass, and the tally this replaced kept keyed digests to count distinct names and the ids of the people failures resolved to, which made a row a list of who was being guessed at. The one size an attacker would otherwise choose is `MaxSourcesPerMinute` (64, with its reason), past which every further source's failures fold into one overflow count, so the row grows with nothing an attacker's address pool decides. NO FACT IS COALESCED PER WINDOW: there was a third door, a once-per-window dedupe keeping a bounded set per class of fact, and each fact went the way that needs no remembered set — a Tier A token's use is on every record it writes, under its name; its overreach is a WARN log line in the guard; a session past its own deadline is an expiry nobody authored, and no row; and a person's second factor reaching its ceiling is announced by the failure the curve itself reports as taking it there. Publishes are DETACHED from the request and bounded, because the gesture already happened and a client hanging up must not erase its row; the loop's final flush on stop is what keeps the minute a restart interrupted |
| `usage` | What each node's seats, schedules and people did each company day, replicated — `statelog`'s FIFTH domain and its second COMPACTED one (ADR-0020). Spend, turn and read history used to be a query over `crewlet_events`, the NODE estate's audit log, so every answer described whichever node served it, a ninety-day chart was drawn over the thirty days that log keeps, and a node that left took its share of the company's history with it. THE NODE IS PART OF THE SUBJECT: each data node derives only its own day from its own event log (`store.DB.UsageForDay`) and publishes one record per (node, day, seat), per (node, day, schedule) and per (node, day, person) — a PERSON being a human seat the auxiliary model spent for (a question answered on the operator surface, a background pass on a unit a person leads), filed under that seat's HANDLE as the principal's own seat names it (`iam.RecordOwner`) and never an identity-directory person id, a principal bound to no seat charging the company alone — WHOLE, every time it moves — so every object has exactly one writer, nothing arbitrates, nothing merges at write time, and a reader sums across nodes. A seat is its AGENT ID and a schedule its scope's identity, never a name, because a name is prose two seats may share; a seat's handle rides along only as a label for a seat the company no longer holds. An apply REPLACES under a monotone position guard, which is what makes a republished day harmless and a replayed older one a no-op. NOT A DUTY: every DATA node runs one publisher, for its own days, armed in the engine's CORE whatever its company keeps — a node without `data` keeps no event log, so its events reach a data node through CUSTODY and its spend is counted in that node's day — re-deriving today and yesterday every 15 s when a cheap fingerprint moves, because a turn that ended a second before midnight is written a moment after it. A DAY IS THE ROWS ITS NODE KEEPS, never a custody batch it has written and not settled (`store`'s kept-rows predicate, a seek of the in-flight identities' key from every row counted, on every usage count), because until the claim settles two data nodes can hold one batch and a reader summing the fleet would bill it twice. A ROW CHANGES ONLY THE DAY IT FALLS ON: a turn's figures — its reviews, send-backs and every segment's duration — are read off the ONE record that ends it, which states the whole turn (`types.TurnTally`, counted as the turn runs and carried across a park), because a stateless node's records reach the data nodes a custody batch at a time and a resumed turn leaves its first segment behind, so summed per node from what each held they were short on one node and counted on none — and a turn whose end states NO tally (ended by a build before it, or resumed from a park such a build made, whose earlier segments nobody measured) is read off its records by its id as it always was, never as zeros, which would publish every pre-upgrade turn as zero-length and unreviewed over the history the old build wrote. So a batch settled as KEPT moves the fingerprint of today or yesterday, and an older day through the mark the settle leaves in its own transaction (`usage_rederive`, a row per UTC day, cleared up to the newest seq the publisher read), and the publisher derives every such day again — NEVER one that starts before `store.EventHistory`, the event log's read floor, whose rows may be swept and whose short derivation would replace the whole day on every data node with less, and a day older than the dispatch ledger's retention without its schedules. A day derived again keeps the instants it was FIRST CUT on (`usage_day_windows`), so a moved company clock never moves rows between days already published — a day cut for the first time abuts its adjacent labels' cuts and is clamped by any farther one it would reach into, so the day the clock moves on is longer or shorter instead, and an instant on a label whose old cut misses it belongs to the label beside it — and NO DAY, today and yesterday included, IS EVER PUBLISHED BELOW WHAT THE FLEET HOLDS: inside the floor a day only grows, so a smaller derivation proves the node's own log lost rows (a store restored beside a later usage log, one started empty, a parked turn's earliest records swept), and what goes out is the derivation JOINED with the fleet's record (`usage.Join`), every figure at the larger of the two — the derivation itself on an ordinary day, and on a short one the fleet's figure where it is larger while every other keeps moving, where holding the short derivation back whole froze the day — logged once (`usage_rederive_short`), and no day is derived until the node's usage applier has replayed the log to its end, so what it is joined with is the fleet's rows rather than a restore's. What the publisher itself still owes — the day before the newest it fingerprinted, or an older one a tick could not settle — is a WATERMARK in the node's store (`usage_watermark`), which the next start derives every day from again, so the tail of the day a node stopped in reaches the fleet when it comes back within the read floor; an ORDERLY stop also flushes today and yesterday once more, after every writer of a usage row has stopped (the custody keeper leaving its group among them), on a budget of the state log's own resolve budget, so a node stopped and never started again leaves its last day whole — a crash it never comes back from loses up to a tick. An unresolved publish is sent again rather than counted — while a refusal no retry changes (a record too large, a full log) is logged ONCE per object and skipped until that object next changes, never re-sent every tick. Turn durations are a mergeable 2^(1/6) HISTOGRAM (±6% quantiles) rather than a list or a stored percentile, because a week is seven days merged and a fleet three nodes merged. The 181-day HORIZON is the APPLIER's — a record for day D deletes rows older than D − 181 in its own transaction, a pure function of the record — because a sweep would be a second writer of the replicated estate. A person's record is written at the base version and published at once, like every other record of the domain |
| `chatlet` | Chatlet, the engine's own CHAT, and `statelog`'s NINTH domain — channels, unit rooms, direct conversations, threads, messages, edits, tombstoned deletes, reactions, retention, erase and the prune — on `CREWLET_CHAT_LOG`. It is the domain with TWO DISCIPLINES ON ONE STREAM, and that is the whole of what makes it different: a room's own state (topic, purpose, membership, archive, retention) arbitrates on the room's subject as the other domains do, while a MESSAGE is `PatternSequenced` on the room's MESSAGE subject — one subject per room rather than per message — because two people talking in one room are not racing for anything, so an expectation there would serialise the hottest subject in the company behind itself and answer `ErrConflict` on a message somebody typed. SEQUENCED AND NOT ADDITIVE, because a message does not commute with what lands just ahead of it: the APPLIER decides each message record at its position and writes a VERDICT for every one (`chat_verdicts`, replicated migration 0068 — `applied`, or `archived`, `not_member`, `gone`, `deleted`, `reactions_full`, or `repeated` for a second copy of an operation, which does nothing; an archive and a full message are each refused as the STATE of what the gesture is about, `ErrArchived` and `ErrReactionsFull`, a conflict — kept the operation ledger's thirty days beside the highest position the prune has let go, `chat_verdicts_lost` (0070), because a pruned verdict and a record every node's gate dropped leave the same absence and the first is answered `unknown` and unvouched, never dropped), the writer reads it back before it answers and answers a refusal with the sentinel its own decision would have refused with (`Store.decided`), a retry the ledger answers reads its first copy's, and the change feed withholds the wake of a record this node refused while failing open where it has not decided one, so the company's wakes never wait on one node's applier; a rule a later build may change travels on the record (`MessagePost.MembersOnly`, `Reaction.Cap`) and the applier decides only the facts at its position. A message's SCOPE is its CHANNEL'S and there is no per-message term in the alphabet: the applier mints `channel_seq` from log order, which is deterministic only while a deferred record BLOCKS its channel (ADR-0046). A keyed gesture's operation id is derived from the caller's key on the actor (`Actor.OpKey`) through `statelog.KeyedOpID` over what the gesture ASKS, never what it resolved to, and a message's id IS its operation's. A SPEAKER IS A SEAT: a credential acting as itself (`AuthorOperator`) speaks in no room and reads none, and may only erase, prune and set a room's retention, which reach past membership without reading. The STORE judges values, never authority: a moderator's delete arrives as `MessageAuthority{Moderate}` and an archive as `ChannelAuthority`, decided by the asking node's authz and judged against the stored row. A DM's identity is DERIVED from the sorted handles in it. WHO A MESSAGE WAKES is `recipients.go`, one function set the decide and the parser both call — a PURE `Candidates` over the record and an impure `Route` over the live roster — and PEOPLE ARE NEVER WOKEN. A PERSON IS NEVER ANSWERED BY SILENCE: a person's message in a named room that names no seat the company has (a person included) and reaches no agent wakes the room's ANSWERER, one seat, resolved at write time through the roster and carried on the record (a unit's lead, otherwise the room's most senior agent member by `Roster.Senior`), and a person's `@channel` is a request (`collective_request`, addressed) where an agent's is news (`collective`): two reasons rather than one read with its author, because whether a wake obliges an answer is the reason's alone. A ROOM IS NAMED AS PEOPLE WRITE IT by one rule (`RoomRef`: its id, or its name with or without the `#` before it), which a seat's tools and the API's room questions both read a typed room by, and the set of rooms a viewer may read, which scopes a search, carries each room's name and kind beside its id (`ReadableRoom`), read with the set, so a hit is shown under the name the room had where the search was allowed to read it. The names of every room a viewer may read are a read of their own (`RoomNames`), the named rooms alone and bounded at `MaxRoomNames`, every live room before an archived one: what a reader tells a room written in prose (`#e2e`) from a colour or an issue's number by. What is NOT here: read cursors, mutes and do-not-disturb, which are in `coord` because a cursor is one reader's attention nobody replays; and its search, a keyword index of its own in `search` (`ChatIndexer`) with no vectors at all, because chat is a keyword corpus only (ADR-0071). Its read types DECLARE the fields that hold somebody else's words (`untrusted:"fence"` or `untrusted:"name"`, `provenance.FieldTag`), which the agent's read tools may not answer unlabelled |
| `knowledge` | The backend-neutral seam the turn-start knowledge prefetch and the `search_knowledge` builtin both read through. Exactly ONE backend per company, because two would make "what do we already know about this" depend on which searcher was asked. A caller passes a seat, plain text and ancestor exclusions — never a CQL fragment, a space key or a project list — and search is BEST EFFORT: every failure path is an empty result, so a turn never dies because a wiki was slow — and a search that never ran says so (no mode served, none of the corpus covered), so "the knowledge base could not be searched" is never told as "nothing matched". It also declares the ONE bound on a query's text (`MaxQueryBytes`, 400 bytes) for every ranked search — this seam's, the tracker's and chat's — which every surface, the dashboard included, refuses past and none cuts, because a search on part of a question answers another one. A HIT CARRIES ITS OWN LABEL (`Hit.Provenance`, ADR-0040), decided by the backend that found the page — the engine's own pages a colleague's with nobody named (`doclet.HitLabel`: every write there is a company principal's, and the index does not say which), a Confluence page `external` with nobody named (`confluence.HitLabel`: a result never says one person wrote every word, and the site is not the company's alone) — because only that backend knows who can write there and the node rendering a hit is often not the one that searched; it crosses the estate router's wire with the hit, and `Render` is the ONE renderer of hits into a model's text, one fence of the reader's token around the list, for the turn-start block and `search_knowledge` alike |
| `providers/llm` | The model contract, sized to what a harness can be handed and can honestly report — EVERY `providers.llm` entry is one (`harness`), and the only other implementations are the chain and test doubles. A conversation in and an answer and its usage out, and, for a seat at work, the phase's tools beside the conversation as a `ToolHost` (`toolhost.go`): the backend serves it to its harness as ONE MCP server for the length of the call, tells it what the harness's own record shows of each model request (`Observe`, a `Turn` with its `TurnCall`s) and what each cost (`Spent` — per request while running, and the unreported remainder at the end on EVERY outcome), stops its harness when `Done` closes, and hands a fallback member what the phase already did (`Pending`) unless the phase is decided (`Halted`). The harness calls the tools NATIVELY, so there are NO TOOL CALLS IN A `Completion`: they happened during the call, through the host, which decided and recorded them. `ServeOptions` is what the backend tells the host about its harness because the host decides with it — whether it re-lists (`Relists`), its own tool timeout, whether it reports requests at all (`ObservesRequests`: a harness whose record shows none counts as one round, and is never refused for it), the `_meta` key its call ids ride (`CallIDMeta`), whether its record shows calls only after they ran (`RecordsCallsLate`), which of a response's calls it makes at once (`Concurrency`: at once, read-only only, in turn) and a callback for when tools are in flight (`OnCall`). Offered, never forced: there is no tool-choice field. NOTHING ELSE: no temperature, no output cap, no effort, no reasoning field, because no harness takes one per call, and a field no backend sends is a setting every caller believes it changed — a caller that needs a short answer says so in its prompt. THREE stop reasons (`end`, `tool_use`, `cap` — the harness's own turn cap), the three a harness can name; a harness that hit its ceiling or declined says so in prose or fails. It does NOT retry (fallback belongs to the chain) and does NOT decide what a failure means beyond a coarse kind. An entry has NO MODE, and every phase runs the harness's own loop over the seat's tools: a seat's executor is always its harness's own run, launched in a box by `internal/sandbox` with the seat's tools bridged in, and every other phase — the reviewer, a worker, the onboarding pass, a side pass — runs on this host over a `ToolHost`, so only an entry whose harness has an in-box runner can be an executor and any harness serves the rest |
| `providers/llm/chain` | The seat's model fallback: an `llm.Provider` over its entries, tried in order. Fallback is per CALL — for a seat at work one harness run, a whole loop of requests and tool calls — and a member that fails partway has already had calls run, recorded and charged, so the next member is handed THE SAME REQUEST AND HOST and its backend shows its harness what the host holds since the conversation was last folded (`ToolHost.Pending`) as work already done, never re-run: a second post written by a model that never saw the first. Only the failed member's unfinished prose is dropped, and a stream that moves on says so (`Delta.Restart`) rather than concatenating two half-answers. The walk STOPS WHERE THE PHASE IS DECIDED: a member whose run the host ended — the phase submitted, parked, ran out of rounds or was stopped, or the run was cut for something the next run of the loop answers — is not continued by another (`ToolHost.Halted`), because what follows is the tool loop's. A FATAL kind stops it too — a call the engine or its host cannot make (a prompt it cannot render, a harness it cannot start), which is the operator's to fix — and nothing else does: a harness that RAN and failed, however it failed, is its vendor's outage or its own crash, and the next member shares neither. It names the member that answered on the answer itself (`Completion.ProviderKey`), because a chain shared by concurrent callers has no other place that fact is true. A seat's EXECUTOR falls back per LAUNCH instead (`Pick`): its run is the harness's own, in a box and hours long, so there is no call to hand to the next member once it started, and the launch asks each member whether it is ready in the same order and with the same vocabulary a call uses — a retryable refusal (a member sitting out a spent plan) hands off, a fatal one or the caller's own cancellation stops the walk, and running out is the same `Error` a call reports. And a chain can say WITHOUT A CALL what one would meet: `Unready`, the failure that clears first where every member would fail, and `KnownLimit`, the whole `Limit` where every member sits out one with an instant, read as `Limited` reads a call's failure, which a coding run's collected completion parks its seat on before its review is ever called |
| `providers/llm/llmtest` | A harness for tests: a provider that, handed a request with tools, connects to the tool host's session exactly as a harness's MCP client does — through the relay's socket and the real MCP server — and plays a scripted model against it: each response's prose, then the calls it makes, then the next response, until the script ends, a response calls nothing or the host ends the run. THROUGH THE REAL SESSION, never around it, because a double that called the host's dispatcher directly would test a path no harness takes: the socket, the tool list, the withheld answers and the release are on the way of every real call. A script can give up, die, rewrite a name (as Hermes does), run a tool of its own and report its requests with or without per-request structure, so each gate is exercised against what a real harness was measured doing. Shared, additive-only: a field or a function is added, never changed under an existing caller |
| `providers/llm/harness` | The ONLY model backend: a harness driven as a process, because each vendor's own harness — the request shape each model takes, how its thinking is carried, caching, which sign-in bills which account — is work the vendors keep current and a copy of it here was permanently behind. Three problems make it more than "shell out": SHARED MEMORY (every call runs in its seat's own home with an ALLOWLISTED environment, `workspace.go`, `env.go` — and a call that hands its harness a KEY in a KEY HOME of its own, never seeded with the login and never carried back from, its credential files dropped with each generation, because a harness may keep a key's session in its login's own file: Cursor Agent writes `CURSOR_API_KEY`'s, the key among it, into the `auth.json` its login lives in, which carried back became every subscription seat's sign-in), A LOOP OF ITS OWN (a seat's phase hands the harness its tools as ONE MCP server — the host's `Serve`, the profile's `mcp` route rendered into its argv, environment and working directory — and the harness calls them natively until its model stops or the host's `Done` stops it; the harness's own record, its stream or a file it writes, is read as it goes for each request's prose, calls and cost and handed to the host (`record.go`, `tail.go`); `timeout_seconds` bounds one STRETCH of the harness's own work and is stopped while the seat's tools run (`stretch.go`), because a run is a whole phase and a wall-clock cap derived for one answer would kill a healthy one; a slot is one LIVE PROCESS for its whole run, and a model call made inside one of its tool calls on the same entry BORROWS the run's slot (`slots.go`), which at `max_concurrent: 1` was otherwise a deadlock; a refreshed login is carried back as each call starts; the harness's own turn cap is `StopCap`, an answer rather than a failure; a failed run says why from stderr and the harness's own failure events ONLY, never its raw output, which carries every call's arguments and results; each run after a phase's first is a fresh process handed the whole transcript, NONCE-FENCED (`prompt.go`) so a tool result cannot forge a heading; and a harness whose loop runs in a server of its own, OpenCode 2.1, has that server started and polled until it has the tools, the run its client (`serve.go`) — see `toolrun.go`; a text function is one prompt in and one answer out) and VENDOR DRIFT (every flag and JSON path is profile data, `harnessprofile`). A SPENT PLAN is recognised by the vendor's verbatim sentence plus the reset instant it carries, never by keyword — and on a run the harness did not report failed, only where that sentence OPENS what it said, because what a clean run says is otherwise the model's own prose and tool arguments, and a sentinel matched inside them threw the answer away and benched a working entry (stderr, a failed run's output and a harness's own failure event are matched throughout) — and BENCHES the entry's SIGN-IN on this node (`bench.go`, `plans.go`; ADR-0069): what a vendor says is said about an account, so the window, the readiness verdict and the last answer and refusal are kept per `SignInID` (an HMAC, under a key derived from the keyring, of the harness, the auth mode and every credential its harness is handed, or of its login's install record, which a refresh never moves) in one node-wide `Plans` the engine makes at boot and hands every revision's build, so a rebuilt entry on the same account keeps its window, a new credential starts clean, entries on one credential pause together, and a model's own limit (`window: model`) holds that model argument alone; a limit is dated and named from the harness's fields first (`auth_events`, then `limit_reset_events`, then the sentence and `limit_windows`), and every child and box is handed `TZ` (the engine's own, else UTC) so a reset named with no zone is read in the zone it was written in: calls fail at once as `rate_limit` until that reset, capped at an hour, or, where none was named, for a window stepping 1, 2, 5 and then 15 minutes (`limitBackoff`), one step for each report that finds the last window over, back to the first only on an answer dated after that window; every window is DATED BY ITS REPORT, so an executor run's ending (`Provider.RunEnded`), read again when its job is collected again, opens no new window and takes no step, and every window is KEPT ON THE WALL CLOCK (`onTheWall`), the clock a vendor names its reset on, since a monotonic reading stops while the host sleeps and a window kept with one refused for the length of the sleep while naming an end already past — so a seat moves to its next model without a process launched to hear the same sentence, and NOT shared with the fleet, since each peer learns it from its own calls once per window — at most `max_concurrent`, because a call that queued for a slot is refused once it holds one — and a coordination read in front of every call would put the store under every turn. A missing LOGIN is never benched: `crewlet llm login` mends it from another process, and the next call must find it. Usage is the harness's own report, cache reads and writes summed back into the input — on a run with tools per request as its record shows it, the remainder at the end on every outcome; a harness that reports none is charged four characters a token, which `doctor` says. `doctor`'s smoke test is the production route end to end: one `crewlet_smoke` tool on a real session, passing when the call was recorded and the record showed its request, and reporting the exact tool set the harness offered its model (`smoke.go`). A harness that STARTED is never `fatal`: `run`'s error means only that none did, and everything that happened to one that ran is a result its caller classifies (`exec.go`). Its output is on pipes the engine owns, because which stream a helper the harness left running still holds decides the call — a held stdout is an answer nobody can show is whole, refused as `server`, while a held stderr costs only diagnosis and the answer stands — and os/exec, handed writers, reports both as one `ErrWaitDelay`. Building a provider writes NOTHING (the workspace is a process-wide map keyed by the absolute state directory AND the harness, each harness keeping its files under `<state dir>/<harness>` so two harnesses on one directory never meet — keyed by the directory alone, an entry moved to another harness was refused by every running node — and a configured bundle is restored by `RestoreConfiguredBundle`, never by `New`), so `crewlet validate` is not a write to the host. A credential BUNDLE NAMES THE HARNESS it was exported from, in a manifest (`harnessprofile.BundleManifest`) that is never written into the credential directory, and a restore or an import refuses one naming another harness or none (`ErrForeignBundle`) before anything is written: a bundle is keyed by the entry, which can move to another harness, and a file name cannot say whose a login is — six harnesses keep an `auth.json` — so judged by its files' names alone, codex's bundle was written into pi's empty login, which then turned the right bundle away as a login already present |
| `providers/llm/harness/harnessprofile` | What each harness ACCEPTS — the profile table (`profiles.yaml`): its binary and flags, the JSON shapes its answer and usage arrive in, where it keeps its login, which variable carries its key (`api_key_env`) and its headless token (`token_env`), and the grammar its model flag reads (`model_names_provider`) — plus the merge of an entry's `overrides` into it and the rules a merged profile is held to. A built-in may carry a `platforms` section (`darwin`, `linux`) merged in BEFORE the overrides, so an operator's field outranks it, and every built-in is validated as each platform reads it, not only the one CI runs on. A LEAF beside the backend rather than inside it, because `config` judges an entry against its profile rather than leaving it to the epoch build, which the API never runs. A typo in `overrides` (a profile that does not load) is refused by every write path — `crewlet validate` and every API admission — and by every apply. A credential no variable of the harness carries, or one handed over in a variable outside the reference grammar (`overrides.token_env: MY-TOKEN`), is refused by every write path and only warned about on an apply: the entry still runs, signed in by whatever its harness does read, and refusing a revision a newer peer admitted would take the node off the fleet's epoch. Standard library and the YAML decoder only, because `config` imports it. The TOOL ROUTE is profile data too, measured per harness: `mcp` — how the harness is told of the `crewlet` server (args, an allowlist that names the server rather than each tool, seeds written ONLY into the call's working directory because the socket is the run's alone and the seat's home is shared, environment, its own tool timeout, whether it re-lists, which calls it makes at once, its failure events, a run's own argv, usage command and output, and a `serve` lifecycle for a harness whose loop runs in a server of its own); `requests` — how its own record shows each model request (a stream or a file, the file under a shared home told apart by `{session}` or its owner line, rule by rule, the `_meta` key its call ids ride, how it spells or files the server's tools, whether calls appear only once they ran); `cap` — its own turn cap; `failure_markers` — a stderr line saying its confinement failed to load, which stops the run; and `key_seed_files`, settings a KEY home alone gets. A profile with no `mcp` answers text functions and refuses a seat's phase naming `overrides.mcp`; one with no `requests` counts a run as one round, never refused. Its WEB STANCE too: the seats keep a harness's own web tools, and an entry that sets `overrides: {web_tools: denied}` runs through the profile's measured `web_denial` instead (refused on a profile that has none), which `Load` applies so every reader sees the argv that runs; and `tools_off`, the argv runs that leave a harness with NO TOOL OF ITS OWN beside the engine's server by construction, which `ToolsOffProblem` checks against the argv itself and the `mcp` allow list (the Dashboard Assistant's gate; a deny list is always a release behind, and Codex keeps apply_patch, which has no switch). |
| `providers/embeddings` | The vector backend: the knowledge corpus duty, each knowledge search's query vector, and diary and episode recall. It owns the FACT of what a model accepts — `Limits` in bytes of `Prepare`d text (whitespace collapsed and each stray byte a U+FFFD, as the request's JSON encoding makes it, so every bound, digest and cut is of the text the server decodes), resolved from `config.EmbeddingModels`, because every tokenizer these models use emits at most one token per byte plus a wrapper, so no tokenizer is needed — and enforces it: an input past the bound is refused before anything is sent (`ErrTooLong`), and `EmbedBatch` packs a batch into the requests the model's limits admit (`Limits.Requests`). The POLICY for a longer text is the caller's: the corpus embeds its `Opening`, a turn's ask is `EmbedWhole` — pieces cut between words, one call, a length-weighted mean of their directions. Nothing retries, because what a failure costs differs by caller; what every caller gets instead is the CLASS — `ErrRefused` (refused again unchanged), `ErrTransient`, `ErrConfiguration` — so the corpus can set aside one poison input rather than resend its batch for ever. A batch request has its own ceiling (`BatchTimeout`, a minute, a fifth of the corpus tick's progress budget) apart from a single call's fifteen seconds. The fake enforces the same limits by the same code and fails on request in the same classes. It is also the engine's one model-vendor SDK client (`openai.go`, `vendor.go`), since no harness computes embeddings: built with NO SDK RETRIES and with every `OPENAI_*` setting the SDK reads from the process environment undone (`withoutAmbientEnvironment`), so an admin key, an organization header or a custom header exported for something else never reaches the endpoint an embedder names, and with what the endpoint SAID about a refusal (`detail`) on the error, since the SDK's own text names the status alone |
| `sandbox` | The executor runtime: every agent seat's executor is its harness's own run in a box. A run is DETACHED: the launch starts it, the turn parks, and the engine resumes the same turn with the run's report — minutes later, possibly after a restart, possibly on another node. Nothing parks a goroutine on a running job. EACH COLLECTED LAUNCH IS A RECORD OF ITS OWN (`types.ExecutorCollected`, `Coordinator.publishCollected`) — its tokens, its harness's turns and price, its report and transcript — published ONCE per launch with its charge (`LaunchRecord.Published`), and NO PHASE: the work of the `execute` round that launched it, which every spend reader files it under. A run ASKS a person through `ask_and_wait` on its bridge (ADR-0068), in the shape of a `decision.Decision` (ask.go): the tool validates the whole ask, `PendingStore.RecordAsk` writes it onto the job's row only while that job is running, and the collection parks on what the row holds, never on a file in the box. A run that asks a person something frees the seat, and the answer CARRIES THE RUN ON (`LaunchRequest.Continue`): the same round relaunched in the box it kept, continuing the harness's own session, where the relaunch reuses that box (`PriorBox.ContinuesIn` and `Coordinator.hostsPrior`: the same harness, the same cell, a box this node drives), or afresh from its pushed branch otherwise. The answer is SEALED on the run's row under the fleet keyring (`seat.run.answer`, answerseal.go), bound to the run, its question and its deliveries, and its words and answerer exist there only inside the seal: every reader opens it first, one under a key the node does not hold waits recorded and owed, and one that does not open is handed to no turn, attributed to nobody and let go of. Every HOST-SIDE ACCESS to a local box's files — read, write, directory made, file removed — goes through its home (or its records) opened as an `os.Root` (`boxLayout.openHome`), by a name relative to it, every directory on the way opened as a directory (a pipe there refused at once, never waited on), never a path checked and then opened: a directory the box made a link out of the home is refused at the access, whenever it was put there, and `hostbox.SafeJoin` only words the refusal (`directBox.explain`). A WRITE REPLACES THE FILE WHOLE (`replaceIn`, over `hostbox.ReplaceFileIn`): staged beside it under a fresh name, synced and renamed over it, so a reader in the box — the git credential helper reading the token file the poll rewrites under a running job — finds the old file or the new one, never one truncated and half refilled, and the rename writes through no link or other name at the path; only a regular file is replaced, anything else there is refused and left as the box made it, and nothing staged outlives a failed write. An E2B box's write is envd's upload under a fresh name beside the path and its filesystem `Move` over it (`envdClient.writeFile`), because envd writes an upload in place. A STREAMED write (`Sandbox.WriteStream`, for a session archive carried into a box, which grows with its session) is the same replacement fed a piece at a time and never held whole — copied into the staged file on the engine host (`replaceFrom`), and on E2B the upload's multipart body written through a pipe by a goroutine the call stops and waits for (`envdClient.uploadStream`), chunked and so sent once — and a source that fails part way leaves the file as it was; `sandboxtest.Box` holds every backend to it, and `sandboxtest.HostWrites` holds the engine host's boxes to refusing what is not a regular file for both writes. A `direct` command starts in its home and its own shell enters its working directory (`inWorkdir`), so the engine never walks into it. A resume the budget refuses is HELD (`HeldError`, `PendingRun.HeldFor`/`HeldUntil`), the row back to running, skipped by the waiter and refused by every claim (`PendingStore.ClaimForResume`, an announcement made before the hold and still queued behind the resume that held it included) until its window turns or the budget is readmitted (`PendingStore.LiftHold`), rather than abandoned. A job a collection READ BACK and handed back to running (`LaunchRecord.Collected`) is announced again by the poll with its paused box left ASLEEP (`Waiter.reach`), never woken or kept alive: the job is over, and the next collection wakes the box to read it. A completion whose resume met a MODEL'S LIMIT after its claim is held by THE NODE THAT MET IT, in its memory (`keepRefused`, refused by `stillRefused` until the limit's instant), NEVER on the row, which every node reads: the limit is that node's models', so a restart or `ReleaseSeat` forgets the hold and the seat's next holder asks its own models, and a hold kept after that release (the release waits for no resume, and the resume returns once its review has run) asks for the seat's lease once kept and is dropped where the seat is gone; written on the row, it silenced the job for every holder until the instant. A person's pause with `stop_running` ENDS the seat's launching and running runs at once (`Coordinator.StopSeat`, `stop.go`): a decided ending like any other, with WHO stopped it recorded at the top of the row in the deciding write (`PendingRun.Stop`) so the seat's next holder finishes the same stop after a crash; finished by closing the bridge session, reading the run's spend off its box — on another node's host only by this node's own retry of the kept ending, never on the frame that decided it (`Coordinator.endRun`), a silent host asked again until it answers or the box timeout since the stop has passed — and charging and publishing it ONCE (`PendingStore.CollectStop`), reclaiming the box, announcing `executor_stopped` — never `executor_failed`, which is a failure by type — and closing a parked turn as stopped through the engine (`CoordinatorOptions.CloseStopped`). A LOSS — an ending that names why the run was lost (`PendingRun.lost`) — hands the run to the engine as a step of the ending, after its announcement and before the delete (`CoordinatorOptions.TurnLost`), which answers the colleague whose ask the lost turn took up: no frame of that turn is left to, and best effort, because the turn is over whatever the asker hears. It is NOT the seat fence: a seat that moves leaves its run running; a stop leaves resumed and parked runs alone (`Stoppable`), and a job a collection already read and handed back to its held or retried resume, which is its turn's (`LaunchRecord.Collected`; the store refuses a stop of it) and whose WAIT the stop ends instead, the row's hold lifted and a completion this node kept re-fired (`Coordinator.endCollectedWait`), the engine's gate admitting a stopped seat's completion past its budget and its models' limit (`Engine.seatStopping`), a resumed turn whose call meets a limit or the budget as the stop lands settled as stopped rather than reverted to wait (`Engine.resumeWaits`), and a keep the stop raced re-fired rather than held (`stopLanded`, signalled by the stop), and a launch asks its turn's fence again once its row exists (`LaunchRequest.Fence`); a stop decided on a launching run is kept for the frame launching it (`Coordinator.LaunchStopped`), which closes its turn from it rather than from the pause. Two real backends behind one contract — a remote VM per run (E2B, cloud or self-hosted) and the engine host itself — held as a CATALOGUE keyed on `Placement`, because `providers.sandbox` configures which boxes exist and the `providers.llm` entry a launch picks places its run with its `run_in` (else `default_run_in`).
