The row carries a person's NOTES to the run's turn (`RunSteer`, `notes.go`): filed by any node, sealed by the engine, each WAITING until the write that hands it over records it read — the launch for a brief (`PendingRun.BriefNotes`), a take pinned to the job, its statuses, an undecided ending and its lease (`TakeNotes`), every such write numbering itself among the record's reads (`RunSteer.Reads`, `NoteRead.Order`), which is the order the turn read them in — bounded by the caller's figures (except the first segment's box's own, filed waiting by the write that creates the record, `PendingRun.HandedNotes`, which the box already bounded and answered accepted), watched by the node serving the run's bridge (`WatchNotes`), kept by every reset and park, and expired by the ending before its delete (`CoordinatorOptions.SteersExpired`) — every note no job ran with (`PendingRun.UnreadNotes`): waiting, or read by the brief of a job that never started, which the launch says it wrote the moment it did (`LaunchRequest.Recorded`) — STARTED being the job's own record (`LaunchRecord.Started`, written by the attach that names its command), never the row's command, which a park's teardown and an expired pause clear from a job that ran. A Claude Code run READS its notes between its steps through HOOKS in a settings file of its own (`executor`, `ClaudeCode`, `--settings`): two `mcp_tool` hooks calling the bridge's unlisted `steer_take` after each batch of the main agent's calls and at Stop, `disableAllHooks` written FALSE so a repository's `true` cannot switch them off, no field newer than the `mcp_tool` hook (print mode ignores a file it cannot validate), passed only to a release at or after 2.1.163 (`claudeNotesFloor`: the run's script reads `claude --version` first, because an older release runs the hooks and drops what the Stop one answers, recording a note read that never reached the model), and with them the run's standing instructions TOLD OF NOTES (`Paths.ClaudeNotesSystem`) — an older release is handed them as they stand (`Paths.System`), so the gate (`claudeNotesGate`) chooses the system text with the hooks, its brief on STDIN rather than argv, `CLAUDE_CODE_SIMPLE` and `CLAUDE_CODE_SAFE_MODE` unset by the wrapper (`Output.Unset`) because only it reaches every source of them, and a `hook_stopped` terminal reason read as a FAILURE rather than an empty success. An OpenCode run's CONFIGURATION is written beside its other artefacts (`Paths.OpenCodeConfig`), never into the checkout, and handed to its server alone as `OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT`, which OpenCode ranks above a repository's own `opencode.json` and `.opencode/` (`OPENCODE_CONFIG` would rank below them), so the repository's file is left as it is and loads beneath it; every entry the run owns states `disabled: false`, and every MCP server value — url, header, environment — is a `{file:}` reference to a file of its own under `Paths.OpenCodeValues`, a directory the clear removes whole, so the content holds paths and no value; a home that text cannot name (a brace, which opens or ends a `{env:}`/`{file:}` reference OpenCode replaces before it parses, a quote, a backslash, a control character, or a relative path) is refused before anything is written. An OpenCode run READS its notes at its next step through the engine's own PLUGIN (`opencode_steer.js`, embedded, written into `Paths.OpenCodeSteerPlugin` before the server starts and named by the configuration with the notes route as a `{file:}` reference): it polls the bridge's notes route only while the run's own session — named to the server alone as `CREWLET_STEER_SESSION` — is executing, admits each note as a steer with `resume: false` under its job-derived inbox id (`OpenCodeInboxID`), moves its cursor only past an admitted note, confirms only what OpenCode reports delivered, backs off from one second to thirty, never polls again sooner than a second after a poll that admitted nothing, stops for good on a refused token or a missing route, and writes nothing; once the run has ended its script CANCELS every steer the plugin admitted and OpenCode never delivered (`openCodeLeftoverSteers`), because the session's inbox outlives the server and would hand a continued run the note its brief carries too; and the run's answer is its LAST message's text, never every step's joined. The standing instruction about notes is its harness's own (`noteReader.NoteInstruction`: `ClaudeCodeSteerInstruction` names the hook-context headings Claude Code puts a note under — a system message, or on some models a system reminder at the end of the last tool result — and `OpenCodeSteerInstruction` a message from the user), ADDED BY THE HARNESS rather than the runner (`withNoteInstruction`), only to a run wired to them that its harness's reader is started for, and a run that succeeded without once asking (`RunSteer.Pulled`) is said at its collection (`steer_reader_never_ran`), only where the runner says its harness reads notes and the record names a seat. Every write to a run's ROW is a decision handed to the coordination store's read-decide-write (`coord.SandboxRuns.MutateSandboxRun`, coordstore.go), which takes it again on a lost race for the store's patience, never for a count: one row takes every tool call its box makes over the bridge beside its seat's own writes, and the sixteen attempts it once made could report that busy row as one that "kept changing" and drop the write. The coordinator's own loops over OUTCOMES — a seat's recount, an answer's record, an ending's rounds, a let-go, a launch's create-or-reset — each carry a named bound with its reason. A run's placement is on its ROW: the turn that collects it may be another process on another node, and reconnecting to a remote box through the local backend reports a box that vanished. A `direct` or `container` box is its LAUNCHING NODE'S (`PendingRun.LaunchNode`, boxhost.go, ADR-0065), because no other node can reach it: there are TWO WAITERS (`Drives`) — the fleet's, under the duty, for the boxes every node reaches, and every node's own for the boxes on its host, which also reclaims a box no run names any more off the run each box records (`Waiter.sweep`, `HostBox`) — and a seat's holder that is not the box's host asks the host to read it back over a signed channel (`BoxReader`, `sandbox.box_read`; the host reads only what the row licenses, `Coordinator.ReadHostedBox`), leaves the box's reclaim to its host and tells it (`Nudge`), and relaunches in a fresh box of its own; a relaunch's row keeps the previous job's box with its host, and none of that job's command or session, until it attaches a box of its own (`BoxRef.Node`). A launch node away for the box timeout, watched within one stint of the duty (`Waiter.forgetAbsences`), is found gone by the fleet's waiter, which records its running jobs lost with it (`PendingRun.Lost`, `launch_node_gone`) — never a node merely restarting inside that bound. A JOB LOST WITH ITS HOST IS CARRIED ON, NEVER COLLECTED (`lost.go`): a `direct` job dies with the restart that replaces its engine — its container, its systemd unit's control group, its host — leaving what a crash leaves, so every local box records its job's WITNESS, the engine process that last saw it running by pid and kernel start time (`local_witness.go`), and a job that is not running ENDED while that process is still there and was LOST once it is gone too (`JobState`, `Sandbox.Job`, the runner's `Poll`); a `container` box asks its runtime's listing whether the container is up before it asks the job, so a daemon that is away is no answer rather than an ended job. The waiter that drives the box records the loss (`engine_restarted`, `box_gone` for a box its backend says is gone after the give-up window, `launch_node_gone`) on the row before announcing the completion, and the seat's holder reads what the job spent where its box is left — asked again as a collection is, left behind past the give-up window — charges and publishes it once, noted cut off, and resumes the turn with `ResumeRequest.Lost` to relaunch the same round (`PendingRun.Continues`), at most `MaxRoundLosses` (2) times a round (`PendingRun.RoundLosses`, counted by the relaunch that carries a lost job's round on); a lost job that had asked is parked on its question. A run whose `submit_work` or `ask_and_wait` lands over its bridge is polled at once and on a short schedule after (`Waiter.Nudge`); the 15 s poll is the fallback. The closed set `run_in` accepts is asserted against the switch that builds them. A run's bridge session is opened once its JOB is named (`LaunchRequest.Opened`) and before any box exists, so a launch the bridge cannot serve is refused with nothing started, and it numbers the job's calls after the ones the row keeps (`OpenedJob.LastSeq`), so a job carrying on one that asked a person goes on in the round's order rather than starting again at one beside the asking job's calls; and the coordinator tells the engine of every run it recovers still RUNNING (`CoordinatorOptions.Recovered`), which re-opens that job's session on this node with the surface rebuilt from the run's row — a reopen that cannot be made logs `bridge_not_reopened` and never fails the recovery. A Direct box may be CONFINED (`LocalOptions.Confine`): every command its job starts runs under sandbox-exec, bubblewrap or Landlock (the engine binary re-executed as `sandbox-confine`), kept from the paths its caller names, every other box and its own records, and refused outright on a host that can enforce none (ADR-0054). WHERE A LOCAL BOX LIVES is one rule (`BoxRoot`): the company's `providers.sandbox.local.state_dir`, else `DefaultBoxRoot` — a seat's boxes under its `boxes/`, and the Dashboard Assistant's confined deep-work boxes, which the engine makes under `assistant/` beside them, a directory no seat's backend lists, reattaches or reaps — and never the store's directory, which is the volume holding the store and the stream. Every process a local box runs goes BEFORE ITS ENGINE when memory runs out (`oom.go`, Linux): raised to the engine's own `oom_score_adj` plus the kernel's whole scale of 1000, held to its ceiling (`boxOOMScoreAdj`), because that is the one lead at which the kernel takes the box first whatever the two hold, and relative because the engine's value is its deployment's decision — a `direct` box by its first process, which raises itself and then execs the command, AHEAD OF ANY CONFINEMENT (Landlock leaves a box nothing to write outside its home) and never by a write from the engine after the start, which would race the box's first fork (`oomFirst`); a `container` box by the runtime's `--oom-score-adj`, which both runtimes give every exec, spliced before `run_args` so an operator's own wins. A seat's executor box on the `direct` cell is NOT confined: its run is the seat's fully permissioned harness as the engine's own user on the engine host, which is why a shared host places its seats' runs in `container` or `e2b`. An address the engine hands a box (the bridge, the telemetry receiver, an HTTP MCP server, the run's notes route) is written in the ENGINE HOST'S network, so a box with a network of its own — a `container` box — is handed a loopback host as its runtime's name for the engine host (`EngineAddress`, enginehost.go, applied in the one runner `Start` and in the telemetry merge); a `direct` box is handed it as written, and so is a value an operator declared for the box itself. The bridge's address is the engine's choice per CELL (`mcpbridge.Bridge.Reaches`): a `direct` box dials its launching node's own loopback listener where that node has one, and every other box — a `container` one included — dials `CREWLET_MCP_BRIDGE_URL`, which reaches a `container` box under that name when it names loopback. The telemetry receiver's is chosen by the same rule (`OtelReceiver.Reaches`, from the spec's cell in the telemetry merge): its host address is taken ONLY WHERE THE NODE FORWARDS (`OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT` set), since what reaches a node with no upstream is dropped, and a box no address reaches is handed no endpoint at all Each job's record names the sign-in its harness was handed (`LaunchRecord.SignInID`) and why it follows the job before it (`FollowUp`), and a cell's readiness is probed with the backend's own functions (`probe.go`), never by making a box. A run's COMMITS CREDIT CREWLET THROUGH GIT, never through an instruction a model may forget (`executor`, attribution.go): where its request asks (`RunRequest.Attribution`, which every launch path takes from `Manager.CommitAttribution`, on unless `providers.sandbox.commit_attribution` is false, naming a seat's run by its turn and deep work by nothing), the runner writes one hook script into the box, links it under every hook name git runs, and the wrapper appends `core.hooksPath` to the run's `GIT_CONFIG_COUNT` entries after the profile is sourced, which outranks every git configuration file without writing one; the script hands each hook on to the repository's own (its `core.hooksPath` included), and prepare-commit-msg adds `Co-authored-by: Crewlet <noreply@crewlet.ai>` and `Crewlet-Run:` only to a commit whose author is the identity the run commits as, so a person's commit the run amends, cherry-picks or rebases is never credited. A run is CHARGED WHAT IT SPENT, never its session's spend, which both harnesses report on a run that continues a session: Claude Code's per-run `model_usage_since_last_result` summed over the run's results (2.1.310+), else its result less the one the job before it left in the box (`Paths.BaseResult`, kept at `Start` before the clear, where the run continued its session and the release recorded in `Paths.SessionRecord` restores the session's totals, 2.1.277+); OpenCode's session report after the run less the one its script read before it (`crewlet.usage_before`). A run's HARNESS SESSION IS NAMED BEFORE THE HARNESS STARTS (`executor.Harness.Session`, returned as `RunHandle.SessionID`) and RECORDED by its script (`Paths.SessionRecord`: the session the harness ran, whether it CONTINUED the one it was asked to, and the harness's `--version` — `Result.SessionID`, `Result.Continued`, `Result.HarnessVersion`); a session the box neither holds nor can restore is never resumed — the run starts the one minted in its place (`Session.Fresh`) and says why, on its error stream and in its record (`Result.FellBack`), since Claude Code fails a run on a session it does not hold and OpenCode creates an empty one under the id. Claude Code's session layout is PINNED by the wrapper over whatever set it (`Output.Pin`, `claudeSessionLayout`: `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` the box home's `.claude`, `CLAUDE_CODE_PROJECT_DIR_NAME` `crewlet`, auto memory off), a run is started with `--session-id` or `--resume` and never both, a resumed one with `--system-prompt-snapshot off` from 2.1.267 (`claudeSnapshotFloor`) so it reads THIS launch's standing instructions, and the transcript's trim is on from 2.1.304 and unset before. Once the harness exits a run PACKS its session (`Paths.SessionCapture`, `Result.Captured`, streamed by `Runner.OpenCapture`): Claude Code's transcript, sidecars and task list as a gzipped tar (`claudeCapture`), OpenCode's `session export` through its own server, gzipped (`openCodeCapture`) — never a login, the harness's own state, file checkpoints or OpenCode's database, which holds the login. A run handed a session packed in another box (`RunRequest.Carried`, refused unless its harness, format and session are the run's — `refuseCarried`) has it written in with `WriteStream` after the clear and restored by its script — Claude Code's three paths unpacked under the pinned layout, OpenCode's export imported with `session import --standalone --directory "$PWD"` before its server starts and required to say it imported that session, which also starts its instruction state over. A box that already holds the session is compared BY ID AND BY THE ARCHIVE'S DIGEST (`Paths.SessionDigests`: the POSIX `cksum` of every archive of a session the box packed or restored, kept for the box's life): its copy is never written over with an archive it holds, which it may have run past after a capture that failed, and is replaced by one it does not, from a box the session went on in — Claude Code's files removed once the archive proves to hold a transcript, OpenCode's copy deleted with `session delete --standalone` once the export unpacks. Whether a box can continue one is its harness's release, read in the box by one login-shell exec (`Runner.CheckCarry`, `carrier.Continues`): Claude Code 2.1.310 and a writer of 2.1.300, OpenCode 2.0.32, never a box older than the writer, never across harnesses. |
| `hostbox` | The primitives for running somebody else's process on the engine host. Three subsystems must do it IDENTICALLY — the subscription harness backend, which drives a harness per seat with its own HOME, the local sandbox provider (a harness in a box, and the container runtime's own CLI), and the MCP client's stdio children — and each primitive is a guard, so a second implementation is not drift but a hole. THE ENVIRONMENT IS ONE ALLOWLIST: `PassthroughEnv` for every child, `HostUserEnv` beside it for a child that runs as the host user in no box of its own (a box replaces those), `ContainerRuntime` for a container runtime's own CLI — the sandbox's and a `command: docker` tool server's alike, one rule so neither copy loses `DOCKER_HOST` — and `Environ` to render it, which is NEVER nil because os/exec reads a nil `Cmd.Env` as the engine's own — and the engine's own is where Tier A's keyring and token values resolve from. The MCP client handed every stdio server that whole environment, every container-runtime call reached its CLI the same way, and the CLI backend's private copy of the list had drifted by five names. It is not isolation: a child runs as the engine's user and can read what that user can, so what the allowlist removes is the engine HANDING its secrets over |
| `flock` | The ONE exclusive cross-process file lock: the node database's (`store`), the embedded broker's store directory (`queue/jetstream`) and a harness's shared login (`hostbox`), which each carried a copy of the syscall before. flock, never fcntl (an fcntl lock is dropped when ANY descriptor for the file closes in the process); the file never unlinked (a peer mid-open would lock a dead inode while a third locked a fresh one); the holder stamped only once the lock is held, and not synced, since a reader sees the page cache and a crashed holder has released the lock with it. PER DESCRIPTOR, so two takers in one process exclude each other as two processes do; a caller that wants its handles to SHARE a claim (`store`) counts its sharers on top of one lock. Non-blocking: a caller that waits (`hostbox`) polls with its own bound and its own words for the holder. |
| `procgroup` | Addressing a child process TREE with one signal. Every long-running child starts behind something that forks — `uvx`, `npx`, a Node runtime, a shell — so signalling the process Go started reaches the launcher and nothing beneath it. Its own process group makes the tree one negative pid, and it lives here rather than beside either caller because two copies of a platform-conditional signal helper is how one stops matching the other |
| `learning` | What a seat remembers. Everything here is BEST EFFORT by design: a failed write is logged, a failed read answers empty. `memsync` is the exception that makes the rest true across a fleet: a seat's memory is written to the NODE's store, and placement moves seats — so every memory row rides a COMPACTED CHANGELOG (one subject per row, one message retained per subject) and a node hydrates a seat's rows before its mailbox attaches — READING TO WHERE THE CHANGELOG'S LEADER SAYS THEY END, because the replay is a consumer the server places on any replica, and one hosted on a copy behind an acknowledged write stopped short of the seat's newest rows and reported success. Deletes deliberately do not travel: the lifecycle re-converges, and a tombstone protocol would be a second thing to keep correct forever. It DECLARES the two `statelog` contracts it rests on rather than implementing their shapes privately: `ReplayCompacted`, which every rule in the hydration follows from, and STREAM IDENTITY — because a recreated changelog is EMPTY, so hydrating from one reports success and gives every seat on the node a blank diary, and a compacted stream has no checkpoint and no generation to answer a recreation with, so its only honest move is to refuse the seat. A SEAT'S MEMORY IS KEYED ON ITS HANDLE, which is immutable (ADR-0013): the diary and onboarding markers on the agent id derived from it, and episodes, both skill tables, counterparty profiles (a colleague subject included) and the conversation ledger on the handle itself, in the `agent_handle`/`observer_handle`/`subject_handle` columns memsync carries rows between peers by. `memread` is the other half: every node that ever ran a seat keeps a copy and only the HOLDER's is current, so a screen's read of a seat's memory or conversation ledger follows the seat's LEASE — answered here, asked of the holding incarnation on an ephemeral scatter every node serves (a node serving no API may be the holder), or empty and naming nobody for a seat no node holds — and a silent or still-hydrating holder is `unavailable`, never an empty memory. A read names its seat both ways (`memread.Seat`): the agent id its lease, diary and onboarding marker are keyed by, and the handle the rest of its memory is keyed by. A screen's read of a seat's skills carries its ORDER (`skills_order`: `newest`, the zero value, `uses` or `updated`, `learning.SkillOrders`, each ending on the id) and the HOLDER orders the whole set before it cuts the page — an additive field within the wire's version 1 — because a page sorted after it was cut answers "the most used of the newest fifty"; the dashboard's `LEARNED_SKILL_ORDERS` is held to the list by a `clientsource` row. Its reads RAISE, unlike the turn's: "remembers nothing" and "the store did not answer" are opposite facts on a screen. And the WRITE side follows the seat for the same reason: post-turn reflection is fed by a `reflection_due` wake on the SEAT'S OWN subject, attached by the holder after hydration and let go before the release's last flush — on one fleet-wide group, all but one in N of a seat's reflections wrote memory where nothing carried or read it. A seat's PAST ASK keeps WHO WROTE IT: an episode stores the `provenance.Label` its turn's ask was read under (`ask_provenance`, node migration 0058, carried by memsync with the ask), and `RenderPastTurn` is the ONE rendering of a past turn into a model's text — the ask fenced under that label, everything else on the line neutralised — because a stranger's message recalled a month on is not the seat's own memory, which is the voice it trusts most; a label that does not decode reads as the zero label, fenced and vouched for by nobody, never as none. And the POST-TURN WORKERS that write that memory (the persist decider, the profiler, the skill synthesizer and refiner) read what the turn was told FENCED as the turn read it — each inbound message under its own label through ONE rendering (`inboundBody`), the ask under the turn's record of it (`describeTurn`), under a token minted per call beside the standing sentence their system prompts end on — and everything else they are shown neutralised, because what they write the seat reads later as its own |
| `schedule` | Role- and unit-scoped cron work, with at-most-once delivery, missed-tick catchup and a wall-clock cap |

## The edges

| Package | What it is for |
|---|---|
| `api` | REST + the dashboard. Served INSIDE the engine's process and nowhere else: `crewlet run` builds it over the engine's own store, broker and coordination plane, and `New` refuses a missing one by name, because a narrower answer built around a nil (an omitted health field, a 503, an absent route) reads as deliberate and hides the wiring mistake. `NodeRuntime` is the seam it asks the engine through. EVERY REQUEST LEAVES THE GUARD CARRYING A PRINCIPAL — resolved, anonymous, or unknown — and the EXEMPTION LIST is the whole of what is not guarded (probes, webhooks on their own HMAC, the per-run token paths, the dashboard shell, the sign-in routes, and the sign-out of THIS session — which clears the cookie whatever the node can read, where guarded a node that could not read its identity estate answered it 503 before it ran, and which verifies every bearer it ends itself). There is no list of what IS: guarded is what a route is, so the always-guarded prefix list is gone and each surface states its authority as a VERB where it is enforced — which is why every surface `New` mounts, `/config`, `/secrets` and `/setup` included, is a `guardedMounter` whose routes go through `authz.Router`, and why the deployment's own controls (`/backup`, every `/worklet/retention*` route and the fleet broker's removals) are mounted there on `admin`. None of them decided anything before: the guard answers only whether somebody resolved, and a surface that relied on that opened itself to every signed-in reader the day sessions arrived. The query registry is where both transports meet that: one question, one declared POSTURE (`queries.Posture` — `Everybody()`, `Requires(admin|app)` or `DecidedBy(verb)`, whose answer decides record by record), and a REST route and a socket frame decided by the same check, so no question is reachable by choosing a channel — and a registration with a zero posture, a `Requires` of anything but admin or an app, or a `DecidedBy` of a verb the table does not know PANICS at wiring time, because a question nobody decided about is an ungated surface that ships looking deliberate — and so does a `DecidedBy` of a verb that asks a recent proof or a person present, since the socket has no code for either refusal, a walk in `internal/authz` holding the verbs an answer or a watch decides by inside itself to the same rule; a refusal travels as the decision whole (`queries.Refusal`, built only by `NewRefusal`) and both transports word it through `authz.RefusalFields`; a principal whose stage may not act is refused before any answer runs. THE OVERVIEW IS EVERYBODY'S (ADR-0033): the org chart, the agents' and coding runs' STATUS, spend, budgets, schedules, tools, health and `viewer` answer any resolved principal, while a call's or a run's BODY — prompts, responses, tool arguments, narration, `llm_history` payloads, a coding run's task, question, reply and bridged calls — goes only to admin, to the seat's lead (`audit.seat.read`) and, for a run's question, to the person it is put to, through ONE mask per surface (`livestate.MaskCall`, `ExecutorEntry.For`, the executor key walk) that zeroes the body's versions and sets `body_withheld`, held by walks that fail on a field nobody classified; and a cross-app answer filters its rows through `authz.Shows`. Its refusal is three-valued for `iam.From`'s reason: an identity estate this node cannot read is `ErrUnavailable` with a Retry-After, a context no resolver touched is a plain FAULT (a route nobody wired through the guard never clears by waiting), and only a credential positively checked and refused is unauthorized. THE VIEWER IS THE CALLER: the principal travels in the context on both transports and no operator id rides beside it, and `viewer=` is gone from `work_items` and `work_views` — it had exactly one honest value, and any other was a free choice of whose pins, personal views and queue to read. CHAT'S QUESTIONS GO FURTHER (`queries/chat.go`): they take no handle at all — a room is read as the seat the principal acts as, the same `iam.ActorFor` a chat write's author comes from, so whoever may post in a room is exactly whoever is shown it — and each decides its own SEATED verb on the authority table before it reads anything, so a credential acting as itself is refused `unseated` rather than shown the company's rooms as nobody; the badges ride the rail from the coordination read state, and one that could not be read is `read_state: false` rather than a cursor at zero. A question that names somebody else's record resolves the name first — `iam.OwnerOf` through `Sources.Holders`, which `New` REQUIRES, so a login is its holder's record and never read literally, and only after the question's own verb has been decided on the unresolved login (`Sources.mayLook`), so a caller who leads nobody is refused before the directory is asked — and is decided by the authority table on what it resolved to, under the QUESTION'S OWN VERB — a person's queue by the owner-or-lead class through `Sources.Chart`, whose UNKNOWN answers 503 rather than a refusal, and a seat's trail (`conversations`, `agent_memory`) by `audit.seat.read`, its lead or admin — and a refusal carries the rule's `reason` and the `grants` that would have admitted the caller rather than a sentence written beside the rule — and which `New` REQUIRES, because the production wiring once left it out and every lead's read of a report's record was a 503 no retry could clear; it used to be "any operator credential", which made every Tier A token a reader of every seat's inbox. A token acts AS A SEAT only when the identity directory holds an ACTIVE MACHINE row under its login bound to one, and that seat is resolved against the running org through the SAME `session.ResolveSeat` a signed-in person's is (`auth.SeatBindings`) — `403 seat_unavailable` once the running company no longer holds it as a human seat, `503` on a node that runs no company yet — because the lookup it replaced returned the row's raw handle, so a token went on acting as a removed seat; an UNBOUND answer is served even by a stalled node, which is what keeps break-glass reachable — and a seat's contact block names no credential at all, because it says how to reach a person and not which credential they hold. A CROSS-SITE WRITE is refused on its `Origin` before the handler runs, landing BEFORE any cookie-minting route exists rather than after, because the other order leaves a window in which every write on this surface is forgeable — and it judges the SIGN-IN routes the guard exempts (only the server-to-server edges, `/webhooks/`, `/otlp/`, `/mcp/`, are not judged), because exempting them from both left login CSRF open: a form on another site could post an attacker's password or redeem an attacker's invitation and leave the victim signed in as them. THREE CREDENTIAL SHAPES meet at that one frame — a Tier A bearer composed from the configuration alone, a SESSION COOKIE resolved through `internal/iam/session`'s two tables, and a MACHINE TOKEN (`auth.Tokens`, `Options.Tokens`) resolved from one read of the directory to its OWNER, carrying what it was minted with that the owner still holds, and naming itself on the principal's `Via` so every record and audit event it causes carries `pat:<id>` beside the owner — a configuration revision and a stored secret included, which keep the author, its kind and the credential in three columns (`auth.AttributionOf`) where they kept one, and it was the credential; the identity trail included too, a removal and a company-wide invalidation among it — which is also where `auth.PresentedToken` reads it, one carrier rather than a context value beside the principal — the two bearers told apart by the value's SHAPE, which picks the check and never admits anything; `crewlet run` builds the token arm on EVERY node that serves the API, and every one of them holds the estate (`ingress` requires `data`) and applies the directory from boot, company or none, so a token minted on a peer resolves there too — and a node that has not applied the mint yet answers 503 rather than a 401 teaching a pipeline its credential is broken — and the HEADER WINS when both arrive, because a browser sends its cookie whether or not the caller meant to and an `Authorization` header is only ever there because somebody put it there, so a request presenting a WRONG one stays anonymous rather than being upgraded by whatever was in the jar. The node's `api.auth.admin` posture clamps a person exactly as it clamps a token: on a node set to `refuse`, admin is withheld from every credential the guard resolves and every app it includes is kept. A PRESENTED BEARER MEETS NO CURVE, and that is decided rather than missing (`bearers.go`): a bearer names nobody until it is compared, so the only curve that could stand in front of the comparison is one keyed on the ADDRESS, and one was tried — a stranger refusing one guessed value every twenty-five seconds held every valid bearer at the address, break-glass included, at `429` on every guarded route, and a client with a dozen VALID requests in flight met the same `429` because an unresolved attempt counted as a refusal. A curve on `POST /auth/token` alone slows nobody, since every guarded route answers the same guess the same way. What protects a bearer is its VALUE — 32 bytes of `crypto/rand`, a keyring HMAC, a Tier A value config refuses under 26 characters — and what a guess costs is a count on the audit trail's per-minute failure row. And AN UNGUARDED ROUTE NEVER COMPARES ONE (`Guard.resolveUnguarded`): it resolves a session cookie, which the sign-out reads, and only MARKS a bearer, because nothing unguarded acts on whom a bearer names and comparing it there timed a right value against a wrong one — a matching Tier A token reads the directory for its seat binding before it answers — at line rate on `/health`, `/static` and the webhooks, where no refusal is counted. And TWO answers are neither a resolution nor a refusal the routes make — an `auth.Refusal` the guard writes ITSELF about somebody it has RESOLVED (this node knows exactly who they are), each carrying the routes it leaves (`Refusal.Applies`), because the two leave different things: a person — or a bound token — whose SEAT the chart no longer holds is `403 seat_unavailable` naming the seat — 401 would loop a browser through the sign-in page for ever — everywhere but `/auth/`, whose subject is a person's own credential rather than the seat, or an offboarded person would hold a live cookie with no way to sign out; and a session that may only ENROL A SECOND FACTOR is `403 second_factor_enrolment_required` everywhere but the three guarded routes `auth.EnrolmentAdmits` names (reading who it is, the enrolment, a password step-up) and the one sign-out no guard stands in front of, because the rest of `/auth/` regenerates recovery codes and signs a person out everywhere. Where both meet on one person the enrolment refusal holds, being the narrower. The guard also marks every answer under `/auth/` `Cache-Control: no-store` before it resolves anything. THE HUMAN SURFACES ARE BUILT HERE, by `NewHumanSurfaces` — `/auth`, `/iam`, the human write surface, and the session and machine-token arms — and `HumanSurfaces.Mount` is the one hand-over into `Options`, so `crewlet run` and `internal/e2e` build them through one constructor rather than two wirings that drift; a sign-in surface handed over as a typed nil had mounted `/auth` over nothing on a node with no active company, and `Mount` hands over only one that exists. `Options.Inbox` is REQUIRED: it is the engine's `SetOnInboxMoved`, which the app turns into `inbox_changed` frames, and its `SetOnInboxGap`, which sends every watched seat one that counts nothing — and every node that serves the API applies the tracker's log, so a node that heard nothing would be a wiring mistake rather than a narrower node. `Options.Objectives` is the node's objective meter and never the rooms': every committed change the socket pushes is timed into it, an inbox movement as much as a room's, and carried by the rooms it left the inbox untimed on a node that serves none. ONE SOURCE AT A TIME: a node hears its own applier while its copy serves and a sound peer's, relayed over the broker (`engine/liverelay.go`), while it is out of service and the node stays in rotation, and every switch is a gap, because what moved during it is not replayed. `Options.Credentials` is REQUIRED for the same reason: it is the engine's `SetOnIdentityMoved`, which the app turns into `stream.Service.CredentialsMoved`, and every node that serves the API applies the identity log, so a node that heard nothing would leave every revoked session's open tab streaming the company. The stream's `SeatOf` is the published company's roster read in memory (`seatOf`, by `org.Organization.Role`), which an open socket acting as a seat is decided by when a company is published. An UNBOUND viewer — `/viewer` and the inbox and My-work callouts that read it — is a Tier A token's session or a service account, never a person (ADR-0032), and its remedy names a HUMAN seat, since a bind to any other is refused. `/health` says whether the company has its FIRST PERSON (`identity`: `ready`, `unclaimed`, or `unknown` where the identity estate cannot be read — never folded into `unclaimed`), through the optional `Identity` seam — because it is the one surface an install with nobody in it can reach, and the first person is invited or created under a Tier A token like everybody after them — onto a human seat the company declares, so on a node with no company the token's session creates the company first — and a created person makes it `ready` as their create lands, before they have spent their first password link: it says SOMEBODY EXISTS, not that somebody can sign in yet; `crewlet run` wires it on every node that serves the API, company or none, over the engine's `AnyPerson`, which PROVES a "nobody" against the identity log's end, because a node that has just joined a fleet holds empty rows until its applier catches up and read bare they told an operator to invite a founder into a company that had one; and it never moves `status`. THE NATIVE HALVES ARE READ PER REQUEST (`native.go`: `NativeSources`, `NativeOperatorHalves`; `workapi.Options.Halves`): the human write surface, the operator surface (`/operator/mcp`, `/operator/act`) and the work, page, chat and search questions are mounted on every node and answer `503 no_active_revision` until the node's first company brings the tracker, the knowledge base and chat up, and a company that keeps any of them on a vendor answers `404 no_route` from then on — they were taken ONCE when the API was wired, so a node handed its first company by apply served none of them until it was restarted. `/config`'s ORG CHART routes — `PUT`/`PATCH /config`, a revert, and the seat and unit entity routes — are `config.org.write` and `config.org.read` rather than `admin` alone: a unit's LEAD writes and reads what is inside the units they lead, and every write path (dry runs included) is ADMITTED on what it changes between the revision it replaces and the one it proposes, after the document is built and before it is validated or the identity directory is asked who holds a seat it removes (`configapi/admission.go`, over `config.DiffOrg` and `authz/orgchart`), refusing a lead's write whole with the answer `authz.Combine` gives its parts' own decisions at the route's instant (a `refused` list of only the parts refused on authority, each with its own grants; `403 step_up_required` for a write refused only for a lapsed proof; a bar on the caller naming no permission and no part), and asking `config.org.write`, which no agent's seat passes, of the whole document whenever an administrator's write changes the chart, whichever route reached it — and a read or a write of ONE seat or unit (`/config/roles/{id}`, `/config/units/{id}`) is decided where it sits FIRST, before the id is looked up or the body read, at the root for an id the revision does not hold, so one outside the caller's subtree and one that does not exist answer the same 403 and only admin learns an id is missing; the engine's own writers (`Apply`, `ApplyEntity`, `Reload`) carry no principal and are not asked. And while Tier A names the document's writers (`api.auth.company_writers`, ADR-0030) every write that CHANGES it is refused `403 config_managed` on the CREDENTIAL — admitted only through a listed Tier A token, its login in `operator_id`, or as the engine itself — after the route's verb and before the body is read, so a person's session, a machine token acting as its owner and a lead's write of their own team are each refused, while a reload and the secret store stay open to whoever the table admits: the managed check composes with admin and the subtree rather than replacing either. ONE route table, and an optional SECOND LISTENER (Tier A `api.public`, whose own `external_url` is the address vendors and sandboxes are given) that serves only the routes outside parties call — `/webhooks/`, `/otlp/` and `/mcp/`, `auth.Public`, the same set the cross-site gate leaves unjudged, so no sign-in route is ever on it — and takes them off `api.port`: both sockets serve the same chain behind one partition that answers the other side's routes 404 BEFORE the guard, so the published socket requires no operator token and a route it does not serve is absent there, as the mux's own 404, whatever credential is sent. The table is a `routeTable` that keeps every pattern mounted, because the partition is a predicate over paths and only the table says which paths exist — the listener cases walk it whole and hold the published set to a reviewed list. A node that sets `api.port` WITHOUT the `ingress` role serves the network the PROBES alone, still behind the guard — `/health` answers, the dashboard is absent and every guarded route is 401 — so a port opened for a load balancer's health check needs no `api.auth` block and serves nobody the company; on a node that runs seats it also serves the two routes its own direct boxes dial, the bridge and the telemetry receiver, behind ONE host check over both prefixes (`thisHostAlone`, `mcpbridge.FromLoopback`): a caller on this host's loopback reaches them, and any other meets their absence whatever its method — never a 405 that would say what is mounted — and while the node starts a loopback caller of either is told to come back (`Boot`) |
| `api/webhooks` | The seven inbound vendor sources — GitHub, GitLab, Jira, Confluence, Slack, Datadog and Atlassian's Forge relay — plus Slack's OAuth landing and the GitHub App return. Six verify a signature over the body; Datadog and Confluence Cloud cannot, because their providers sign nothing, so a shared token IS the authentication there and it has a length floor for that reason (`secrets.CheckSharedToken`). A route whose secret is unset has nothing to verify with and answers 503 — it does not accept the delivery |
| `runtoken` | The signed, self-describing credential a PER-RUN endpoint carries in its own URL path: the OTLP receiver's and the MCP bridge's. Minting and verifying happen on DIFFERENT NODES of a fleet, so a token has to carry its own subject and expiry rather than be a key into a map. One implementation because it was about to be two, and the two would have had to agree about five decisions nothing compares; the DOMAIN in the key derivation is what stops one endpoint's token validating at the other. A keyring that cannot sign for the fleet is REFUSED (`ErrNoKeyring`) rather than given a per-process key, which validated only where it was minted — that fallback was the posture of a node with no keyring, and there is no such node any more. The version segment is the LITERAL `crewlet_rt1`, on every token it mints (the OTLP receiver's, the MCP bridge's, an artifact view ticket's), which `internal/redact` keys on as it keys on a vendor's prefix, because no node that collects a run holds the tokens its launch minted, so what a box prints is redacted of them by their shape or not at all — the bare `v2` it was is a word prose holds, which no rule could key on; the two packages spell the literal apart, since `redact` imports nothing of the engine, and `TestAMintedTokenIsRedacted` holds them to one spelling |
| `signedask` | THE ONE RULE FOR A REQUEST ONE NODE ASKS ANOTHER TO CARRY OUT over the queue's ephemeral verbs, and its reply (ADR-0031). The broker authenticates nothing, so a message on a subject a node SERVES is whatever anybody who reaches the cluster or leaf port wrote — a forged question where the node only answers, an INSTRUCTION where it acts — and these act: the estate service (a data node writes as the actor the request names, and answers a chat read for the viewer it names), the fleet broker's membership (a member removes a voter through its own system account), a note to a running turn (the running node offers it as the person it names), a read of a run's box asked of the node whose host it is on (`sandbox.box_read`: the host reads the box back and pauses it), the box bridge's relay (`internal/httprelay`, on `mcpbridge.open` and `mcpbridge.frame`: the node holding a box's session runs its request as whoever its token names, and each frame of the answer is written to the box as that node's) and the assistant's replay and Stop (`internal/assistant/live`, on `assistant.replay` and `assistant.stop`: the node running a person's turn hands it over or stops it on the asking node's word). Each is a `Channel` over the state log's own frame (`statelog.Signer`), in two key domains of its own (`<name>.request`, `<name>.reply`) so nothing verifies across channels or as a log record; a request carries an id and a deadline INSIDE its signed body and `SealRequest` refuses one without, a serving node remembers the ids it took until their deadlines in a `Guard` of its own (one per node and channel, never shared, because a scatter hands one request to every node), and an asker believes only a reply that verifies AND answers the id it sent — `OpenReply` refuses a reply read against no question, since a refusal bound to no request is exactly what an unbound read would believe. What arrives is one of FIVE dispositions (`Admit`), each handled once by every channel: admitted; tampered (nothing believed, not even the id, logged at error); key unknown (a fact about this node — "did not run", bound to the id read UNTRUSTED from the body); undecodable; stale (unbound, past its deadline, or already taken). It exists because the estate's copy was about to become three: the broker channel and the steer wire shipped unsigned beside a signed estate, and ADR-0031 and SECURITY.md said every instruction was signed. ONE READ is a `Channel` all the same: the LIVE RELAY (`internal/engine`, `live.relay`), whose answers a node pushes to every socket it holds as a peer's appliers' work, so a forged one is words shown under somebody's name; its asks are bound like any request, because sealed one way a captured answer would be believed by any follower asking inside its validity. The scatters whose answer is only READ, back by the caller that asked (history, search slices, held memory, a sandbox tail), are not channels here. TWO frames that are no instruction are sealed here all the same, ONE WAY (`OneWay`), each with its expiry inside the signed body in place of an id and a deadline, in a key domain of its own: a presence broadcast (`presence`), which no node answers and every node displays as a person in a room, carries its room's kind and id, and a frame the fleet did not seal shows nobody; an assistant frame (`assistant.frame`), which every node following a person's turn streams to them, is encrypted under the keyring's AEAD before it is sealed, and a frame the fleet did not seal is dropped |
| `api/mcpbridge` | One box run's tool surface — a seat's executor, or the Dashboard Assistant's deep work — served to the harness in its box over MCP on HTTP — the same `toolsession.Server` a phase on this host reaches through its relay, on the route the nodes serving the API mount, because a box is another machine or namespace. The dispatch is `tools.Surface` itself — never a second idea of what is granted, what a guard refuses, or what gets recorded — so a bridged call is held to its tool's schema by the surface's `tools.Invoke`, its canonical arguments and its arguments note included (ADR-0059), with no check of the bridge's own. What it adds is what a DETACHED run's calls are owed that a surface cannot know: the SEAT'S FENCE before and after each call (the run outlives its turn, and the node may stop holding the seat); the ORDER calls arrived in, fixed at admission (`Surface.Admit`); a call under the RUN's context, stripped of its cancellation, never the HTTP request's, so a disconnect abandons only the answer; a PANIC contained and the run's tools closed to it; a run that is OVER refusing; the relist hold, so an activated tool is listed before the next request; a DURABLE call log with each call's order, timing and origin, a call refused once the run's tools closed and a suspend a box cannot honour included, each with its class (a bridged run outlives its turn and its process, so the reviewer's evidence cannot live in memory); and an IDENTITY — the per-run token IS the credential, so every call acts as the run's SEAT, set by the bridge per call OVER whatever the request carried: `/mcp/` is exempt from the guard, which therefore resolves every box as anonymous (or, on a laptop, as the `-dev-principal`), the SDK keeps the opening request's context for the whole session, and the turn's own principal defers to any answer already there — so every gated builtin refused the seat's own call as needing a credential. It is overridden HERE and nowhere else, and a session whose surface is bound to no seat is refused at `Open`. Not added: rounds, a terminator, a per-request budget — a box run's loop is its harness's own, and those are settled when it is collected. ADDED, for a person's NOTES to the run's turn (`steer.go`): the session's INBOX (`Session.Steer`, the engine's), served two ways behind the run's token alone — `steer_take`, a tool a harness's hook calls that is NEVER LISTED, answered by the MCP server's own receiving middleware so it never reaches the surface or the ledger, ALWAYS TEXT and never an error (a failed hook call makes Claude Code re-initialise the whole bridge), taken only at a boundary of the main agent's loop and within half the hook's timeout; and the notes ROUTE (`SteerPattern`, under the bridge's prefix so every gate that covers the bridge covers it), a poll HELD up to ten seconds — under Envoy's default route timeout — and released at once on a change, the run's end or `Shutdown`, and a capped read. The routing is the endpoint's own (`routed`, `heldOnly` for a relayed call, `admitRelayed` parsing the token as the path's first segment), and a fenced opening takes nothing more from its notes. The bridge decides nothing about notes: what waits, fits, is read and is recorded is the inbox's. THE TOKEN NAMES THE RUN AND ITS JOB — the launch id on the run's durable row — under the fleet's keyring, NEVER A PROCESS, so a node that restarts re-opens the job's session as it recovers the seat and the box's token resolves again, and a relaunch is a new job whose old box is refused; nothing secret is kept for that, since a job id is no credential without the signature. It names the SEAT too, and carries NO ADDRESS, because the box needs none of the holder's: `CREWLET_MCP_BRIDGE_URL` is the FLEET's address — any node serving the route, a load balancer in front of several included — and the node a call lands on reads the seat's lease and relays the whole exchange to the holder over the broker (`Join`, `internal/httprelay`), so a run whose seat moved goes on reaching its tools, and a node serving no HTTP runs agent seats as well as one that does. A `direct` box is a process of the launching host, so on every node that runs seats and binds a listener it dials the node's own listener on loopback (`Options.HostURL`, `Reaches`), DERIVED by the process that bound the listener and never configured — asking the operator for an address the node already knows is the one setting a single-node company, and a seat pool with no route in, would otherwise need before its first turn — and every other box dials the fleet's address. A node without `ingress` serves that route on api.port to its own host's loopback alone (`FromLoopback`, internal/api's `Probes`), through the same routing, so a call for a seat it has since handed on is relayed to the holder. ONLY THE HOLDER ANSWERS, and it says a job it holds no session for is OVER only once it has finished taking the seat (`SeatReady`, after the seat's runs are recovered) — before that it is not yet the holder, and a call waits for it, up to two seat-lease lifetimes, then 503. An opening whose FENCE closed defers to the lease rather than refusing, and a node that hands a seat on ends the seat's sessions (`ReleaseSeat`), which would otherwise pin its surface and its children's credentials in a process that no longer speaks for it. ONE MCP HANDLER PER PROCESS serves a box's own request and a relayed one, because the SDK keeps each MCP session in its handler's table. A session is decided as its `Caller`: a seat's executor as its seat (the default, byte-identical on the wire), or the Dashboard Assistant's deep work as nobody, because the turn's own surface carries its grant (ADR-0054), with a token capped to the run (`Session.TTL`) and admitted from loopback alone for a run confined on the host. A SESSION WITH A CALLER OF ITS OWN IS PINNED to the process that opened it, because it has no seat to move and its opener's turn watches it to its end: its token names that incarnation in place of a seat, a call that lands elsewhere is relayed straight there, and a loopback session is never relayed at all. THE HOST A CALL NAMES IS JUDGED FOR A SESSION HELD TO LOOPBACK ALONE: the token in the path is the route's credential, which a page cannot make, so the SDK's DNS-rebinding guard is off and nothing stands in for it on a seat's session — it refused the hop an executor's call takes through a reverse proxy or tunnel on the engine host that reaches the node on 127.0.0.1 and passes the public `Host` on (Caddy, cloudflared, ngrok as they come), and never held a seat's token to an address anyway. A session held to loopback (`Session.Loopback`) admits a call only from loopback under a loopback name or a container runtime's name for the engine host (`loopbackRefusal`: `sandbox.LoopbackHost`, `sandbox.EngineHostName`, the name Docker Desktop and Podman's machine deliver a container box's call under), refusing any other with the route's own 401, so a page on the host under a rebound name cannot spend a held run's leaked token; judged in the routing the MCP endpoint and the notes route share (`routed`), and never on a relayed call, since a held session is never relayed. `LoopbackBase`, whether a run can be held at all, reads loopback as `sandbox.LoopbackHost` does, the predicate that hands a container box the runtime's name in the base's place, so a base the rehosting moves is always one the hold covers |
| `mcprelay` | `crewlet mcp-relay <socket>`: the stdio end of a seat's tool session — the one MCP server a harness starts, which copies its stdin to the engine's per-run unix socket and the socket back to its stdout. A RELAY because the seat's tools are objects in the engine's process and a harness reaches an MCP server only as a command it starts or a URL, and a URL needs a listener a node may bind none of. IT READS ITS ONE ARGUMENT AND NOTHING ELSE — no environment (each harness decides its servers' environment, and several pass only HOME, PATH and TMPDIR), no logging (stdout is the protocol stream) — and is dispatched on ARGV, in the binary's switch and through `DispatchIfRelay` in a test binary that stands in for the engine, because a dispatch keyed on a variable falls through to the suite the first time a harness strips it. It ends when the engine closes the socket, and HALF-closes when the harness closes stdin so the answer to a call already sent still arrives |
| `toolsession` | One phase's tools served to one harness run over MCP: the engine's end of `crewlet mcp-relay`. THE TRANSPORT ONLY — an MCP server kept in step with what a `Dispatcher` offers, and a unix socket per harness run; every decision about a call (grant, skill guard, fence, budget, round cap, terminator, record) is the dispatcher's, which is the tool loop, so there is no second idea of what is granted to drift on the security half. ONE SOCKET PER HARNESS RUN, retired when the run ends and only after the calls running on it return, so a relay a killed harness left behind finds nothing rather than the next run's calls. The socket carries NO TOKEN: it sits in a directory this process made with `os.MkdirTemp` and then CHECKED (a 0700 directory, this user's, not a link), which separates the engine's user from other users and does not separate seats — each harness's tool confinement does that. A temp root too deep for a socket path (104 bytes on darwin, 108 on linux) is refused at the start naming TMPDIR. A connected harness is one that LISTED the tools, not one that sent `notifications/initialized`, which a client on a newer protocol revision never sends. Binds no network port, so a node with `api.port: 0` keeps its seats' tools For a client that re-reads the list when told it changed — every harness whose profile re-lists, and every box — the answer to a call that changed it is HELD until the client has listed again, up to two seconds (`HoldAnswersForRelist`): answered at once, Gemini CLI, Copilot and OpenCode asked their model again before the re-list landed and offered an activated tool one request late. A dispatcher is handed a call WITHOUT the MCP request's context: a call is the run's, run under the context of the run the dispatcher serves, so a harness that cancels or drops it cancels nothing a tool is half-way through — and the request's is not on offer to be used by mistake. |
| `toolsession/toolsessiontest` | The ONE contract suite every `toolsession.Dispatcher` runs (`queuetest`'s idiom): the tool loop's, which serves a harness on this host, and the bridge's `binding`, which serves one in a box — the same call made from two places, so the gates it passes are written once and each package runs them from its own test against its own dispatcher. The shared gates only: the seat's fence before and after a call; a panic answered as a failure that ends the run; a call under the run's context, never the request's, whether the client cancels or hangs up; arguments that did not parse answered with the loop's own words; a call after the run ended refused, at the session and at the dispatcher; each call's place in the call log fixed as it arrives; an activation answered once the client has re-listed; a suspend the run cannot resume refused, and recorded as the call that ran; and the class every refusal carries on the dispatcher's record. A gate one of them lacks BY DESIGN stays in its package (the loop's round cap, terminator barrier and steer pause; the bridge's token, route and durable ledger). THROUGH A REAL SESSION: the suite connects an MCP client as a harness does — over the socket, over the bridge URL — and hangs up by cutting the transport the factory hands it, never the client's polite close, which waits for the calls in flight. Because the loop WITHHOLDS every answer from a harness it is stopping while the bridge answers at once, a case never waits on the answer to a call made once a run's end was decided: it reads the dispatcher's record and what the tool did |
| `whsec` | The Standard Webhooks signing-secret format, and the one place that decides what a valid one is: `whsec_` plus padded base64 over a 32-byte key, with the DECODED bytes as the HMAC key. Three places need the rule and none may disagree — written twice it drifted, and a `${VAR}` holding a 16-byte key was refused as a literal and silently accepted as a reference |
| `atlassianhost` | Which Atlassian deployment a Jira or Confluence address is on, decided once (`Of`): a host of one of Atlassian's two multi-tenant Clouds is Cloud — a SITE under `atlassian.net` or `jira.com`, or Government Cloud's `atlassian-us-gov-mod.net` (Confluence under `/wiki`), or a GATEWAY a cloud id is read through, `api.atlassian.com` or `api.atlassian-us-gov-mod.com` — and EVERY OTHER HOST IS WHAT THE COMPANY DECLARED in `integrations.atlassian.deployment`, Data Center when it declared nothing. The declaration is not a fallback for a guess: Atlassian Isolated Cloud gives each customer a base domain of its own that no host list can name, so only the company can say its unrecognised host is Cloud — and a recognised host is never overruled, which validation enforces by refusing Data Center beside it. A url whose HOST is a `${VAR}` is likewise the declaration's, and unknown without one; a reference in the path leaves the host judged. The gateway a cloud id is read through follows the SITE beside it (`Gateway`), because a cloud id names no environment and the commercial gateway does not serve a Government site. Every source an Atlassian document or a measurement, named in its package doc. Three places need the rule and none may disagree: config validation (a Data Center url must carry a signing secret), the Jira client (REST v3 or v2) and the Confluence client (`/wiki` or not), each handed the same declaration — a guard refuses a client construction that omits it. Written three times it drifted: Jira counted every `atlassian.com` host as Cloud while the other two did not, so `jira.atlassian.com` — a self-hosted Jira — was Cloud to the client that read it and Data Center to the validator that admitted it |
| `api/authapi` | `/auth`: how a person BECOMES a principal. Its routes sit over machinery that already existed — argon2id, TOTP, recovery codes, the throttle, the session signer, the domain's writer — and what it owns is the HTTP shape of the sequence and the REFUSALS. A SIGN-IN SURFACE MUST NOT BE A ROSTER: every failed sign-in answers one code, whatever went wrong, after the same argon2id derivation — a miss is verified against the hasher's dummy verifier, because otherwise the ABSENCE of that cost is the answer; every verification, decoy and new password's hash waits for a slot of the verify cap, and one whose request went away while it waited is `503` and counts as no attempt. Neither half works alone — a distinguishing code makes the equal work pointless and a missing derivation makes the code pointless — and what they leave unequal, the directory read before the derivation, is stated rather than padded. The named exceptions each disclose nothing the caller did not have: the throttle is keyed on what was TYPED from its source, never on the source alone and never on what it resolved to, and its `429` carries the wait as a `Retry-After`, a second-factor prompt is reached only past the first, and an invitation's refusal is read by somebody holding the link. FIVE ENTRIES ARE EXEMPT FROM THE GUARD and the list is EXACT rather than a `/auth/` prefix, because the same surface ends every session a person holds, ends other sessions by name and enrols second factors — the posture read and the sign-in, through which a credential is OBTAINED, the invitation pair and the password reset pair (one prefix each), whose link IS one, and the sign-out of THIS session, which clears the cookie whatever the node can read (guarded, a node that could not read its identity estate answered it 503 before it ran, and a shared machine stayed signed in) and verifies every bearer it ends itself — and the exemption and the registration are one set of constants and a gate walks the mux in both directions. A GET on an invitation RENDERS and never spends (a mail client prefetching, a scanner, a preview card are all GETs). THE LINK IS THE DASHBOARD'S SCREEN, `/dashboard#/invite/<id>.<secret>` — it was the JSON route, which a person clicking it saw as a JSON document — and the secret after the id travels BESIDE it and never in a URL: the view's `X-Crewlet-Invite-Secret` header and the redemption's body. A secret that is not the link's is the same `410`, in the same bytes and counted the same, as an id nobody issued, because told apart it says which ids exist — while a link that PROVED ITSELF and is spent is the same `410` and NOT a failed attempt, because that is its holder or a mail scanner re-reading it, and counted it named the scanner's address as a guesser. A link meets NO CURVE (`Service.uncounted`) — it names nobody, so the only key it had was the address, which a stranger there held shut for everybody, and its secret is 256 bits nobody without the company's key can compute (`iamdomain.Blinder.InvitationSecret`). A REDEMPTION CAN BE RETRIED until it lands: it is ONE record (`iamdomain.Writer.Redeem`) — the person, their address, their login, the human seat the invitation holds and the spent link — so an attempt refused for a login somebody holds writes nothing and the redeemer chooses another; what keeps a link single-use is the link (redeemed, expired, or an address somebody is ENROLLED under answer 410). The view ALWAYS carries that `seat` — part of what the person agrees to — and a seat the chart has since retired or made an agent's is the link's own `410`, never a `400 seat_required` asking the invitee for a field the form does not have; a seat a PERSON was bound to since is `409`. Ending a NAMED session reads its owner from this node's rows, since a lineage is not a secret, and asks the AUTHORITY TABLE about that owner — `iam.session.end`, the verb `DELETE /iam/people/{id}/sessions` is mounted on, with the caller's own subject named by the id the self arm compares — because it decided by a check of its own that admitted a deployment grant a machine token could carry, for anybody's session with no step-up; the logout's own lineage comes from a bearer whose SIGNATURE verified, and ONLY A SESSION THE ROWS STILL HOLD IS ENDED: a sign-out, named or not, of a session already over (a deadline, a revocation, a record that ended it) clears the cookie and writes and announces nothing, because read under the signature alone every cookie ever signed wrote a close and another `iam_session_ended` per post. A SECOND FACTOR IS DECIDED ON THE PERSON'S OWN CURVE before its code is checked, as well as on the pair's — every address's wrong codes climbing one curve, a wait past five seconds `429`, the wrong code that takes it to its ceiling announced as `iam_second_factor_throttled`, once per climb because the curve reports the transition — and it is SPENT BY THE SIGN-IN IT COMPLETES, decided in the snapshot that checked it: an app code records its step and a recovery code is removed, either accepted wherever the person holds it, and a spend this node cannot record is a 503 rather than a session on a code that would still work — both were missing, so a recovery code could never be used by anybody holding an app and neither was ever spent. A REQUIRED SECOND FACTOR IS ENROLLED BEFORE ANYTHING ELSE: a sign-in this surface verified — the password route, a password step-up, a redemption — that proved a password and no second factor, where `api.auth.totp` is `required` (its default), opens a session marked ENROLMENT-ONLY in TWO places, both read — its signed BEARER (`session.Bearer.EnrolmentOnly`, the cookie's optional scope, because a node that has not applied the session serves reads on the bearer alone and every sign-in answers before any node applies it: marked on the row only, a password reached every read for that latency on every node) and its own start record (`iamdomain.Session.EnrolmentOnly`) — and answers `status: second_factor_enrolment_required`; the guard refuses that session every guarded route but the three `auth.EnrolmentAdmits` names — the sign-out of this session is unguarded and reached besides — and completing `POST /auth/totp` through it REPLACES it with a whole session as a step-up does, keeping the restricted session's PROOF INSTANT (the enrolment's code proves a seed that session was just handed, not who holds it, and dated at the enrolment it handed the restricted cookie a fresh step-up window) — the setting was validated and read by nothing, so a password alone reached everything. A model's removal (`DELETE /config/llm-providers/{id}`) and the repoint of every written reference to it (`config.Company.ProviderReferences`), each addressed by a seat's handle and job or the assistant's chat or deep work, never by an index, land as one revision; a reference left unanswered is `422` listing every one, and a job that named nothing and moves with the company's fallback is a `resolution_moved` warning, from `phase.ResolveWithSource` asked of both documents. A server's removal (`DELETE /config/mcp-servers/{id}`, mcpremoval.go) with `clear` takes every field that names it alone (`config.Company.RemoveMCPServer`, over the one walk `MCPServerReferences`) in the same revision, and without it is `422` naming each; it may delete the stored values the removal leaves nothing reading (`config.Company.VariablesRead`, the answer `/secrets` gives), asking `secrets.write` on top of `config.write` before anything is read, AFTER the revision is written and each asked again of the fleet's revision just before it goes, through `Options.Secrets` (the fleet's credential store, which `New` requires), answering only names and an outcome per value. And a secret write asks `Reads` (the paths of the fleet's revision that read each name) and `Reload` of this surface, answering `Reloaded` (ADR-0070) |
| `api/setupapi` | `/setup`: connecting an integration from the dashboard rather than a shell. It performs no write of its own — a credential goes through the store `/secrets` serves and a pointer through the merge, validation and compare-and-set `PATCH /config` performs — so there is one write path onto the company document and one onto the sealed store. Every route is `admin`'s, reads included (`setup.read`), because the list of which credentials a company has NOT configured is a map of what to attack; CONNECTING is `setup.connect` and asks a recent proof, and wherever a credential is sealed — a submission carrying one (which only the body can say), a provisioning pass, a GitHub App — it asks `secrets.write` too, through the same guard, so a credential is written here on exactly the terms `/secrets` writes one and a step-up the store's own row asks for cannot be skipped by choosing this door. A GitHub App's return carries no credential of ours, so the state the begin route mints is SEALED under the fleet keyring and names who began the creation — the key, the webhook secret and the seat's revision are written as that person, where they used to name `setup`, which is nobody — and sealed rather than signed because it travels through GitHub, a browser history and every ingress log. A write that changes the company document asks the MANAGED document's rule too (`Authorize`, ADR-0030) — at the begin, at a disconnect, and in the App's completion BEFORE the code is exchanged — so a credential the document does not name is refused before anything is sealed, queued or created at the vendor. IT DESCRIBES THE FLEET'S COMPANY, READ ONCE PER REQUEST (`configapi.Service.FleetCompany`, ADR-0057): the document, its org and the revision it is, from the pointer, never this node's own copy, and a submission without `if_match` expects that revision, because what each field holds decides the write — an empty slot gets a fresh `${VAR}`, a literal is refused — and built on whatever the pointer named when it landed, a write in between had it decide on one document and patch another. Every refusal after a credential is sealed names it (`wrote_secrets`), the unknown outcome and the 503 included, a rotation's failed reload and a later credential's failed seal too (`setup.Result` carries them on every return after the first seal). A ROTATION'S RELOAD IS HELD TO THAT REVISION TOO, to its compare-and-set: reloading whatever the pointer named by then answered 201 for a rotation the running document never reads. A SEAL THE STORE DID NOT ANSWER IS ITS OWN ANSWER (`setup.SealError`): the secret store is a coordination write and fails with the error a pointer read does, so it is the retryable 503 naming the credential whose write may have landed as a field, `unanswered_secret`, beside `wrote_secrets`, never "the fleet's activation could not be read". It carries no `outcome`, because its remedy is the plain 503's — the document was not changed, and the submission sent again writes every credential again by name — and a field, not only the `reason`, because a client branches on fields and read a 503 without `outcome` as one that wrote nothing. A forced disconnect's `If-Match` is its BASE, and a stale one is `412 revision_advanced` (marked as stated, `configapi.ApplyRequest.Stated`, the only setup write that reads the header); a connect's body `if_match` is still `409`. Its reads and writes answer a condition of the node the same way, through `operator.AnswerCondition`, naming what a submission sealed before it (`wrote_secrets`) beside the sentence. THE GITHUB APP RETURN TELLS THE UNKNOWN OUTCOME TOO (`AppFailure`, `AppFlow.unconfirmed`): a seat write whose activation went unanswered is read back once, answered as the creation finished where the seat records the app, and otherwise told that whether the app was added isn't known yet, under a heading of its own, with a refresh of the spent link as the answer (`AppFlow.usedLink`). Told as a write that recorded nothing, "couldn't add the app, delete it on GitHub", it sent a person to delete the app the agent was running EVERY ACCESS SYNC ENDS IN THE CHECK (`AccessSync.Then`, `Service.recheck`): a save, an action at the vendor and a keep each start a sync that answers only for its seats, so each carries the check route's own read-only pass, run through the same held pass (`runHeld`) and recorded through the loop's fold while the integration still reads as syncing; without it the integration's issues, notes and card stayed as the last pass left them until somebody pressed Check now |

| `api/operator` | Worklet, Doclet and Chatlet as ONE OPERATOR CATALOGUE, served to the people who run it, because the premise of running this engine is that an AI manages your company, and the person doing that is usually working through one too. THREE TRANSPORTS reach a tool through its ONE DISPATCH (`Server.Dispatch`) and nothing else — MCP at `/operator/mcp` (`mcp.go`) for an operator's own assistant, the ACT route at `POST /operator/act/{tool}` (`act.go`) for a person at the dashboard, and the human write surface's tool-backed routes (`internal/api/workapi`) for a script or the CLI — because a transport that assembled its own tool set would have a second chance to wire it differently, and a verb one surface offers and another does not looks complete from inside each. None of them decides a schema, a hint, a refusal's wording, its status (`Fail`, `refusal.go` — the ONE mapping from a result's CAUSE, never its sentence, to a status and the engine's envelope, with exactly ONE refinement, `FailIfMatched`, for a route whose version arrived in an `If-Match` header: a stale version there is `412 version_moved`, decided inside the stale-version class arm alone so every earlier arm is `Fail`'s byte for byte) or what is recorded about the call.
