A templated page is allowed its inline blocks by their sha256 hashes taken from the template RENDERED (`ForTemplate`), never from its source, because html/template strips comments inside a block and a hash of the source refuses the page's own script. HSTS follows the address the LISTENER is published at — `api.external_url` for api.port, `api.public.external_url` for the public listener and the bridge-only one — and never the bind address or `r.TLS`, since the engine sits behind a TLS-terminating proxy; one flag for both sockets sent none on the landings a deployment had published over https beside a private http admin side: a YEAR, with no `includeSubDomains` and no `preload`, because this engine is one host and has no business committing its neighbours under the same domain to https. The ARTIFACT ORIGIN's policies live here too, so every policy the engine writes reads side by side: `ArtifactDocument` (a nonce fresh to the response with `'strict-dynamic'`, published handlers by digest under `'unsafe-hashes'`, the artifact's own origin as the only place it connects to or loads images, media, fonts and styles from (scripts are trusted by nonce, and a script the page's own script creates loads from any host, so the policy bounds what a viewer's row can run and not what the page's author can send elsewhere; the package comment states that residual), object, base, form and frame sinks `'none'`, the frame's own sandbox, every device feature denied, no COEP ever, CORP by origin mode), `ArtifactSubresource` and `ArtifactPublic`; and `DashboardFor` is the dashboard's policy with a `frame-src` naming that origin. So is the ONE gzip negotiation (`AcceptsGzip`, `GzipTag`, `GzipSmaller`) the dashboard's assets and the artifact origin both answer by |
| `artifactupload` | How bytes become a version, and THE publish pipeline every surface calls. BYTES ARE RESERVED BEFORE THEY MOVE: an intent decides paths, types, limits, carries and quota first, mints one key per new file at the engine's clock and holds the bytes in the fleet's reservation ledger (`coord.UploadReservations`, one compare-and-set record, per principal and four times that per company) until the collector could have deleted them, so unnamed bytes never exceed the holds; the pipeline's own objects (a linted page, an edit's result, a copied CDN file, inline content, a copy of the adopted brand's logo or face) are reserved the same way; a brand kit's logos are stored sanitised (`brandformat.Sanitize`), and an ADOPTION copies every file the kit serves, re-sanitised, beside the stylesheet generated once, under one reservation, after refusing an unreadable text colour before a byte moves. FINALIZE READS BACK BEFORE IT DECIDES, on the engine-streamed leg (a put once under a claimed key) and on a presigned S3 PUT bound to exact length, type and digest alike, and holds every file to its type's leading bytes. Then edits against the base's source, the linter, vendoring, search text and the runtime contract, and the domain's decide. A REFUSED PUBLISH LEAVES NOTHING BEHIND (its own objects deleted, the upload open again); a landed one deletes what nothing names; an agent's turn is held to the D28 guardrails before a byte moves |
| `artifactupload/edits`, `artifactupload/lint`, `artifactupload/vendor` | Pure exactly-once `old_str`/`new_str` edits against the base version, overlapping matches counted; the publish-time policy linter (allowlisted script and stylesheet hosts at exact versions, the nonce placeholder written into every script element and the rewritten page verified by a second tokenize, inline handler digests, eval flags, remote module imports refused, every import map entry on another origin copied in or refused, warnings); and allowlisted CDN files fetched once per node over https through `httpx.Guarded`, integrity-checked, then copied into the artifact's own version, so a view fetches nothing third-party and no object is shared across artifacts |
| `artifactserve` | The artifact ORIGIN, the `api.artifacts` listener, and the frame builder the API mints a viewer's frame through: five routes (a ticket's file, its artifact's asset, the embedded runtime and kit, and the adopted brand's files by hash, served only while an adoption names them) and nothing else, no guard, no session, no cookie read or written (a source walk holds the package to it), no API route, ever. A path-borne view ticket names one principal, one immutable version, the CREDENTIAL it was minted through and the artifact's access epoch; in either origin mode the request's host must be the one the ticket's artifact is served on, and a top-level open is refused. DOCUMENTS ARE DECIDED ON EVERY REQUEST and never read a remembered verdict: the principal re-composed from the identity rows by `api/auth`'s `Lineages` (the guard's own arms, over the credential's NAME), the artifact read through the estate router bounded by the stall grace, `get_artifact` asked again; a subresource rides its document's verdict at most thirty seconds, forgotten the moment this node's applier reports the artifact moved. Anything that cannot be vouched for (a stale or unreachable copy, an identity copy past the grace, a copy older than the ticket, an undecided chart) is a 503 with `Retry-After`, never a page. A real origin only on a site of its own (config's site rule); the version's pinned runtime placed before every script of each HTML page (past its doctype, a bounded scan and never a parse), and the brand stylesheet the version pinned linked after it unless the page's `<html>` opts out; a strict script policy by a fresh nonce substituted per response into the linter's placeholder; every byte read through the object store's checking reader, ranges by length; documents and small gzips kept per node by digest; every write at the object store's pace. `artifactserve/ticket` mints and opens the tickets |
| `presence` | Who is in each room and where. A ROOM IS ITS KIND AND ITS ID (`Room`: an artifact's room `RoomArtifact`, a chat room's `RoomChat`, ADR-0038), and the vocabulary — `Member`, `Activity`, a `Focus` checked per kind, `Roster`, `Counts` and every timing below — is this package's rather than the artifacts', because what a kind owns is only where its members can look and who may be in its rooms. TOLD, NEVER STORED: rosters in the memory of the node holding the sockets, crossed between nodes on the queue's `Broadcast`/`Listen` (no row, no log record, no coordination key; the hub's broker seam has no durable verb at all) under `crewlet.rooms.<kind>.<id>` (never `crewlet.presence.`, the hub's metric namespace), every frame sealed ONE WAY under the keyring (`signedask.OneWay`, key domain `presence`, the room and a 60-second expiry inside the seal) because it names a person, so a frame the fleet did not seal shows nobody. A node listens on a room only while one of its SOCKETS is in it (one reconciler goroutine listens and withdraws), fans each room out to its sockets at most ten times a second and tells the fleet its own members' moves at most twenty times a second (one short window on each side, so a cursor crosses nodes within 150 ms), re-presents at most ten members a frame (the change waiting longest first, so nobody starves and a socket's bytes stay bounded), rebroadcasts its members in full every 15 s (a peer replaces what it held from that node, so a lost leave costs one rebroadcast), expires a peer's member unheard for 45 s, asks its peers once when it gains a room's first socket and applies each answer as it arrives (`AskEach`), and tells the fleet its members left when it stops. Large rooms present the fifty most recently active and count the rest by kind, back to everybody only below forty. A room's fan-outs reach the sinks ONE AT A TIME IN THE ORDER THEY WERE TAKEN (`delivering`, the one lock held across a sink, taken before the hub's own), and a joiner is handed the room AS THIS NODE'S SOCKETS WERE LAST SHOWN IT, itself beside them (`rosterFor`), because each fan-out is the difference from that baseline: handed the room as it presented at the join, a member who came and went between two fan-outs stayed on the joiner's screen for good, and two fan-outs handed over out of order left a member who had gone. And it is handed IN THAT SAME ORDER: `Join` takes a `Hand` and runs it under `delivering`, the hand placing the socket where the surface's own sink finds it and sending it the roster (a hand that declines undoes the join, `ErrDeclined`), so no fan-out taken after a join reaches the socket before its roster and none taken before it is lost — a roster answered as a return value was sent whenever its caller got to it, and an artifact room's joiner was put back to an older room by the answer to its own join; a caller never joins holding a lock its hand or its sink takes. An agent's tool call TOUCHES the room (present as an agent, viewing or editing, for 45 s after its last call) and never makes its node listen; in a CHAT room an agent holds one member per thread it answers in (`Focus.Thread`), naming its turn and the phrase the room shows (`Agent.Status`) — the working indicator IS that member — and is taken out at once by `Withdraw` when the turn lets go rather than lingering. A chat room's typing member LAPSES: editing is put back to viewing `TypingLapse` (6 s) after nothing re-asserted it, because a lid closed mid-word must not leave somebody typing for 45 s, while an artifact's editing member stands until it changes. Who may be in a room is the socket's decision, never the hub's |
| `provenance` | The ONE fence around text a model reads that somebody else wrote (page bodies, work comments, project files, chat messages and a room's own topic and purpose, artifact files and rows, the adopted brand's voice, every wake's body — a change's note, a monitor's alert, an unprompted integration's notification among them — and a colleague's ask and answer, each a closed `Source` the agent runtime's page is held to in both directions): a per-turn random boundary (minted per turn segment and carried on `turnctx.Turn`), an author label and a trust level (`company`, for what an administrator adopted for everybody, is never inferred from authors and governs tone and wording only) — a fence's three facts as ONE VALUE (`Label`), so a text's label travels with it (onto a turn's record of what it was asked, a past turn's row, a search hit between nodes) and is fenced again by whatever reads it later rather than decided again from what that reader cannot see, `Merge` the one rule for several labels over one text (the least trust, every writer once, the shared surface or `notification`) and `Present` how such a text reaches a model (fenced under its label, or neutralised as the reader's own) — the envelope a whole read answer carries, `Unfence` for a write that sends the fence back, and the one standing sentence the seat prompt carries. Content cannot close a fence: the boundary is removed from it and every marker-shaped `[` is escaped. `agent/builtin`'s read tools answer through it (`fence.go`), the chat tools' reads included (every message, thread and search snippet under its author, a room's topic and purpose as `chat_room`) and the tracker's listings (`workfence.go`: every change's excerpt — the feed's, a history's, an inbox notice's — under who made the change by `worklet.ExcerptSource`, an ask under its asker, and every title and name neutralised and read as a colleague's nobody named, so an answer carrying one is never the reader's own), which also defuses every other string in the answer and writes it as every tool answer is written, `<`, `>` and `&` as themselves (`plainjson`); the writes a fenced read feeds take their text through `unfence`, the three chat posting tools among them. A FENCE IS A MODEL'S: every answer `fence.go` labels carries the same value as it was stored beside it (`mcp.Result.PersonReceipt`), and `builtin.ForPerson` answers a person's surface (the act route, the human write surface) from that, because a receipt echoing a room's topic or a page's title reached a person between a fence's two lines, and an escape does not come off the labelled answer. A leaf. EVERY MODEL CALL IS ACCOUNTED FOR: a walk of the tree finds each function, or package-level variable, that builds a model's input, an `llm.Request` or the launch of a harness's run (the runner's `AgentRunRequest`, the sandbox's `LaunchRequest`, `Continuation` and `RunRequest`, the assistant's `DeepRun`: every seat's executor is such a run), and refuses one that names neither the tests holding its fences nor why it reads nobody's words (`modelCalls`, two-sided). ONE TRUST RULE FOR A SURFACE THE ENGINE HOSTS (`TrustForHosted`, `LabelForHosted`) — its tracker, knowledge base, chat, artifacts, project files and agent-to-agent channels — whichever door the text comes through: named in full, what its writers earn; short of that never `own`, and a colleague's with nobody named, since only company principals write there, a writer a record carries no handle and no kind for being one it could not name rather than an outside party; a vendor's surface takes `TrustFor`, where nobody named is `external`. It is one rule because a Doclet page read `external` through its wake and `colleague` as a search hit, and every door that decides a trust — a rule called, a label written with a trust, a level used as a value — is found by a walk and held to the rule its surface takes, the adopted brand's voice (the one hosted text an administrator vouches for) to `company` alone and a page script's report of what it applied (which the engine cannot verify) to `page` alone (`trustDoors`, two-sided). WHICH FIELDS ARE SOMEBODY'S WORDS IS DECLARED ON THE FIELD (`FieldTag`: `untrusted:"fence"` for words at length, `untrusted:"name"` for a title, a name, a label), for the reason a credential's `secret:"true"` is — whoever adds a field declares it, where a list is kept by whoever remembers it — and a walk holds every tag in the tree to the two kinds and to a field that holds text |
| `trashreap` | The trash reaper duty: purges what has been trashed past its term, as the system, for every domain that keeps a trash (the tracker's removed work items first, then pages, then artifacts), under derived operation ids so a moved duty never purges twice |
| `objectives` | Service objectives as named constants with their reasons (publish success and latency, served first byte, browser-reported ticket-to-ready, datastore round trip and resolution, change visibility, wake latency, best-effort presence), each an EVENT objective over thirty days whose good event is answered without fault within its bound (`Objective.Good`), so an alarm borrows its threshold rather than inventing one (ADR-0015). The `Meter` every recording site calls (the publish pipeline, the artifact origin, the datastore writer, the socket's push of a committed artifact, datastore or chat change, the change feed's every published wake, and the presence hub's joins and peers' moves) classifies once and counts twice from that one call: `crewlet.objectives.events{objective, outcome}` into the engine's recorder, and a minute ledger over six hours the multi-window burn rates are read from (1 h and 5 m at 14.4, 6 h and 30 m at 6, at least two bad events), which `objective_burn` raises. An event stamped by ANOTHER clock — the broker's instant for a stored record, a peer's for a member's move — is recorded through `RecordSince`, which ends it on the meter's own clock and CLAMPS a negative span to zero, since a start a little after the end is the clocks' skew rather than a fault. The `Beacon` admits the dashboard's ready reports (`POST /artifacts/ready`): one per frame, never longer than the frame has existed, a sustained one a second per credential |
| `api/artifactsapi`, `api/artifactdataapi` | The HTTP surfaces for artifacts and for their datastores on the API listener, every route stating its authority where it is mounted. A caller with no relation is answered exactly as an unknown id. Writes carry an Idempotency-Key (datastore writes too, never socket frames); a publish names its base version and a concurrent one is refused with the live version to merge onto. THE API ORIGIN NEVER RENDERS AN ARTIFACT: raw bytes here are attachments, a doc's source is JSON, and a page is opened only as a frame on the artifact origin. ONE ADMISSION (`artifactsapi.Gate`) for the artifacts' and the datastore's routes and read questions alike: it reads the head, decides the verb on its owner and shares before anything else is read, stamps the exact role into the write, and records an elevated read before the admin path's read is answered, refusing it when the record cannot be written. A TRASHED artifact's content is `artifact_trashed` — `409` on every route, the same code on every question (`queries.ErrArtifactTrashed`) and on a room's join and withdrawal — to a reader it is disclosed to, and `not_found` only to one with no relation, the admission deciding before the trash is looked at so nothing about existence leaks; a PURGED one stays `not_found` on the questions and `410 data_gone` on the routes, because a purge is permanent and the trash is a state a restore ends |
| `api/datasetsapi` | A `dashboard` artifact's datasets, answered AS THEIR VIEWER (`adr/0043`): an engine dataset is the very question the dashboard's own screens ask, through the registry, on the viewer's own request (the agents' status is `GET /agents`' own decision), projected into the catalogue's columns; a store dataset reads under the collection's read rule; a calculation runs over inputs read the same way. A source the viewer may not read is a refusal of that dataset naming the grants that open it. A binding may name only a question decided on grants alone, refused at wiring otherwise, because answers are cached 30 s under a key of everything about the principal such a question reads. Live reads are charged to `costlimit` per viewer and per artifact |
| `artifactspike` | Test-only: the artifacts capacity spikes (datastore rate and footprint, sandbox streaming, the linter and strict policy over a committed page corpus, edits, presence fan-out, the object store under a publish) as reproducible benchmarks over the shipped components, whose figures `docs/reference/artifact-capacity.md` records and the limits and objectives are checked against. Benchmarks gate nothing; the corpus records are tests. Forms clusters, so it runs alone |
| `setup` | What an integration NEEDS before it works, in one vocabulary every vendor and the dashboard share. A vendor declares a list of requirements — where each value lives in the config, what it is called at the vendor, whether it is a credential, whether the engine can mint it, and which reconcile FINDING satisfying it clears — and the screen renders a form from that without knowing any vendor. It also owns the write ORDER, which is the whole of it: the credential is sealed first, then the config gets a `${VAR}` pointing at it, then the epoch advances. Pointer-first leaves a route refusing every delivery; value-only reaches no running seat at all, because a `${VAR}` resolves from a snapshot taken at apply time. A rotation, which changes no pointer, ends in a reload HELD TO THE REVISION THE SUBMISSION READ (`Config.Reload`'s `expect`), as the patch is, because a reload of a document a write in between changed reaches nothing; and every return after the first seal carries the names sealed (`Result.Secrets`), a failed reload's and a later failed seal's (`SealError`, its own error because the secret store fails with the coordination store's ErrUnavailable and is not a pointer read) included — which holds by construction, because everything that can refuse without a write, the patch's or the seat's encoding among it, runs before the first seal. It also runs a vendor's PASS — the same Reconcile the loop runs, with the same sink and the same public base, because CONNECTING IS THE PERMISSION and a timer running what a person already asked for is not a second decision. What it adds is `Hold`: the ONE guard every writer at a surface takes, an in-process claim AND the surface's fleet lease, because either alone leaves the collision reachable — a lease claim by an owner that already holds it doubles as a renew, so two goroutines in one process are both told yes. It also holds a credential's CHECK before it is stored (`verify.go`): a vendor's `Probe` is one read-only call that answers a `Verdict` (`works`, `refused`, `unreachable` or `unchecked`, a named string type with `Valid`) and writes nothing, served at `POST /setup/integrations/{kind}/verify` within `VerifyBudget`, and a requirement says whether it is `Checkable` or, as a webhook secret a vendor only signs with, how the vendor proves it (`CheckNote`). Every probe runs ONE suite, `setup/setuptest`, in the `queuetest` idiom, with a meta-test that hands it vendors which lie, because a probe that answered `works` for a refused key is the one failure a person cannot see |
| `jsprovision` | The ONE place that decides how long a replicated JetStream create gets, how often a forming cluster is re-asked, and what "still forming" looks like on the wire. Both subsystems that provision replicated objects at boot are the same call underneath — `queue/jetstream` makes the streams and consumers, `coord/kv` makes the buckets, and a bucket IS a stream — and written twice the rule drifted exactly as `textcut`'s and `whsec`'s did: five decisions spelled separately, each doc comment asserting it matched the other with nothing enforcing it. The budget BRANCHES on clustered, which is what the flat thirty seconds could not: a solo create is local file-store setup and a clustered one is a raft round trip against peers that are themselves booting, and the flat number was measured failing a fleet's boot on a different object every time — which is what says the object was never the problem. It also bounds the SEQUENCE, because a bring-up is many creates in a row and a per-create budget bounds only the term: without a ceiling the real limit was the product, which nobody had declared and raising the term would have multiplied. And it is where a stalled create gets its one BREADCRUMB — the step was otherwise silent, so a node that hung emitted nothing between the line before it and the failure a budget later, and the log could not say which of fifteen objects it was on. Its `Settle` owns the READ-BACK window rather than taking one, which is the only arrangement where both halves are bounded — a window the caller built separately was still running while a lookup blocked past it — and it is handed the context that bounds the BOOT, never the per-create one: two of these read-backs exist precisely BECAUSE a create's deadline expired, so a loop gated on that deadline was dead code on the paths it was written for. Every duration it waits on is ONE `Timing` — `Clustered.Timing` is the production value, a test runs `Settle`, `Ask`, `Read` and `Place` at a scale it can afford, and a field left zero is refused by name — and it splits a request by what the broker does with it: a READ (does this stream, consumer or bucket exist) is answered on arrival or never, so `Read` gives a request up after `ReadTerm` (a second) and asks again after `ReAsk` (another), while a WRITE's reply is held until the object has a leader, so `Ask` waits `AskTerm` (fifteen seconds clustered) before it asks again. `Refused` names the answered refusals that skip the read-back, and every create site — `coord/kv`'s buckets and the object store's included — asks it |
| `jsinflight` | How a CONDITIONAL publish to a replicated stream is answered while the leader still has another write to that subject in flight. The leader gives a compare-and-set THREE answers: it lands; it is refused on a comparison (10071, naming the revision the subject IS at); or it is refused WITHOUT one (10164, "wrong last sequence" and no number), because the leader marks a subject in flight when it proposes a conditional write and clears the mark only after it has applied it AND acknowledged it — so the write ahead may be the caller's own, already confirmed. The client maps both refusals to one revision mismatch and calls them equivalent, and both conditional writers here believed it: under load a removal at the very revision its caller had just read back was refused, leaving a create race over that record with no winner, and a charge spent its sixteen compare-and-set rounds on refusals no other writer caused. `Decide` retries the identical publish while the answer is in flight (1 ms doubling to 64 ms, jittered), within the publish's own deadline or the context's DefaultTimeout, and one still undecided at the end is `Undecided`, which wraps the context error and NEVER the refusal, so no caller reads it as a race somebody else won. One package because the rule is the server's and two writers publish conditionally — `coord/kv` to the buckets, `queue/jetstream` to the state logs — and written twice it would drift as `jsprovision`'s did; `statelog`'s appender, which reaches the log through the second, reads 10071 alone as a refusal, so 10164 is a compare-and-set answer nowhere in the tree. A LEAF over the JetStream client and `backoff` |
| `jsapi` | WHICH JETSTREAM API A CONNECTION SPEAKS, decided once. A member of the embedded fleet runs JetStream itself; a LEAF — a node without the `data` role — runs a broker with JetStream switched off and reaches the members' across its link; an external cluster is somebody else's. A leaf's broker answers nothing on `$JS.API.>`, and what crosses a leaf link is a DOMAIN, so every embedded member serves one fixed domain and every client of the fleet addresses it — a member's own clients too, whose server answers the domain's subjects as it answers the plain ones, so no caller ever has to know which kind of node it is on. Five subsystems build a JetStream client (the queue, both coordination stores, the memory changelog, the backup), which is five places to disagree; the zero `API` is REFUSED rather than read as either, because each reading is a node that boots and times out on first use. It also holds the ONE LEADER READ of a single stream message (`jsapi.Leader`, answering `ErrNoMessage` only on the leader's word), which coord's certified KV listings and `objstore/natsobj`'s metadata reads, `Stat` and `Delete` share, because a direct get can be answered "not found" by a replica that is behind |
| `jwks` | THE ONE CACHED READER of somebody else's published key set, and the one place that decides what this engine trusts one to say. The Forge relay a webhook arrives over verifies Atlassian's signed token against the keys Atlassian publishes, and what it needs from a key set is SIX decisions none of which is about Forge and none of which is locally obvious: the staleness budget, the unknown-kid refetch floor, the document ceiling, whether one unusable entry discards the rest, whether a stale key still verifies when the source is down, and whether the fetch happens UNDER THE LOCK. Kept apart from the relay, each is stated and tested once, against a key set rather than through a webhook, and the relay's own copy had already got the last of those wrong before it was extracted: it held its mutex across a ten-second network fetch, so a CDN having a bad minute serialised every delivery in the process behind one hung request. THE FETCH IS OFF THE LOCK and the window that opens — every caller deciding to fetch at once — is closed by a SINGLEFLIGHT, so one key rotation is one request however many tokens arrive during it, and every caller waits on ITS OWN context rather than being held to the leader's deadline — while the flight runs on NOBODY's, bounded by the fetch timeout: it ran on the first caller's, so one webhook sender giving up failed every verification waiting beside it. A failed flight is CLEARED rather than latched, because one bad minute at a provider must not refuse every token until something restarts. An unknown `kid` refetches at most once a minute, because a kid nobody published is what a forged token carries and treating it as staleness makes verification an amplifier an unauthenticated caller aims at whoever hosts the keys — and that minute is counted from the last ATTEMPT, a failed one included, because counted from the last success an outage made every forged kid a fetch again once the set aged past it; inside it a STALE key the set names is served without asking a source that just failed, and only a COLD cache, which has nothing to answer from, asks again at once, one flight at a time. It reads RSA keys and nothing else, because the relay pins RS256 and a key nothing should verify with is a key nothing should hold, and skips every other type (`EC`, `OKP`, `oct`), an `oct` key above all, since a symmetric key read out of a published set is a signing secret anybody can download. And a key the issuer marked `use: enc` is skipped while one with no `use` at all is kept: the field is optional, so requiring it would discard every set that omits it, and honouring it is refusing to check a signature against a key its own publisher says cannot have made one |
| `logging`, `secrets`, `provision`, `envref`, `envfile`, `redact`, `workkey`, `backoff`, `cron`, `period` | The small shared grammars. Each imports nothing from the rest of the engine but others in this row and `words`, a leaf over the standard library, `secrets` reads `envref`, `redact` and `words`, `logging` reads `redact` (every record passes it on its way to any destination, so a credential a call site did not know it logged reaches neither the console nor the file), and `provision` reads `envfile`, `envref` and `secrets`, which is what lets `config` itself depend on them, and what let the org model validate a schedule's cron with the parser the scheduler evaluates it with, where it had counted the fields because the parser sat in `schedule`, above it. `secrets` seals TWO SHAPES under one keyring: the envelope (`Cipher`, a whole value in one AES-GCM call, base64, for a field) and the SEALED STREAM (`StreamSealer`, STREAM over AES-256-GCM in 64 KiB chunks under a per-object HKDF key, its associated data the caller's, the chunk's index and a final flag, so truncation, reordering and extension all fail as `ErrDecrypt` — and what it hands out before the cut is provisional until `io.EOF`), because a value of tens of mebibytes — a harness's captured session — sealed whole held five times itself in memory, where streamed it holds two chunk buffers a side. `secrets` is also where WHICH FIELDS HOLD A CREDENTIAL is decided, once: a `secret:"true"` tag on the field, `secret:"content"` for a BODY, a setup step's file, read whole at launch through `secrets.ReadContent`, read through `secrets.Field` and shown by `secrets.Mask`, because a tag is maintained by declaring it where a list of paths is maintained by whoever remembers it exists. It also holds THE SIZE OF A VALUE, `secrets.MaxValueBytes`, which every store asks `secrets.CheckValue` at the write as it asks `CheckName` and `CheckSource`: one limit rather than the API route's alone, because a `crewlet secrets set` run with the engine stopped writes the fleet's store directly, with no route in front of it, and every reader of a revealed value through a node is sized from this one number, so a store that took more could hold a credential a reveal through a node cannot give back. `provision` carries a run's ask for a fresh value SPELLED AS ITS CALLER SPELLS IT (`provision.FreshSwitch`: `-recreate-webhooks` from the command line, `recreate_webhooks` from the setup route), one value rather than a bool with a name beside it, because `provision.RefuseFresh` (the refusal of that switch over a value the document owns, a literal with no variable to mint into) names the switch as one of the two ways on, and a pass that spelled the switch itself could spell only one surface's, so a caller of the setup route who sent `recreate_webhooks` would be told to re-run with a flag that surface does not have. `period` is the company CALENDAR, the day, the ISO week and the month a moment falls in on the company's clock, and a window's identity is its LABEL (`2026-09-23`, `2026-W39`, `2026-09`) rather than an instant, because a label is what every node computes alike, what a counter rolls over by comparing, and what sorts as a string. A day begins at the FIRST instant of its date, which `time.Date` alone gets wrong in the zones that move their clocks across midnight (Santiago resolves to the evening before, Amman to the second of two midnights); the relative date tokens (`today`, `eow`, `+7d`, a date, an instant) are RESOLVED here (`Resolve`), so the tracker's filters, the act tool that writes a due date and the assistant's preview of that write read a token one way, and there is one calendar rather than a copy |
| `skipgate` | THE ENFORCEMENT BEHIND "a skip is not a pass", which was a convention held by memory and had already decayed twice. It reads `go test -json` on both test pipelines, renders it back so a CI log is unchanged, and fails on a skip nothing declared — plus it carries the run's own verdict, because in a pipeline `go test`'s exit status is gone by the time make looks. The allowlist is keyed on the test's own NAME with a reason per entry rather than being a count, because a count goes green when one skip is fixed and another appears. Two-sided where it can be: an `Always` entry that stopped firing is stale and fails, scoped to packages the run actually covered — without that scoping each half of the partition fails on the other half's entries. `-timings FILE` writes how long each passing package took (the partition's weights) — read from the package-level records it already judges, and written whatever the verdict; a failed package's time is left out because it stops wherever the failure stopped it |
| `clocktest` | INSTANTS WHOSE TWO CLOCK READINGS HAVE PARTED, for tests of code that measures time across them: `AfterSleep` (the wall reading moved, the monotonic one not, as a host that slept reads its clock) and `Parted` (each moved by its own amount, as a wall clock stepped back does too). Go compares two instants that both carry a monotonic reading on that reading alone, so code that holds such an instant against something counting on the wall clock (a vendor's reset, a lease the store expires) answers wrongly after a sleep, and only a forged instant can show it: no exported call moves one reading without the other, so the wall seconds are written in place and the forgery checks that it moved exactly what it meant to, failing with the layout it assumes named. A leaf over the standard library, imported only by tests |
| `solo` | WHICH TEST PACKAGES NEED THE RUNNER TO THEMSELVES, as a declaration rather than a list. A package imports it from `TestMain` and `internal/solo/partition` derives both halves of the suite from that, so `make test` / `make test-solo` / ci.yml share ONE computed answer instead of three hand-maintained copies of a `grep -v`. Its roster guard is what makes the declaration self-maintaining, and it fails in BOTH directions — a package standing up a multi-member broker without declaring (the flake) and a package declaring without needing to (the flake-suppressant). The predicate is exact rather than approximate: the cluster harness's ENTIRE exported surface is four multi-member constructors, so importing it IS forming a quorum. The package doc is also where every alternative's measurement lives — a build tag hides files from `go vet` and golangci-lint and can drop a package from `go list` entirely, `-short` skips nothing on its own, a custom flag kills every test binary that does not define it, `-skip` leaves `[no tests to run]` and exit 0 — and every one of them fails SILENTLY, which is why this is an import and not a tag. `partition` also ORDERS the parallel half from measured seconds (`-weights`): longest first, because `go test` starts package runs strictly in command-line order and alphabetical order started the three longest packages last; the solo half runs at `-p 1`, where no order ends it sooner. Weights decide order and NEVER membership — an unmeasured package weighs its half's median |
| `e2e` | The gates only a whole running node can answer: a real engine on a real embedded stream, the real API in front of it, a real turn driven by a scripted model, and the dashboard's OWN modules consuming the frames that come out. Nothing is stubbed but the vendor endpoint, because what it exists to catch is a misunderstanding BETWEEN two components that each implement consistently with their own tests — an event shape the projection does not key on, a slice the client does not read — which no suite of one component can see. It has no exported surface, and it forms clusters, so it runs in `make test-solo`. It holds the one piece of code in `internal/` that is not Go: `replay.mjs`, the CLIENT HALF of `golden_test.go`'s wire-protocol gate, which pushes the frames a real company's socket sent, the `/operator/act` answers its writes got (each with the questions that read the log it wrote) and what the captured tab was pushed in the company's own chat, through the committed `static/dashboard/protocol.js` under plain `node`, and fails on anything the client cannot read. It sits beside the test that runs it because the two are one test that changes together: not under `dashboard/`, since it is no test of the dashboard (its Vitest suites are), and not in a top-level `tests/` tree, where it outlived the old dashboard's own suites it had been written beside |
| `docsgate` | The tree's prose held to the files it points at: every relative markdown link names a file that exists, every `#anchor` names a heading its target renders under GitHub's slug, and every page under `docs/` is in `docs/index.md` — the docs site's navigation. It reads this tree through `sourcetree.Walk`, so a nested checkout's stale copy of a page is never judged as this one. TEST-ONLY, and scoped to markdown on purpose: a dashboard address (`#/worklet/ENG-42`) is a ROUTE, which only the dashboard's resolver can evaluate, so `dashboard/src/app/links.test.ts` holds those (and every pointer at a `dashboard-design.md` heading, which lives mostly in code) rather than a second copy of the route table here. A link is never followed across the network: whether somebody else's page exists is not a property of this tree |
| `docslinks` | Test-only, like `docsgate`, and it certifies the links INTO the documentation site rather than between files: every address on the docs site that this repository carries — in the docs and the skill it publishes, the dashboard a person clicks through, the schemas an editor resolves, the code and the process files — names a page the site builds under its product and version segment, because the site forwards no path it used to serve and a link outside that shape is a 404 however it once resolved. It lives under `internal/` because the site copies `schema/` and `skills/` verbatim and serves them, so beside the skill it guarded it was itself published |
| `clientsource` | Finds a declaration the DASHBOARD makes, so a gate can hold it against the engine's own. Several lists exist twice by necessity — the dashboard is a separate build in a separate language and cannot import a Go identifier — and each copy has drifted at least once, silently: a chip for a category no event carries filters a log down to nothing and reads as a quiet engine. The check belongs on the engine side because the engine owns the value. KEYED ON THE DECLARATION, NEVER A PATH, so a screen moving is invisible and the only two failures it reports are the ones that matter — nothing declares it, or two declarations do, in one file or in two. KEYED ON THE SYNTAX, NEVER THE LAYOUT: the source is SCANNED — strings, comments, template literals, regular expressions and JSX prose each recognised for what they are — and `Literal`, `Scalar`, `Union`, `Interface`, `Calls` and `Property` (one row of a table keyed by name) read tokens, because every regular expression they replaced was a claim about how prettier lays a declaration out, and one that stops matching does not fail, it matches LESS. What is not a literal (a spread, a computed key, a union member that is not a string, an expression where one value was expected) is refused by name rather than skipped. ONE REGISTRY, BOTH WAYS: `Contract()` names every declaration a gate reads, its reader and its ONE owning gate; a reader refuses a name the table does not carry, and `contract_test.go` refuses a row that does not resolve to exactly one declaration or whose gate does not read it. ONE HOME: every row is declared in `dashboard/src/contract/` and every name that directory exports is a row, held from both sides — the contract test here, and the dashboard's own `contract.test.ts`, which keeps each module there PURE (siblings-only imports, no behaviour, every export read). `Tree(t)` is where the dashboard's source IS, anchored at the module root (`sourcetree.Root`), because a relative `../../dashboard/src` is right from exactly one depth — every gate one level deeper prepended another `..`, and three packages kept a copy of the path to avoid it — and its one walk is `sourcetree.Walk`, so a nested checkout's copy of a screen is not a second declaration. A normal package rather than a test file because the gates of every package that owns a declaration read it — engine packages under `internal/`, and `cmd/crewlet` for a declaration the CLI's own commands decide (the terminal steps a screen hands an operator, which only package main's tests can run) — and a `_test.go` helper is visible to its own package's tests alone |
| `sourcetree` | What is and is not THIS REPOSITORY'S TREE, for everything that reads its own files: the module root (`Root`) and a walk (`Walk`) that never enters VCS metadata, a NESTED CHECKOUT or a `node_modules`. `Root` is where every path a test takes out of its own package starts — a docs page, the examples, the schemas, the dashboard build, the directory `go list ./...` runs in — never a counted `../..`, which is a depth every copy had to get right for itself and one did not. The middle one is why it exists: this repository is developed with `isolation: worktree`, which puts full checkouts of it under `.claude/worktrees/`, and of twenty-one walks, each with its own idea of the tree, the only one that stepped over them did so by skipping every dot-directory — a name, not a rule — so the rest read those copies as this tree's, so the withdrawn-vocabulary gate failed on a clean tree by finding its own list in a nested copy, and a presence check passes on a stale one. A nested checkout is recognised by STRUCTURE — any directory below the start holding a `.git` entry of its own, file or directory, whether or not it resolves to a repository — never by the name `.claude`, which would miss a clone dropped anywhere else. That is DELIBERATELY BROADER than git's embedded-repository rule, which walks into a dangling gitfile or an empty `.git` and would stage what it finds there: a pruned worktree is still a copy of this tree at another commit, and broader is the safe direction for a guard, because git never tracks a `.git` path, so a fresh checkout — CI's — holds none and the walk there reads every committed file. Every other skip (static/, dist/, schema/, testdata) stays the guard's own decision, and reaches only what lies BELOW the start: `Walk` never hands its start to the callback, because the start's name is wherever the checkout lives, and a checkout at `../dist` was skipped whole by every gate that steps over dist/ — and each of those asserting an absence without counting what it read passed having read nothing. A start that is not a directory is an error for the same reason, since `filepath.WalkDir` does not follow a symbolic link at its start, and `Root` resolves its links so a checkout reached through one is still read. `gofiles` is the same rule for the formatter: `gofmt -l .` enters every nested checkout, so `make fmt` rewrote another worktree's files. The disk rather than `git ls-files`, because the compiler reads untracked files and a guard over the index passes a violation until the commit that tracks it. It is also where a gate learns what it may SKIP without reading: `Required` derives from a pattern's own syntax tree the substrings every match contains (folded literals compared on the text's Unicode lowering, with the runes that fold onto an ASCII letter without lowering to it — derived, today U+017F — admitting a text outright), `LineLocal` says when the pattern may then run on the admitted lines alone, and `Identifiers` skips Go source spelling none of the identifiers a syntax-tree matcher compares — exact because an identifier has no escapes, and refused for anything that is not one. ONE derivation rather than a literal list beside each pattern, because a hand list drifts in the unsafe direction: the withdrawn-vocabulary gate's did, and skipped a long s. `ModuleRoot` is `Root` for a value several gates of one binary load once |
| `conflictmarkers` | A test-only package — what it certifies is content rather than code, as `internal/docslinks` does for the docs' links — that FAILS THE BUILD on a file in this tree still carrying a merge conflict's markers. A Go file with one fails to compile, the one place a marker is loud; everywhere else it is silent — a docs page renders it as prose and the site publishes it, a YAML or SQL comment swallows it, the committed dashboard bundle ships it — and a long branch resolves the same page one commit at a time, each commit permanent history. It reads the DISK through `sourcetree.Walk` rather than the index, because the `.orig` copy a merge tool leaves beside a resolved file carries the conflict whole and is one `git add -A` from a commit, and it fails a walk that read under five hundred text files, since a root resolved to the wrong place passes with no offence found. The seven-`=` divider is refused inside a conflict and everywhere outside Markdown, and allowed in Markdown directly under a line of text, where it is a setext heading — a stated gap rather than a hidden one: a divider left behind with both of its markers deleted reads there exactly like a heading |
| `adr` | The gate behind `adr/`: every record carries its fields under a number no other record holds, names an Authority that exists and mentions it, and an Enforced-by gate that exists (or the literal `nothing`, saying why); every citation of a record names one that exists, and the index names every record. It runs inside `make check` because the register it replaced, `docs/reference/design-decisions.md`, was referred to by nothing and drifted until it described an engine that no longer existed |
| `version` | What the binary calls itself: the tag goreleaser stamps at link time (for the image of `main`, `main-` and twelve hex digits of the commit, stamped the same way), falling back to the module's own build info and then to `dev`, so a binary built outside the release path names itself honestly rather than claiming to be a release it is not. `IsDevelopment` is the one PREDICATE over that, and it is an ALLOWLIST of the two unversioned spellings (`dev`, `(devel)`) rather than a test for a release shape — its caller is the development principal, which authenticates every request with no credential, so a spelling this build does not recognise, `main-<commit>` among them, must refuse the bypass rather than enable it. Split as `IsDevelopmentFor` for `resolve`'s reason: the process's own version is a `sync.OnceValue` over link-time state, so no test can put a release in front of the predicate that decides whether a bypass exists — and that predicate is certified beside its caller, in `internal/api/auth`'s development-principal suite. The package's own tests cover the package and nothing else now: four cases over the stamp/build-info/`dev` fallback. They used to be ten times its code and to assert the RELEASE SURFACE instead — the ldflags stamp, the pure-Go build, the image's `${TARGETPLATFORM}` copy, the catch-all notes category, the pre-release flags, the single tag trigger and every Dependabot entry — because each of those fails silently and has no other symptom |
